 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Forced ventilation to check H-Bug suggested... it was a particularly virulent breed of staphylococcus that was developing the most marked resistance to antibiotics. <i>(Mar 28, 1957,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

### H.B.
 
 <i>abbr.</i>

>  A large number of sheep in for sale 15000 i am told. ... 382 withdrawn at 13/6 best lot of H.Bs. <i>ibid.</i> 437. 79 Romney Rams, 33 Merino Vermont, and H.B. Rams. <i>(May 14, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,432,1950 ed.</i>)

### H.P.R.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> homestead pre-emptive right [(hist.)]

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> Although many runholders came to regard the land they leased as their own, their occupancy was intended to be only a temporary using of waste land until it was required for closer settlement. Anyone - the runholder included - could buy the freehold of all or any part of a run at any time to establish a farm. However, some protection for runholders was essential, for they could not be expected to improve their runs if the land and improvements were in danger of being freeholded without warning by an outside buyer. Runholders were able to register with the Provincial Council improvements such as buildings, plantations, fencing, and yards, for which they were granted [246] an improvement pre-emptive right (i.P.R.) covering fifty acres of land surrounding the improvement. A homestead pre-emptive right (H.P.R.), which afforded protection for 250 acres, could also be registered. ... One H.P.R. could be taken out for each property and it was only businesslike that each runholder should use this right to secure 250 acres around his homestead against purchase by an outsider. An H.P.R. was initially intended to cover the owner's or manager's home, but, in the case of runs of over 5,000 acres, could be extended to cover an outstation. <i>(1981,Station Years</i>) [NOTES: [ca1878]]

>  The term `hack' as understood in this country is not at all to be conflicted with the same term as applied elsewhere to horse racing under that category. A hack in New Zealand is a horse that has not won a certain amount of moneys [sic], and so it is quite possible for the Derby favorite of the year, or the New Zealand Cup favorite to be a hack. <i>(May 4, 1907,NZ Truth,98/2/3</i>) [NOTES: (hist.)]

 <i>abbr.</i> Head Rousie.

>  H.R.: Head Rousie. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/25</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### H1
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics, informal) Helen Clark, New Zealand Prime Minister

>  More recently i got drunk in Wellington with political people. One of them referred in a hushed and reverential voice to H1. "Who" i said. "H1," he said. "Helen. As opposed to H2." "H2?" i said. "Heather he said. "Heather Simpson. Her political adviser." <i>(Oct 1, 2008,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### H2
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics, informal) Heather Simpson, powerful political adviser to Prime Minister Helen Clark.

>  "She does not trust those outside the small circle in Labour and her chief of staff, Heather Simpson, is known as the second most powerful person in New Zealand (known as H2)." <i>(Aug 20, 2008,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  H2 likely to follow her leader out of Parliament.(hdg). Asked if she would carry on with him, new Labour leader Phil Goff said he believed the outgoing Prime Minister's chief-of-staff, known as "H2", "will have things she wishes to do". <i>(Nov 12, 2008,NZ Herald,A5</i>)

>  More recently i got drunk in Wellington with political people. One of them referred in a hushed and reverential voice to H1. "Who?" i said. "H1," he said. "Helen. As opposed to H2." "H2?" i said. "Heather," he said. "Heather Simpson. Her political adviser." <i>(Oct 1, 2008,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Heather Simpson, the long-term assistant of former prime minister Helen Clarke who was once known as H2, has returned from New York to marry her partner, Sue Veart, in Wellington. <i>(Oct 25, 2009,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  HEATHER SiMPSON, the long-term assistent of former prime minister Helen Clark who was once known as H2, has returned from New York to marry her partner, Sue Veart, in Wellington. <i>(Sep 25, 2009,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

### Haast schist
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. classification

>  Of considerable general interest is the plotting of the mineral-bearing lodes in the Haast Schist Group, although it is disconcerting to find in the legend that effectively identical symbols are used for quartz reefs and igneous dikes. <i>(Jul 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,23/8</i>)

>  in contrast to the Torlesse Group the Haast Schist is represented with considerable structural detail, mainly derived from Grindley (1963). <i>(Nov 1968,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,Vol 26</i>) [NOTES: No page numbers in Newsletter 26]

### habilitation centre
 
 <i>n.</i> a residential centre for offenders which operates programmes intended to discover and address the cause of their offending. [Jim Cameron writes : Introduced by the Criminal Justice Amendment Act 1993]

>  Another old word - habilitation - was dusted off a few years ago and is used in the compound habilitation centre, a residential centre operating programmes tor offenders. This may be a distinctively New Zealand term. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

### habitual
 
 <i>n.</i> ellipt. for habitual criminal.

>  <b>MacKENZiE</b> in fact most habitual criminals were very well behaved in prison, precisely because they were habituals and knew the ropes. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,10</i>)

### Habitual Criminal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> This is an indeterminate sentence, and can only be given to a man who has been declared an Habitual Criminal. ... Unless the `Act' is lifted from him by a judge of the Supreme Court it will continue in force for the rest of his life. Among convicts this particular sentence is is known as the `dog collar' for obvious reasons. <i>(1945,in Prison,133</i>)

>  GLOSSARY OF TECHNiCAL TERMS: A boobhead is a prisoner; a screw is a warder; a topper is a toady or an informer. The Act or the Collar - The Habitual Criminals Act, under which persons declared habitual criminals can have their sentences increased by the Prisons Board for offences against prison discipline during their incarceration. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,14</i>) [NOTES: (ian Hamilton `NZd's Last Flogging')]

>  <b>MacKENZiE</b> in 1953 i made a study ... of New Zealand's habitual criminals. These 520 people ... had received the accolade of New Zealand criminality ... in prison language they had been given the `collar', the `key', the Kathleen Mavourneen sentence - `it may be for years and it may be for ever'. ... The unfortunate recipients of the new title `Habitual Criminal' would serve a head sentence of Hard Labour to be followed by an unspecified period of imprisonment which could last until death. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,9</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

A hack in New Zealand is a horse that has not won a certain amount of moneys [sic], and so it is quite possible for the Derby favorite of the year, or the New Zealand Cup favorite to be a hack. <i>(May 4, 1907,NZ Truth,98/2/3</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON & FRASER</b> The old-time shepherds .. of Scottish descent and all of them owning the best dogs on the station or riding a hack with more commonsense to the square inch than any other horse. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,31</i>)

>  <b>GASCOiGNE</b> Tied to the fence, and under the trees, a line of shepherd's hacks stood patiently resisting the flies. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,33</i>)

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> it was then sown with oats to provide chaff for the team and also the farm hacks. <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,193</i>)

### haddock
 
 <i>n.</i> In early use applied to NZ fishes thought to resemble northern haddock, esp. <i>Gadus australis</i>. [[Paul bibliog. gives only haddock].]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i>[as-acute] <i> paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale,<i> mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i> tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i> mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The fish to which the familiar name of Haddock has been applied (<i>Gadus australis</i>) is a new species, described by Captain Hutton from a few specimens that were cast up on the shore of Cook Strait ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,115</i>)

>  Haddock .. [Scientific Name] <i>Gadus australis</i> <i>(1878,TrNZi,10/326</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago) [Maori or Settlers' Name]]

>  The Whiting, <i>Pseudophycis breviusculus</i>, is got occasionally as is also the Haddock, <i>Gadus australis</i>. <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> HADDOCK (<i>Gadus Australis</i>). <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,37</i>)

>  Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish; while only the ling, hake and haddock ... are among those of more southern types ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Haddock, n.</i> The New Zealand <i>Haddock</i> is <i>Gadus australis</i>, Hutton, <i>Pseudophycis barbatus</i>, Gunth, and <i>Merlucius gayi</i>, Guich., or <i>australis</i>, Hutton, all belonging to the family <i>Gadidae</i> or Cod-fishes. The European species of <i>Merlucius</i> is known as the `Hake.' <i>(1898,Austral English,189</i>)

>  <i>HadZ.dock</i> (h&abreve.dZ.d&ubreve.k), n. <i>(zoo.l) [Add.] New Zealand haddock</i>, any one of several marine food fishes; as (a) Gadus australis; (b) various species of <i>Merlucius. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,202</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] haddock 808, 823, 838, 840, 847-56, 1613 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our hakes, tench, bream, snapper, haddock, elephant-fish, pollock, salmon, gurnards, pipe-fish, parrot-fish, leather-jackets, cole-fish, John Dorys, sword-fish, cod; various kinds of skate and cat-fish, sting-ray and dog-fish. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (hokoa), hoka red cod.

>  Fam. GADOiDEAE 69.<i>Lota baccha</i>. Cuv., ... <i>Gadus bacchus</i>, G. Forster, ... inhabits Murderer's Bay. it is probably the `haddock' of the settlers: its native name in Queen Charlotte's Sound is `Ehogoa.' -->[Paul Bibliog. TS p.65 no. 796 <i>Gadus bacchus</i>--> (red cod) Forster J.R.1801; 1844:120-2 Forster, G.:2., t.180] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/221</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/221-222]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> red cod, yellow tail.

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 75. RED COD. ... Also called the Yellow Tail and the Haddock, (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>) is a well known fish on some parts of the coast, being the species that is cured and sold as Findon Haddock at Port Chalmers. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,115</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> RED COD (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>). ... Red-cod (also called the yellow-tail and the haddock) is a well-known fish on some parts of the coast, being the species that is cured and sold as the Findon haddock. it is a handsome fish, with a brilliant play of metallic colours when alive. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Red Cod <i>Physiculus bachus </i>Yellow-tail; Haddock <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Moki - the haddock - some call it the rock cod. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,412</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> hake, whiting.

>  <i>Merluccius grayi</i>, [sic] Guichen. Locally known as `haddock'. <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/551</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  MERLUCCiUS GAYi (Guichenot). <i>Whiting</i>. Sold as `haddock' in Dunedin, but not common. Odd examples have been taken as far north as Hawke Bay. Distribution: Unknown north of East Cape. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/120</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> hake

>  <b>PARROTT</b> Hake <i>Merlangius [sic] australis</i> Whiting; Haddock <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,169</i>)

### haere mai
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>BANKS</b> ... as soon as they came near enough they wav'd and called horomai... . <i>(Nov 12, 1769,Banks Journal,1/432,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BANKS</b> Northern Southern Otahite -->a Woman Wahine Wahine Wahine Come here Horomai Horomai Harromai <i>(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/35-36,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENRY WiLLiAMS'</b> After breakfast we went to them all; they were very glad to see us, and gave us the usual welcome, `Haeremai! Haeremai!'' <i>(1832,Journal</i>) [NOTES: >1832. `Henry Williams' Journal,' in H. Carleton's `Life of Henry Williams,' p. 112:]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> But once the `Haeremai' had been called, it meant that the best in the possession of the host shall be set before the newcomer. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,175</i>)

>  Go in peace 1912. Haeremai 1913. <i>(Dec 31, 1912,Chudleigh Diary,461,1950 ed.</i>)

>  int. welcome! halloa! [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Haeremai. occ. horomai! (+by 1898). A `Maori term of welcome, lit. come hither ... it has been' - from ca. 1880- `colloquially adopted': New Zealand. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,366</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> Huge old willow trees shaded one side of the marae which had a carved flagpole in its midst, and there fluttered a banner with the greeting, HAERE MAi, on it. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,10,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Kia ora ... was speedily anglicized, as was haeremai, welcome! as a loose term of greeting. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  haeremai, cry of welcome; really two words - haere, come; mai, this way - come along! Haereatu, go away! Be off! atu, away from the speaker. [OEDS 1933] <i>ibid.</i> 150 haeremai, as this is included in the Dictionary, haerera also should be included, the former being the response to the latter. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/148</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... haeremai: welcome, come here <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  haeremai: Maori greeting. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) haere mai: welcome or, literally, `come here'. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,273,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> Worst of all [pakehas] were the amorous ones who knew only one or two words: haeremai or e hoa, or obscenities. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,219</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> SOME MAORi WORDS ... haere mai come here; welcome <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Haere mai! Welcome! -->welcome; come here. [Maori] <i>(1963,O Te Raki,190</i>)

>  ~ n. Maori term of welcome literally ` come hither' [Maori] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  haeremai ... n. Maori term of welcome, literally come hither [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  Haere mai, mokopuna, she would say. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,91</i>) [NOTES: 1970 ihimaera `The Child' in 1977 Listener Short Stories 91]

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Visitors are being loudly welcomed with haeremais as they come within hearing distance. <i>(1970,Way Up North,26</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> haere mai: literally, come here, an expression of welcome. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  haeremai N.Z. sentence substitute . 1. an expression of greeting or welcome. ~ n. 2. the act of saying `haeremai'. [C18: Maori, lit.: come hither] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,502</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 337; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 222]]

>  <b>HULME</b> 192 Haere mai = as well as a greeting, this phrase means Come here <i>(1983,Bone People, The,467</i>)

>  They do not share the tourist industry's pleasure that the many thousands of dollars this busload represents all go round the economy four or five times. The spirit of haere mai is noticeably lacking. <i>(Feb 14, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

>  Some 200 came down the Waipa in their canoes, and were of course received with the usual "haeremais" and "tangis". <i>(Mar 8, 1873,Auckland Provincial Gazette,14</i>)

>  Peeping out from amongst the foliage is the device, in electric bulbs, "Haere mai," and from the top of the structure flies the American flag. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> These days you even say tena koutou or haere mai without saying Hairy My and looking nervous. <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,30</i>)

>  The Maori people of Wai-ata-tapu are glad that he has come here and would like me to greet him with a cordial haeremai. <i>(1943,Colour Scheme,55</i>)

>  As soon as they came within about a stone's throw of the Ship they would there lay and call out Haromai hareuta a patoo age, that is come here come ashore with us and we will kill you with our patoo patoo's. [fn] Haere mai, haere ki uta hei patuake: lit; Come here, come to shore to be `patu-ed'] <i>(1770,Cook's Journals,1/281,1955 ed.</i>)

>  English New Zeland South-sea islands ... ... ... Come here Haromai Harromai ... ... ... A Lobster Kooura Tooura ... ... ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/287,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLLiNS</b> He-i-de-mai! Come here! <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,560</i>) [NOTES: (NZ Vocabulary)]

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Hurramy ... Come here. <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,76</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> The moment we were preceived, one of their women ... repeatedly cried out ... in a loud and shrill voice, haromai, haromai, haromai, (come hither,) the customary salutation of friendship and hospitality. <i>ibid.</i> 2/330 Come hither Haromai <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/127</i>)

>  <b>183</b> `Hairemi, hairemi, Atua!' - Come hither, come hither, thou God! <i>(1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,1932</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> ...the women on either side waving their mats, and shouting Heromai (Come hither) to each other. ibid. 214 [Notes] March 29th. Heromai, or `Come hither', is the salutation of peace and friendship. Where this word is not pronounced on the approach of the stranger the feelings of the people are not favourable towards him. <i>(Aug 5, 1820,Journal,134,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... we were received with the greatest hospitality by his people who waving their mats called out to us - `Haromai, Haromi[sic],' their welcome to strangers which means literally `Come to us' `Come to us'. <i>(1820,Journal,20,1928 ed.</i>)

>  come here - e|rromai [haere mai] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: SEE ALSO 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284]

>  <b>McNAB</b> A number of natives collected at the place of our landing, and received us with the herremi, or `salutations of friendship' (the principal of these are their joining noses). <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/689</i>) [NOTES: >1829 Mar 2 Atkins's Narrative in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/689]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [The natives] were very glad to see us and gave us the usual welcome, haere mai, haere mai, Come, Come. <i>(Jan 13, 1832,Journal,216,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Heremi checonay [Haere mai ki konei] Come hither; ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),50,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> i ... proceeded to the village with the inhabitants ... uttering, with deafening shouts, `Airemai [e-acute]! airemai [e-acute]!' - welcome! welcome! ... accompanied by a ... waving of mats, and other expressions of applause, which are so liberally bestowed by these islanders, on those whom they may choose to honour. <i>ibid.</i> 1/160 As soon as friends ere espied ... mats were waved, the `airemai,' [e-acute] or wel-[161]come, was shouted ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/76</i>)

>  ... were met by a large body of Mauries ... who first sang the iremi. <i>(Jun 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,225,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> As we approached the village, a number of stout natives, wholly denuded of dress, rushed forward in a tumultuous manner to meet us, hallooing and roaring to the utmost extent of their well-practised lungs, a welcome, (airamai!) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/83</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [We] were received [by the Ngati Whakaue] ... with the salutation `Nau mai, nau mai!' which is here the term of welcome instead of the `Haere mai', of the other parts of the country. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,152</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 152]

>  <b>MANiNG</b> Then, as the boat came nearer, another burst of haere mai! But unaccustomed as i was then to the Maori salute, i disliked the sound. <i>ibid.</i> (ii) 21 The boat darts on; she touches ... a steep rock; the `haere mai' has subsided; six or seven `personages' ... come gravely to meet me as i land. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i)16</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Here we stopped, and the canoes were drawn up in front of the pa, amidst much tangi-ing, and shouts of `Haeremai, haeremai, haeremai!' - the usual amount of shaking hands and rubbing noses being over, a long korero, or talk, ensued. <i>(1866,Chatham islands,17</i>)

>  [spg ?jocular] ... old Matakatea would not let me go ... At last he said `Haere marie' & away we came. <i>(Mar 1, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/232,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Ropata's whare soon they spied, `Tenakoe!' the deputation cried, `Haeremai, Pakeha!' Ropata replied, When they got to the Ohinemuri. <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,33</i>) [NOTES: The Ohinemuri Deputation]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Had we been Maoris of course we should there and then have also squatted down and rubbed noses. Hereafter i will explain why at such meetings the natives do not jump up and say how glad they are to see you, but welcome you in woe-begone `Haere mai's', squatting down and rubbing the lugubrious nose until the spectacle is anything but pleasant to look at. <i>ibid.</i> 4/4/267 The `haeremais' and `tautemais' - `come' - `come to my arms' of some blanketed figures standing near some huts seemed to exercise the most uncontrollable influence over my friends, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/2/142</i>)

>  <b>RENWiCK</b> Haire mai ho! 'tis the welcome song/ Rings far on the summer air. <i>(1883,Betrayed,34</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> ~ Maori term of welcome, lit. come hither; haere is the verb. it has been colloquially adopted. <i>(1898,Austral English,189</i>)

>  <b>J. HAWKESWORTH</b> `When they came near enough to be heard, they waved their hands, and called out `Horomai.' These ceremonies we were told were certain signs of their friendly disposition.' <i>(1769,Voyages,iii, p.,(ed. 1785): ed.</i>)

### haere ra
 
 <i>exclam.</i> Farewell (to one departing) [Maori haere r&amacr.(phs popularised as the title of a well-known Maori song) (Williams: haere v. come; go, depart: ra av. there, yonder; or a particle denoting distance from the speaker), said to a person leaving, goodbye, farewell.

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> You can see a good many bundles of blankets and black top-knots scattered over the beach, all immovable. That is the native fashion of bidding goodbye, ... [34] But meanwhile we must `haere' along or we shall never get clear of Herekino. <i>(1881,Poenamo,33</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Haere ra, i'm going to Napier to get my divorce.' You never hear such a korero. They don't like a divorce. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,169</i>)

>  haeremai, as this is included in the Dictionary, haerera also should be included, the former being the response to the latter. <i>ibid.</i> 55/151 haerera,term of farewell, said by the one who stays to the one who goes: ra, an indefinite `yonder.' The reply by the departer is: e noho (lit. sit still), or hei konei ra (for you this place).<i>ibid.</i> 151 haerera, term of farewell, said by the one who stays to the one who goes: ra, an indefinite `yonder.' The reply by the departer is: e noho (lit. sit still), or hei konei ra (for you this place). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>ZiMMERMAN</b> ... the crowd came with me, and instead of saying goodby or cheer-o, they said Haere ra' and i liked them for it, for it means, `Go, then.' <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,42</i>)

>  haere ra: farewell. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

### hag
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i>, ?ellipt. for hagfish.

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 140. BDELLOSTOMA CiRRHATUM. Forst. Hag. <i>(1872,Fishes,87</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> <i>Bdellostoma cirrhatum</i> Forst. Hag .ho 1929-1930 <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,307</i>)

>  2. <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> Forster. Blind-eel or Hag. <i>(1921,TrNZi,60/139</i>) [NOTES: (Young) Check year]

>  <i>Heptatretus cirrhatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider). Hagfish (slime-eel, hag, slimy, borer). Tuere. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/399</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] hag, hagfish 2-5, 10-17 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> teacher [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 24B HAG -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 28 HAG]

### hag taper
 
 <i>n.</i> mullein

>  <i>Verbascum thapsus</i>, L. The `hag taper' is more abundant in this vicinity [Port Nicholson] than elsewhere. <i>(1878,TrNZi,10/367</i>)

### hagfish
 
 <i>n.</i> blind eel, tuere (See [blind eel](bla-bri.md#blind-eel))

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Pia ... 2. Blind eel. = napia. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,324</i>)

>  <b>SPEiGHT</b> The Hag-fish (<i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i>) is a marine form, with peculiar teeth capable of considerable protrusion,... <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,190</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 190]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Heptatretus cirrhatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider). HAGFiSH; TUERE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,6,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Heptatretus cirrhatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider). Hagfish (slime-eel, hag, slimy, borer). Tuere. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/399</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>POWELL</b> HAG FiSH (<i>Heptatretus cirrhatus</i> ). This grows to about 2 feet in length, ... even more revolting than the lamprey, to which it is closely allied. Parts of the Otago trawling grounds are termed `hospitals' by fishermen on account of the large number of wounded fish which have received the attention of the voracious hag-fish. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,60</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Hagfish <i>Neomyxine biniplicata</i> (L.R. Richardson and Jowett) ... Hagfish <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) (Syns. <i>Homea banksii</i> Fleming, <i>Bdellostoma forsteri </i>Muller and <i>B. heptatrema</i> Muller) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,397</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 397]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HAGFiSH (TUERE) <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) The Hagfish, or Blind-eel as the fishermen call it, is one of the most unpleasant-looking creatures of the sea. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,51,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Pia ... Heptatretus cirrhatus, hagfish or blind eel. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the NZ Language,279</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HAGFiSH ... Other names: <i>Heptatretus cirrhatus</i> ; blind eel; tuere, napia (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,297</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Hagfish .. .. tuere .. .. <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAGFiSH [<i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i>] ... Hagfish and blind eel are widely used alternative names, while the Maori tuere and napia are still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,30</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> .. the common hag fish or `blind eel', <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus ... Eptatretus</i> exudes quantities of thick slime. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,193</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Tuere, Tuare, n. 1. Heptatretus cirrhatus, blind eel. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,448</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HAGFiSH One of the least lovable of our saltwater inhabitants the Hagfish, or blind eel, is the last thing most fishermen want to see on the end of their lines. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,119</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> HAGFiSH <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> ... Hagfish, primitive eel-like fishes that grow to almost 1m in length, are as revolting in appearance as their name suggests. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,39,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> Some of the whalers at Fishing Bay, especially the Maoris, enjoyed sea foods and found turi (blind eels or hagfish) a particular delicacy after they had been properly treated. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,111</i>)

>  [species name] hagfish .. [scientific name] <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> .. [other name(s)] blind eel <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Hagfish <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> also known as blind eel; it is eel-like, but not a true eel. Hagfishes occur in cool waters throughout the world, but this species is restricted to New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,23</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] hag, hagfish 2-5, 10-17 HAGFiSHES 1-19 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### haggard continent
 
 <i>n.</i> Australia [(?nonce)]

>  <b>ALPERS</b> ... as joyless and dreary as the treeless, trackless wastes of the `haggard continent'. <i>(1900,Jubilee Book of Canterbury Rhymes,ix</i>)

### haggle
 
 <i>n.</i> a golf bet

>  <b>DAViD NORTON</b> A haggle is a bet between the players on a round of golf, part of the money allocated to the winner(s) of the first nine holes, part to the second nine (this amount may be doubled by the losers of the first nine), and part to the overall winner(s). Currently on Karori Golf Course: 50c, 50c, $1. Sometimes money is played for on each hole, but i dont think this is part of a haggle. <i>(Dec 3, 1987,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>M.J. ORSMAN</b> Haggle is used on Judgeford Golf Course (Pauatahanui) for playing for a small prize which all concerned share in but which losing team pays for - the whole process is called a haggle. `Are we going to have a wee haggle on the match.' <i>(Feb 1988,Personal Communication</i>)

### hahunga
 
 <i>n.</i> ceremony of exhuming and scraping bones of the dead. [See also lifting the bones]

>  <b>OPPENHEiM</b> [Hdg] The Display of the Bones (Hahunga). ibid 70 in the northern tradition the time lapse between primary burial and Hahunga could be anything from a few months to several years. <i>(1973,Maori Death Customs,64</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Tekoke and most of the natives of the settlement had left to attend a Hahunga at Taeamai. ... [131 May 30] ... two messengers arrived from Waimate ... to request our attendance at the Ha'hunga ... <i>ibid.</i> 244 The Chiefs Hiamoe and Pukututu came from the Kauakaua to request some of us to go up to attend the Hahunga there, the feast which takes place at the removal of the bones of the dead. <i>(May 23, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,130,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Hahunga, a grand annual feast ... No further notice is taken of the deceased till the Hahunga, a grand annual feast; when the [138] bones of all belonging to several united tribes are taken down, and removed to their last resting-place, in or near the sacred grove. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),137</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> A very curious ceremony, called hahunga, takes place when the skulls of the dead are exhibited to their living relations before being finally deposited in the secret family vault. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,147</i>)

>  Hahunga - A hahunga was a funeral ceremony, at which the natives usually assembled in great numbers, and during which `baked meats' were disposed of ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,236,(Auckland edn) ed.</i>)

>  The Exhumation, or hahunga, - i.e., cleaning of the bones, - sometimes took place a year after death. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/20</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  <b>TiNNE</b> The most ... interesting ceremonial ... was a `huhunga,' or bone-scraping, ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,66</i>)

>  ... the hahunga, a feast attending the ceremonial raising of a chief's bones ... <i>(1895,TrNZi,27/611</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Hahunga: ceremony of exhuming and scraping bones of the dead. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,190</i>)

>  For his information, the hahunga (exhumation) did not occur on the same night of the burial or interment, as he did to his grandmother... <i>(Apr 19, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,02</i>)

### haihunga
 
 <i>n.</i> an exhumation

>  <b>POLACK</b> The food of the Chief mourners at an haihunga is not allowed to be cooked by the slaves, to whose office such labour is generally assigned, .. <i>ibid.</i>. 1/75 These orations ended, the crowds depart, and the actions, &c. of the deceased only are spoken of until the exhumation (haihunga) takes place, about one year after the decease of the chief. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/72</i>)

### hair as straight as a pound of candles
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1970 from a woman in her 90s - Pam Byng Wellington 31/1/86]

### hair cutter
 
 <i>n.</i> huhu beetle

>  <i>(Aug 20, 1977,Dominion</i>)

### hair seal
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> any one of various eared seals with coarse hair only, and no underlying fur. [Compare fur seal, leopard seal]

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> Seals of the Hair kind are numerous here [on Stewart island]. ... The Furr seals are here also but not so plentiful as the other ... The Hare seal is a singular looking animal of the brownish colour. <i>(1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 357]

>  hair seal - pa'hka|k [pakakae] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 357 c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112; also Begg 1979 p.85]

>  <b>McNAB</b> Although the Auckland isles once abounded with numerous herds of fur and hair-seal, the ... seamen ... have made such clean work of it as scarcely to leave a breed; at all events there was not one to be found on the 4th of January, 1830. ... We searched then in vain for fur-seal ... <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,82</i>) [NOTES: 1830 Benjamin Morrell in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 82]

>  The hair seal, generally resort to places where there is plenty of fern growing and here they breed; ... they grow to larger size than the fur seal, which generally live on the barren rocks on the seaside. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,105,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [ed. fn 105: Sealions (Photarctos hookeri), pahkak in JB's vocabulary, were reported by Thomas Shepherd at Port Pegasus.]]

>  A fine specimen of the hair seal, weighing between 3 cwt. and 4 cwt., and measuring 7 ft 8 inches in length, and 5 ft nearly in girth, was captured upon the Okain Bay beach, by a man named Edward White. <i>(Aug 2, 1869,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  A hair seal, 12 feet in length, was caught in Akaroa Bay. <i>(Sep 1, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  A fine grey hair seal, over 6ft long, came ashore on the beach here on Monday afternoon. <i>(Aug 21, 1901,Otago Witness,44</i>)

>  A male hair seal, which is intended as a mate for the present lone representative of that species at the Wellington Zoo, was brought to Wellington last night by the Omakura. <i>(Dec 15, 1911,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The hair seal, generally resort to places where there is plenty of fern growing and here they breed; ... they grow to larger size than the fur seal, which generally live on the barren rocks on the seaside. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,105,1986 ed.</i>)

>  The coarse scrub and grasses.. were the sheltering places of hair seals and sea-lions. <i>(Mar 30, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,96</i>)

>  From November to June inclusive in one year is prescribed a close season for seals, except hair seals. <i>(Feb 12, 1886,Southland Times,2</i>)

### hair-pin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The owner neither swore nor raised his voice as he replied, `The horse is in the stall; just go and take him, too, saddle and all, you mongrel.' That boy is waiting to get even, and the owner vows when he does, he will provide a patient for the hospital, and he is just the hair-pin that will keep his word. <i>(Jul 23, 1910,NZ Truth,265/2/7</i>)

### haircut
 
 <i>n.</i> tampering with speedometers

>  Normally, when speedos are illegally tampered with, the odometer is deftly wound back. in some shadier parts of the trade, this technique is known as `giving the car a haircut.' <i>(Apr 1990,AA Motoring Today,9</i>)

### haircut inflation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Create yet another new definition of inflation called haircut inflation, so named for the odometer slice taken before Japanese used cars are exported to New Zealand - no matter what the total on the odometer before the haircut, the level afterwards is always rather low. <i>(Apr 2, 1995,Sunday Star Times,A16</i>)

### hairiff
 
 <i>n.</i> cleavers.

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> CLEAVERS (<i>Galium aparine</i>), also called lady's bed straw, goose grass, robin-run-the-hedge, and hairiff, is to be found on road sides and waste places in many parts of both islands. it is an annual, with sprawling stems up to five feet long often forming tangled masses. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,163,1967 ed.</i>)

### hairy
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants [see birdsfoot trefoil; bitter-cress; cardamine, coprosma,crowfoot; daisy, native; fern, boss; fern, cloak; fern, maidenhair; fern, polypody grass, oat-grass; nightshade, ourisia, pleurophyllum; rata, climbing; vetch.]



 
 <i>n.</i> a wild goat.

 <i>adv.</i> roughly

>  <b>DAViS</b> 'Running pretty hairy, whatever it is,' said Curly. <i>(1964,Walk with the Bishop,136</i>)

 <i>n.</i> rape [ORIGIN: rhyming slang, 1982 Big Huey NEWBOLD Page 249 HAIRY APE (n) Rape.]

 <i>n.</i> an aeroplane

>  see Adam's Ale <i>(Jun 29, 1976,Evening Post,14/4-5-6</i>)

### hairy fern
 
 <i>n.</i> [Transl. of generic <i>hispidus</i> hairy; from its hairy stem.]

>  Common ground species include hen and chickens fern, shining spleenwort, hairy fern (<i>Lastreopsis hispida</i>) and gully fern (<i>Pneumatopteris pennigera</i>. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

>  <b>FiELD</b> (And) <i>Drypteris hispida</i> hairy fern. <i>(1890,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs</i>)

>  Dryopteridaceae <i>Rumohra hispida</i> hairy fern <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>) [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]

### hairy goat
 
 <i>n.</i> [(trampers') (cf hairy a., and literal sense of mountain goat). 1955 R. Mason: Said occasionally of someone who is getting himself into an awkward steep place, and likely to get stuck.]

### Hairy Maclary
 
 <i>n.</i> name of a favourite NZ children's character from the popular books by Linley Dodd.

>  After a hard day rehearsing or performing the Hairy Maclary Hootenanny, singer and entertainer Jackie Clarke returns home to her two-year-old son Stan. And what does Stan want mum to read her [sic]? Hairy Maclary of course... Clarke first encountered Hairy Maclary a few years ago when approached.. to sing a couple of songs, including Slinky Malinki, Open the Door and the theme song for a recording. <i>(Jul 11, 2002,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

### hairy shaker disease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a congenital chorea in sheep, found mostly in Romney and Romney cross-breeds in the South island, in which birth coats of lambs are hairy.

>  Congenital chorea hypomyelinogenesis, Hairy shaker disease. <i>(1971,NZ Veterinary  Association,303</i>) [NOTES: Diseases of Domestic Animals in New Zealand]

>  <b>BARTON</b> The condition is known in this country as hairy shaker disease. <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,19</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> The diagnosis of hairy shaker disease is not always simple. <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,21</i>)

### hairy tip
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) medullated wool in a sheep fleece.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> .. there is a rather common kind of medullation which is generally known as 'hairy tip.' <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,23</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> if hairy tip is present, it can be found over the greater part of the fleece. Hairy tip can be regarded as a very much reduced form of gross medullation. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,25</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Most cotted fleeces have a hairy tip. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,100</i>)

### hairy-arsed learner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  hairy-arsed learner: A man who has probably shorn a hundred thousand sheep in his day but whose skill has been much reduced by age or infirmity. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/25</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### haka
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> To dance Sha[acute]kka <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/340</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [spg 'hakaring] As soon as night had closed in the natives began to dance and after 'hakaring for some time there was a general firing round, many fired ball. <i>(Mar 23, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,115,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We had not been in long, before one of the chiefs called on the girls to come and haka to the strangers, and in a few minutes a number of the prettiest young girls ... were seated in a circle in very shallow water ... They sang us a wild song ... [ed fn] Short for haka taparahi, a term covering all dances and action songs ... <i>(1959,Early Travellers,444</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Dec Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 444]



 
 <i>n.</i> the word suggests today violent and noisy posture-dance (cf. A. 1896)

>  `general term for a posture-dance; dance with song and action; usually supposed to mean a War-dance; but the real earth-shaking and nerve-shattering war-dance is peruperu, with its savage gesturing, eye-glaring, and cries demoniac, the savagest looking of which may be in pure fun and intended as hearty welcome. Both vowels must be short ...' - <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words]



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib.]

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> it was a grand welcome; although the haka dancers unexpectedly faced the wrong way for the movie camera man ... <i>(1943,Brown Man's Burden,88,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Sweet Beulah Land']

>  The entire country, Pakeha as well as Maori, had haka fever. <i>(Mar 21, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in a debased form, often with meaningless Maori-looking words, and, esp. in Otago, English words and cries, a school or team form of noisy encouragement, or rally; often called `the school haka'. [ca 1900 -->eg Boomeranga! Boomeranga! ow-wow-wow!. -->(Apparently used in Australia in the same sense: -->May 1950 Austr. newsreel (West Austr; `Head of the River' Boat-race) `... college boys giving their war cries and hakas']

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Perhaps the most useful and expressive of our loans from the Maori is mana, reputation, honour, etc. Another is haka, now becoming well known wherever our football teams go. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word haka `a Maori war dance' has become common as friendly hakas usually introduce a football match and haka parties associated with students' capping processions fix it in the minds of the leading citizens of tomorrow. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

>  1st XV play Wellington Old Boys. ... We have to know School haka, house haka, 1st XV, house XV, ... by heart. <i>(1972,islands,1/15</i>) [NOTES: Henderson (Freedom's Ramparts)]

>  Nor should we left-wing trendy feminist pooftah intellectuals forget that the ritual haka at the start of a rugby game antedates by many decades the perfunctory `kia ora' with which we nowadays preface our performance. <i>(Nov 8, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Everywhere you look, and for any reason they can think of, there's some people doing the "hukka", as it's now called. Given our love of this ritual, i'm surprised we don't have a national hukka tournament, from which a national team would be chosen. They would represent us at international competitions dressed in traditional apparel and be called the All Flax. <i>(Oct 10, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOLMES</b> Threatening hakas by spear-toting Fijian warriors. <i>(1977,Best of Homespun,36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dance; a song (accompanying a dance) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HARSANT</b> Following the big feast there were haka, songs, poi dances and stick games, until it was time for the evening meal. <i>(1979,They Called Me Te Maari,53</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,243</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> <i>(1958,Spinster,197</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,224</i>)

>  [The Duke of York] was given an uproarious welcome by [Otago Univ.] undergraduates, who performed the vigorous `Haka' and sang the University song <i>(Mar 18, 1927,Glasgow Herald,13</i>)

>  And the parlour pianist could rattle off a spectacular solo called "The Maori Haka Rondoletto". <i>(Oct 19, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),22</i>)

>  A million maggots shall do a haka / on the carcase of a stud bull. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <b>ELKiNGTON</b> You can see the <i>huku</i> danced for a couple of shillings, and even this is not bad, for sometimes the dancers get worked up and forget the two bob. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Someone starts the school haka. <i>(1983,Dominion Sunday Times,149</i>)

>  No, the Don would have seen the frenzied support posses on both touchlines and dubbed this the "Haka on the Parka". <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  <b>ROGERS</b> `When are you going back?' he asked of Hal. `And don't give me a big haka about going to town.' <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Metaphor]

>  "Take the story of Te Rangi Topeora. Through her being Te Rauparaha's niece, we learn about the roots of our Maori All Black haka." <i>(Jun 8, 2006,Dominion Post,EG.  8-14 June:5</i>)

>  The translated [Te Rauparaha] haka goes like this: "it is death, it is death / it is life, it is life / it is death, it is death / it is life, it is life / This is the hairy person / Who caused the sun to shine again for me / Up the ladder, up the ladder / Up to the top / The sun shines!" <i>(Apr 10, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Few New Zealanders know what the Te Kamate haka is about - on the field the All Blacks were doing this haka in front of the South Africans and the rest of New Zealand was trying to get onto the field to stop the game. <i>(Dec 1981,in Touch (Wellington),9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> to kick up a fuss

>  To do the haka about some culturally correct position ... They should possibly be ignoring it and getting on with encouraging our families and parents [to immunise]. <i>(Oct 15, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [not in Savage, though described 1807 p68 etc, not used in Elder Marsden p92 etc [war dance, only] (Griffiths Collection).]

>  <i>(Feb 13, 1777,Cook's Journals,3/996,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 13 Samwell Account in Cook's Journals (1967) 3/996]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> A'ka, ... s. A war dance; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,133</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> haka: dance, song accompanying dance. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,190</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> i'm a full Maori and proud, too. ... Old Mum she meant i'm still a savage, that's what she meant. Just quietly, eh? Haka and tangi. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,43</i>)

>  ~ n. ceremonial Maori war dance; similar dance for entertainment or sporting events [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <b>BRAiTHWAiTE</b> HAKA A dance. it could be performed as a welcome, as a challenge or as a means of working up to a fighting frenzy - and incidentally striking terror into enemy hearts. <i>(1967,Evil Day,379</i>)

>  haka ... n. ceremonial Maori war dance; similar dance for entertainment or sporting events [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>MiTCHELL</b> haka: Concession to race relations by NZ Rugby Union. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> haka: a vigorous chorus of voice and action performed by rows of men to express defiance or welcome; once performed before an excursion of war, now only as ceremonial welcome or entertainment. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <b>BELiCH</b> haka war dance, dance and accompanying song <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  haka n. N.Z. 1. a Maori war chant accompanied by gestures. 2. a similar performance by, for instance, a football team. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,503</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 338; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 223]]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> A large party came from Waitangi, to meet the Napuis; with whom they had a grand Haka. <i>(Mar 31, 1827,Journal,49,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [spg 'hakas] .br;They has several 'hakas (dances, when the ground trembled beneath their feet. <i>ibid.</i> 1832 Jan 13, 217 They now prepare for their Haka, or dance, accompanied with horrid yells and screeches ... <i>(Mar 21, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,113,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> This behaviour pleased the old gentleman, who hastily jumped from the ground and began to dance a haka [a's-acute]; <i>ibid.</i> 1/81 After each of my retinue were presented to the chief, partaking of the honour of the ongi, or salutation, the haka [a1-acute,a2-grave], or dance of welcome, was performed. ... This dance, to a stranger witnessing it for the first time, is calculated to excite the most alarming fears; ... <i>ibid.</i> 1/86 The dance (haka) then commenced amid the discharge of artillery. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/57</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> E' Waiata is a song of a joyful nature; E' Haka, one accompanied by gestures or mimics; E' Karakia is a prayer or an incantation, used on certain occasions, - ... <i>ibid.</i> 2/361 Haka - a dance, a song, a war-dance. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/57</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,198</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> in the evening there was a haka, or dance and song; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/239</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The friendly natives ... were busy ... cleaning their arms, singing `hakas', and making speeches, while waiting till the old women opened the native ovens, which, well filled with pork and kumeras, sent up an appetising steam in all directions. <i>(Aug 24, 1849,Sketches in NZ,29</i>) [NOTES: [1846]]

>  <b>TiNNE</b> ... the native lads ... danced the `haka' outside the door of the `whare' [e-acute]. <i>ibid.</i> 18 ... the young girls were dancing the voluptuous and disgusting `haka' ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,11</i>)

>  He also received a visit from three or four hostile natives, who, with blood-curdling yells, duly performed the indispensable haka. <i>(Jan 23, 1896,Otago Witness,50 col. 5</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> ~ Maori word for a dance. <i>(1898,Austral English,189</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRiE</b> Haka: war-dance <i>(1906,Treasury of NZ Verse,289</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> They danced what is called a `haka', a dance of welcome; the contortions and gesticulations are most violent and quaint... <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,65</i>)

>  HAKA. - A Maori dance, with grotesque gesticulation and accompanied by a chant often as fierce as the dance. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiii</i>)

>  ~ n. a native dance. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> The open marae where the sun beats down relentlessly is now deserted. Afterwards there were pois and there were hakas, ...and there was to be a korero on politics and the land question ... <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,13,1973 ed.</i>)

>  haka, general term for a posture-dance; dance with song and action: usually supposed to mean a war-dance; but the real earth-shaking and nerve-shattering war-dance is peruperu, with its savage gesturing, eye-glaring, and cries demoniac, the savagest-looking of which may be in pure fun and intended as a hearty welcome. Both vowels must be short; h&amacr.ka and hak&amacr. differ altogether in meaning: there is no word h&amacr.k&amacr.. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/148-149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> haka a dance with song, often of a warlike nature <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> [mistaken conception of] The thunderous haka, or war dance, later adopted by New Zealand Rugby football teams, is performed solely by men who accompany it with terrifying grimaces and brandishing of spear, club or tomahawk. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),28</i>)

>  haka: dance, wardance. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  i would include among New Zealand English words: haka <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> Viola Cunninghamii. ... New Zealand Violet. HAKA (dancing). <i>(1952,Chatham islands,33</i>)

>  ~ The Maori dance of war or welcome accompanied by grimacing, leaping and shouting, often imitated by the whites in New Zealand at football matches, etc <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),191,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> in the centre a defiant green banner waves from a stick and above the hubbub rises the distinct bom shikka bom of the visitors' haka. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,78</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> [fn]Haka was a general term for the dance and a perfectly peacable, welcoming and fraternal haka might bear all the marks - to the uninitiated - of extreme fury and blood thirstiness. The war-dance was properly called peruperu and was a really formidable exhibition <i>(1962,Banks Journal,2/30</i>) [NOTES: 1962 Beaglehole fn to 1770 March Banks Journal (1962) 2/30]

### haka party
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Also Weir House.]]

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> The crowd rocked with laughter. `Ake, ake, ake!' yelled the haka party. <i>(1943,Brown Man's Burden,89,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Sweet Beulah Land']

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word haka `a Maori war dance' has become common as friendly hakas usually introduce a football match and haka parties associated with students' capping processions fix it in the minds of the leading citizens of tomorrow. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

### haka'd out
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  [heading] <b>We're nearly haka'd out</b>..i hope a cure will soon be found, before we are all outhaka'd. <i>(Jun 20, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  He was all haka'd out and decided he had better things to do than spend endless hours at practice. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 15</i>)

### haka-hating
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Haka-hating ... a knee-jerk reaction or a recognition of cultural immaturity? <i>(May 1, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C11</i>)

### haka-style
 
 <i>adj.</i> in the manner of a haka

>  iti, who gave most of his evidence in Maori, explained the custom with a loud haka-style speech lasting several minutes from his seat in the dock. <i>(Jun 14, 2006,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

### haka-tormentor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Former England rugby hardman and haka-tormentor Fran Cotton says the All Blacks are heading towards being labelled one of the great teams - so long as they win the World Cup in 2007. <i>(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,D3</i>)

### hakakao
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is a distinction in the case of the godwit (Maori kuaka, or hakakao), the season for which comprises the months of February, March, and April. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,139</i>) [NOTES: [Animals Protection Act]]

### hakari
 
 <i>n.</i> the feast after a ceremonial funeral or other important occasion; a feast, f. Maori: Williams: [Maori h&amacr.kari] [[Ma. <b>hakari</b> feast] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 42.]

>  The well-organised reunion concluded on Sunday with a memorial service and hakari. <i>(Apr 21, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,3</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> One of the gentlemen ... was present at the shackerie, or harvest home (if it may be so called), of Shungie's people. it was celebrated in a wood, where a square space had been cleared of trees, in the centre of which three very tall posts, driven into the ground in the form of a triangle, supported an immense pile of baskets of koomeras. <i>(Apr 21, 1820,Journal,83,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1820 Apr 21 in 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 83]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> A'karii, s. A feast, where large presents of fish, potatoes, fern-root ... are brought by the visitors to the party visited. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,133</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> There is another feast given at another period of the year, called the Hakari; but it is totally different from the Hahunga; the visiters[sic] bringing the cooked food, and receiving from their hosts an immense quantity, piled up in the form of a pyramid, eighty feet high, and twenty feet square at the base. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),139</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> An entertainment, or hakari [a2-acute], was afterwards given by the parents of either party, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/188</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... and confesses that the angry war-speech and wildly excitingdance is attended with infinitely less satisfaction than that of an argument in driving a bargain in commerce and the hakari (dance) of the harvest-home. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/4</i>)

>  <b>ROSS</b> [fn] These feasts, which are called `Hakari' or feasts of peace, are now of but rare occurrence, and not always devoted to their original purpose. <i>(1841,Voyage of Discovery to North Pacific Ocean,2/99</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Hakari - a feast of peace where presents of fish are brought by the visitors; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/361</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The New Zealanders had another feast, called the hakari, very different from the hahunga. The hakari was a banquet given by nations to each other, ... Previously to the arrival of the guests the food was piled either on the ground or on wooden scaffolds. Such erections were sqare pyramidal towers having an elevation of fifty feet, or ranges of six feet high, extending from half a mile to two miles. There were several compartments in these receptacles for food; each being filled up with sweet potatoes, taros, maize, fern root, potted birds, dried fish, karaka berries, ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/189</i>)

>  in afn. ... rode down to Mangaone ...[sic] where there was said to be a great hakari (feast) going on, but it had not begun... in afn. [sic] caught my poney [sic] & rode down to Mangaone ... where there was said to be a great hakari (feast) going on ... <i>(Apr 17, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/34,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>MOSER</b> ... there was to be a great `hakari', or feast, and ... hospitality was to be most lavishly bestowed on the visitors. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,30</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> A HAKARi, OR FEAST. [heading] <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,89</i>)

>  The hakari, or festive dance, was performed by men and women. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,155</i>)

>  it was naturally made the occasion of a hakari, or harvest festival, accompanied by religious rites ... <i>(1903,TrNZi,35/20</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> in addition to other ordinary feasts the great festival of the Maoris was the Hakari. This name was sometimes applied to the building erected for the food as well as to the feast itself. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,113</i>)

>  <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: ANDERSEN]

>  <b>KEENE</b> hakari: great feast; large pyramid-shaped scaffolding, sometimes as high as 75 ft. to 80 ft. with platforms at about 10 ft. intervals from base. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,190</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> [a feast] Every penny gone, not a brass razoo left. Next day back to the wharf! What a hakari. <i>(1969,Awatea,Appendix ii/115,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> After the hakari the Maori hostess led her dark skinned one year old ... to our table ... <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,24</i>)

>  M. feast after a tangi, or other occasion <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary</i>)

>  WHEN ALL the intense intellectual activity was over for the day, the crafts people left for the Mihiroa marae for the hakari. Wine, beer, apple juice. Lots of splendid hot food. Speeches and songs. <i>(Sep 22, 1980,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36/4</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Hakari - this word referrred to the foodstand on which were displayed huge and varied amounts of food that a group was prepared to give its visitors, in order to maintain and increase their prestige or `mana'. (q.v.) The host group would make large personal sacrifices in order to feed visitors, and such sacrifice increased the sense of sharing both among hosts and between hosts and guests. Hakari now refers to the feast itself. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,19</i>) [NOTES: 1985 Howe Towards `Taha Maori' in English (NZATE) 19]

>  hakari n. Maori feast after a ceremonial occasion. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,338</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.]]

>  The delegates.. moved to the National Kohanga Reo HQ for a hakari in his honour. <i>(Dec 1998,Mana,11</i>)

>  Thereafter a visit to the sea and Makara Beach to cast flowers to the tide, and then to Makara Hall for fellowship/hakari. <i>(Nov 2, 2005,Dominion Post,D11</i>)

>  Then unveiling at Awakaponga Urupa, followed by hakari. <i>(Sep 28, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,16</i>)

>  All whanau & friends are invited to the unveiling . . . Hakari to follow. <i>(Sep 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,44</i>)

>  After a burial everyone comes back for the hakari or feast that brings back noa. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Nelson Mail,13</i>)

>  Pohiri at Maromahue Marae, Waiotahe 10 30 am: Waiotahe Urupa 12noon: RSA Cemetery, Whakatane 1.00: Hakari, 53 Goulstone Rd Whakatane <i>(Oct 28, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,44</i>)

>  Hakari to follow [the unveiling of headstone]. <i>(Sep 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,45</i>)

>  12pm at mangere Lawn Cemetery, then return to Manuera Marae for Hakari. <i>(Dec 6, 2005,NZ Herald,C8</i>)

>  Return to marae for kai hakiri. <i>(Dec 9, 2005,NZ Herald,C7</i>) [NOTES: alternative spelling]

### hake
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf Doug Gray's article on naming of new things on Cook's Voyages, see also Cloudy Bay cod, English hake, (southern) kingfish. Take in Young's note following:]

>  Another matter touching on the recurring annoyance of vernacular names is that up till 1891 <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> was universally accepted as the hake: then, in 1912, Waite, for no apparent reason, transfers this name to <i>Merluccius gayi</i>, although he admits that the records of these fish were hopelessly mixed on the expedition. ... <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/370</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>= hake, with a note on its relationship to Physiculus bacchus. He calls Gadus australis (Hutton) the `haddock.' <i>(1872,Catalogue of NZ Fishes,116</i>)

>  Records <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> from the Chatham islands. <i>(1872,TrNZi,5/266</i>)

>  <i>Gadus australis</i> = haddock. <i>(1877,TrNZi,10/326</i>) [NOTES: P. Thomson]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> = hake. <i>Pseudophycis brevissculu</i>s = whiting. Reports <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> from the Chathams, after Hutton. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,17</i>)

>  <i>Pseudophycis</i> sp. Noted as common, with remarks on variation of size and fins. Specimens sent to Hector. The spirit specimen (1) in the Museum is probably one of these, as it is labelled with Sandager's name. <i>(1887,TrNZi,20/131</i>)

>  Hake = <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. <i>ibid.</i> 210 in report from Cape Maria van Diemen, where the terms `blue-fish' and `rock-cod' (<i>Parapercis</i>) are used together , i suspect that the former were specimens of <i>Physiculus rhacinus</i>, and this would extend the range a little. <i>(1891,TrNZi,24/21</i>) [NOTES: G. M . Thomson]

>  This is where the confusion starts. He records <i>Merluccius gayi</i> (Guichenot)-syn. for <i>Merlus gayi</i> (Guich.) and <i>Gadus australis</i> (Hutton) - as the `hake,' and makes no independent record for <i>Physiculus rhacinus</i>, but admits that the records of the two fishes were in a state of hopeless confusion, thus tacitly admitting that specimens of both were recorded under the same name. LiTERATURE WiTH A SUMMARY OF THE DATA iN EACH CASE. <i>(1912,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1(2):182</i>) [NOTES: E. R. Waite]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) ... the natives now and then brought Hake, [ed. fn <i>Merluccius australis</i>.] Paracutas, [ed. fn <i>Thyrsites atun</i>.] a small sort of Mackarel, ed. fn <i>Scomber japonicus?</i>.] parrot fish [ed. fn <i>Pseudolabris pittensis</i> and leather jackets, [ed. fn <i>Cantherines scaber</i>.] <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808]

>  <b>POLACK</b> fn Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para </i>[as-acute] <i>paraua </i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui </i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora </i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango </i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202</i>)

>  <i>Merluccius Gayi</i> ... Hake, Whiting <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/175</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  <b>SPEiGHT</b> The latter is caught plentifully by line and trawl, but is not highly regarded as a food fish, while its close relative the Hake or Whiting (<i>Merluccius gayi</i> ), is much less common but is not more eagerly sought after on that account. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,200</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 200]

>  <i>Merluccius gayi</i> (Guichenot). Hake (whiting). ... Vernacular: The name whiting, though more properly reserved for <i>Gadus</i>, is firmly rooted in Otago and Canterbury. <i>Merluccius</i> in England and America is called hake. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/4</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HAKE or WHiTiNG <i>Merluccius australis</i> (Hutton) Hake is [166] known in Christchurch, and by some in Otago, as Whiting, but it is not a true Whiting. in Napier, Wellington and Marlborough the Southern Kingfish (<i>Rexea solandri</i>) is caught and sold as Hake. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,164,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> HAKE <i>Merluccius australis</i> (Hutton) ... This New Zealand species was for many years considered to be identical with a closely allied species originally described in 1847 from Chile, and is frequently referred to in the New Zealand literature as <i>Merluccius gayi</i> . <i>ibid.</i> 169 Hake <i>Merlangius australis</i> Whiting; Haddock <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,45</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Hake (haddock, whiting) .. .. kehe .. .. <i>Merluccius australis</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Hake (<i>Merluccius</i> ... ), about a foot long, is sometimes sold as a food fish, and is found, none too commonly, on east and west coasts. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,214</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> HAKE (a) Scientific name: <i>Merluccius australis</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Hake. (c) Other common names: Whiting; English Hake. (d) Principal localities where taken: Taken only rarely nowdays off Wellington in deep water; ... Whatever the position was years ago it is a fact that this fish is rare today. The position is further complicated by the almost indiscriminate use of the name hake, by fishermen, [for] other species of fish. ... The name Hake shold not be applied to either Southern kingfish or the Whiptail although there can be little, if any, objection to the commercial name of Blue Hake for the latter species. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,28</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> HAKE <i>Merluccius australis</i> ... <i>ibid.</i> 287 <i>GEMFiSH Rexea solandri</i> (Silver kingfish, southern kingfish, hake, king barracouta) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,150,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] hake .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] in the North island this will be GEMFiSH. in the South island it may be GEMFiSH, or if caught in deeper water, particularly during June, July and August, it will be HAKE, (NZ) [<i>Merluccius australis</i>]. ... [species name] hake .. [scientific name] <i>Merluccius australis</i> .. [other name(s)] English hake, hake, merluccius, N.Z. hake <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our <i>hakes, tench, bream, snapper, haddock, elephant-fish, pollock, salmon, gurnards, pipe-fish, parrot-fish, leather-jackets, cole-fish, John Dorys, sword-fish, cod</i>; various kinds of <i>skate</i> and <i>cat-fish, sting-ray</i> and <i>dog-fish. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> southern kingfish, titaki.

>  Hake, or Southern Kingfish. <i>Rexea furcifera</i> ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/269</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  JORDANiDiA SOLANDRi (Cuvier and Valenciennes). Southern Kingfish. Sold as `hake' in Wellington and `lemon-fish' in Auckland. Large quantities are taken by the trawlers from Napier southward. Common, South island. ... Distribution: Numbers taken during spring, Hauraki Gulf and Bay of Plenty (unknown, Bay of islands). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Jordanidia solandri</i> (Cuv. & Val.). HAKE or SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH; TiKATi. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,48,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Jordanidia solandri</i> : Hake (Wellington), kingfish (Otago), barraconda (Auckland and Napier). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i>Jordanidia solandri</i> (Cuv. and Val.) Southern kingfish (hake). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/415</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Hake or Southern Kingfish <i>Rexea solandri</i> (Cuv. and Val.) (Syn. <i>Scomber macrophtalmus</i> Cuv. and Val., and <i>Rexea furcifera</i> Waite) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,410</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH or HAKE (TiKATi) <i>Rexea solandri</i> (Cuv. and Val.) .../ This fish is known as Hake in Auckland. in New South Wales it is often caught by trawlers in one hundred and fifty fathoms and is known as King Barracouta. it is a favoured food fish. in Tasmania it is referred to as Tasmanian Kingfish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,315,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> There is much confusion in the common names applied to New Zealand fishes, and the application of the name `Hake' to several distinct species of our fishes, is a good example of that confusion. in Blenheim, Nelson, Picton and Wellington, a very different fish, scientifically known as <i>Rexea solandri</i>, and closely related to the Barracouta, with obviously no affinities to the Cod family, is called `Hake' or `Southern Kingfish'. The name Hake applied to this fish is entirely wrong, as it belongs to the Snake Mackerel family, the <i>Gempylidae. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 154 SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH <i>Rexea solandri</i> (Cuv. & Val.) ... The Southern Kingfish is closely related to the Barracouta, and is known in the North island as the `hake' but this name is more accurately applied to a very different fish, <i>Merlangius australis</i>, which belongs to the true Cod family. <i>ibid.</i> 155 [Southern Kingfish, <i>Rexea Solandri</i>] is found also in southern Australian waters. in Sydney it is sold as `hake'. it is also known as `Hake' in Auckland and sometimes sold as `lemon-fish'. <i>ibid.</i> 170 Southern Kingfish Rexea solandri Hake <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Tikati ... <i>Jordanidia solandri</i> hake, South island kingfish. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,417</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH ... <i>Other names: Jordanidia solandri</i>; kingfish, hake, barraconda; king barracouta, Tasmanian kingfish (Australia); tikati (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,280</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Hake (southern kingfish) .. .. tikati .. .. <i>Jordanidia solandri</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH [<i>Jordanidia solandri</i>] ... The name hake is commonly used about Cook Strait. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,46</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HAKE: See Southern Kingfish <i>ibid.</i> 200 SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH <i>Jordanidia solandri</i>. This is the King Barracouta of Australia and the so-called Hake of more southern New Zealand waters. <i>ibid.</i> 201 [The Southern Kingfish is] Called Hake in southern fish shops but bears no resemblance to the traditional English Hake which is far less oily. Tasmanian kingfish, barraconda and `tikati' (Maori) are other names. <i>ibid.</i> 141 Another so-called Kingfish occurs in southern New Zealand waters but this is no relative of <i>Seriola Grandis</i>. He is the Silver Kingfish, who is sometimes called Hake and known in Australia as Gemfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,30</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Hake is a slender, silvery-grey fish found mainly in southern coastal waters, although it is now not as plentiful as it used to be. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,75</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Gemfish <i>Rexea solandri</i> ... Also occurs off southern Australia, as well as Japan. New Zealand names include silver or southern kingfish and hake (which is now easily confused with the true hake). The name gemfish, conveying family relationship as well as irridescence, is more appropriate. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,121</i>)

[ed. fn] 1. Probably Rock Cod <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> (Forster). 2. Snapper and Tarakihi. 3. These were Blue Cod, <i>Parapercis colias. </i> <i>(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/6-7,(1962) ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Rock Cod or Southern Hake, are quite appropriate names [for <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> (Bloch and Schneider)but the name `Hake' should not be used for the so-called Southern Kingfish (<i>Rexea solandri</i>). <i>ibid.</i> 169 Rock Cod <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> Southern Hake; Hake <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> ROCK COD or SOUTHERN HAKE <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) Up to 1891 this fish was universally known to scientists and fishermen as Hake, then for some unknown reason E. R. Waite (<i>Records of the Canterbury Museum</i>, Vol. 1, No. 3) transferred the name Hake to <i>Merluccius gayi</i>, with Whiting as a second alternative common name. ... The most common name among Otago fishermen was Rock Cod, because this fish favours a rocky habitat. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,172,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> [ROCK COD] ROCK COD ... <i>Other names: Lotella rhacinus</i>; Cloudy Bay cod, southern hake. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,210</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The Cloudy Bay cod (<i>Lotella rhacinus</i>), also known as rock cod and southern hake, is rather more brown and lacks the dark blotch by the pectoral fin. <i>ibid.</i> 1/898 HAKE is a name applied to several quite different marine fish in different parts of the country. The whiting or haddock, <i>Merluccius australis</i>, is a true hake, very similar to the North Sea species; it is, actually, often called English hake. it occurs in fairly deep water south of Cook Strait. The southern kingfish, <i>Jordanidia solandri</i>, is also known as hake: it is closely related and similar to the barracouta, and found throughout New Zealand. The Cloudy Bay cod, <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>, is sometimes known as southern hake.(see also Kingfish.) <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/373</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> [ROCK COD] ... Known also as Cloudy Bay Cod in some areas and Southern Hake in others. Known in Australia as a Beardie, because of its barbel presumably. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,38</i>)
