### hakea
 
 <i>n.</i> an Aust. plant genus of the fam. Proteaceae, often erron. thought to be named from maori.

>  Another noxious plant which is beginning to spread rapidly [near Whangarei], and especially on the poor gum-lands, is what is called `Hakea'. <i>(1899,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,13</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HAKEA (<i>Hakea acicularis</i>). A tall spiny-leaved shrub common on the gum lands of North Auckland. <i>(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,137</i>)

>  <i>downy hakea [s] Hakea gibbosa</i> (Sm.) Cav. <i>ibid.</i> - 35<i> hakea [=] downy hakea: fork-leaved hakea: prickly hakea: willow-leaved hakea [s]</i> <i>ibid.</i> - 36 <i>hakea, fork-leaved [s] Hakea suaveolens</i> R.Br. <i>ibid.</i> 61<i> prickly hakea [s] Hakea sericea</i> Schrad. <i>ibid.</i>87 <i>willow-leaved hakea [s] Hakea salicifolia</i> (Vent.) Burtt. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> PRiCKLY HAKEA (<i>Hakea sericea</i>) Australian shrub 2-3 m tall with tough woody stems covered with leaves in the form of spines. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,113</i>)

>  GLOSSARY OF MAORi WORDS AND PHRASES ... hakea<i> a shrub, formerly used as a hedge-plant. </i> <i>(1982,Oxford Book of Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We'd dragged our way through the short hakea and tea-tree scrub covering the long flat. <i>(1965,Open Country,155</i>)

>  For years and years my summers were spent in the company of Madeleine, the girl down the lane and through the hakea hedge... <i>(Dec 28, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  The Messrs Martyn have on their farms a variety of hakea, which bids fair to make a splendid hedge-plant. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  The prickly undergrowth of gorse and hakea, which had taken firm hold while the pine trees were struggling, precluded ground spreading of fertiliser by hand or machine. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/82</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [found elsewhere but sign. to NZ pastoralism] a scrubby non-native weed that establishes itself readily in newly cleared areas.

>  A great deal of this gumland is undeveloped, covered with the drab, monotonous scrub of stunted manuka, kumarahou and the introduced but very much at home spiny hakea, which still covers vast areas of Northland. <i>(Aug 16, 1951,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  <b>TAiT</b> .. and 1800 acres of easy to rolling , heavy clay hills covered with manuka, tawhine, hakea and fern. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol i,122</i>)

>  A quick turn to the left and there was daylight, heavily shrouded by fronds of hakea. <i>(Sep 14, 1961,NZ Farmer,86</i>)

### hakeka
 
 <i>n.</i> [Ma. /hakeka/ W.31 Hakeka =keka, hakeke, n.] Hirneola auricula-judea, see jew's ear fungus. Also called hakeke. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Several fungi were also eaten ... such as the two large terrestrial species called <i>pukurau (Lycoperdon fontanesei</i> and <i>L. giganteum)</i>; the<i> harore (Agaricus adiposus)</i>; the <i>hakekakeka (Hirneola auricula judae)</i>; and the<i> paruwhatitiri (ileodictyon cibarium). </i> <i>(1881,TrNZi,13/30</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Many types of fungus were eaten by the Maori. These were known by various names such as hawai, wairuru, tiki-tehetehe, maiheru, tawaka (a species of <i>Agaricus</i>) and hakeka (<i>Himeola).[?chk ?Hirneola] </i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)

### hakoakoa
 
 <i>n.</i> applied to various muttonbirds (shearwaters) (qq.v.) <i>Puffinus</i> spp.; and esp. to the southern skua.

>  <b>1/161</b> <i>Puffinus assimilis</i>. Hakoakoa. Very numerous. <i>(1869,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 125. PUFFiNUS GAViUS. Forst. Rain-bird or Wet-bird. Shearwater. Laughing Jackass(?). Hakoakoa. <i>P. opisthomelas</i>. COUES. <i>P. assimilis</i>. HUTTON <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,45</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PUFFiNUS TRiSTiS \{GRiSEUS\}. (SOMBRE SHEARWATER.) ...<i>Native names</i>.- Titi, Hakoakoa, and Totorore: `Mutton-bird' of the colonists. [Check that some of this may be 1888 edn only.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,317</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 2/232}]

>  Observations on <i>Puffinus gavius</i> (Forst.), Rain-bird, (Hakoakoa), their Habits and Habitats. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/9</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  Some ... Maoris went past us on horseback ... They have collected 300 Akoakoa. <i>(Apr 9, 1886,Chudleigh Diary,343,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PUFFiNUS CARNEiPES. (FLESH-FOOTED SHEARWATER.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Hakoakoa. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/233</i>)

>  i sent Seymour to look after the Maories. He found their tracks and wings of 39 mutton birds, akoa-koa. <i>(Mar 25, 1890,Chudleigh Diary,371,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> The young of many sea birds ... were used as food [on the Chathams], such as <i>Kuaka </i> (plover), ... <i>Hakoakoa </i> (mutton bird), <i>Taik&omacr. </i>(a smaller-sized mutton bird of slatey blue colour), <i>T&imacr,t&imacr.</i> (a still smaller size) ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,6</i>)

>  . .. the most important [Chatham island] edible sea-birds: AS WRiTTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLiSH hakoakoa h&amacr.k&omacr.k' mutton bird taiko tcha&imacr.k' pied shear water turiwati tch&umacr.riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka k&umacr.k' snipe <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SOUTHERN SKUA <i>Catharacta lonnbergi Other names </i>: Sea-hawk, Hakoakoa. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,151</i>)

>  189.a. <i>Stercorarius skua lonnbergi </i> (Mathews, 1912) <i> Southern [Great] Skua (Hakoakoa)</i> Circumpolar on subantarctic islands; breeding Macquarie, Campbell, Auckland ... and Chatham islands. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,52,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Southern Great Skua Stercorarius skua lonnbergi </i> (MATHEWS, 1912) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Hakoakoa, southern skua, brown skua, subantarctic skua. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,218</i>)

>  New Zealand has a skua of its own... To sailors it is known as the sea-hawk and to maoris of the south as hakoakoa. <i>(May 6, 1920,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### hakoke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Extinct birds ... Laughing Owl <i>Sceloglaux albifacies</i> (GRAY, 1844) OTHER NAMES: Whekau, hakoke (North island),rock owl. The `doleful shrieks' of the laughing owl, which varied seasonally, were heard incessantly on rainy nights. Apparently it was easily kept in captivity, and reports say that it cooeyed in the evening, yelped like a young dog and mewed and chuckled. ... The most recent record of sighting was at Manapouri in 1950 ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,308</i>)

### haku
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kingfish](kee-kiw.md#kingfish))

>  English Name Native Name .. ... Herring Tawa Tawa Yellow tail Haku ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,Vol. iX/125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The Haku of the natives is the King Fish (S <i>eriola lalandii </i>) of Wellington and the Yellow Tail of Australia. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,111</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Haku, Kingfish .. [Scientific Name] <i>Seriola lalandii </i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SENiOR</b> The biggest fish i saw in the [Dunedin] market was the king-fish, called in Australia yellow-tail. [caption] King-fish or Haku. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,296</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> HAKU (<i>Seriola Lalandii </i>). ... The haku of the Natives is the king-fish of Wellington and the yellow-tail of Australia <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>24/203</b> 1. Cape Maria van Diemen. - Kahawai, snapper, moki, trevally, king-fish, haku or yellowtail, rock-cod <i>(1892,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  14. King-fish, or Haku (Seriola lalandii), sometimes also called Yellow-tail. NUMBER OF FiSH RECORDED - 7 LOCALiTiES - Cape Maria van Diemen, Bean Rock, Ponui Passage, Portland island, Manukau Heads. <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/209</i>)

>  if the Bay of Plenty was ... on the Yankee Coast, our cousins of Ammurika would go crazy over the haku ... <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  SERiOLA LALANDi Cuvier and Valenciennes. <i>Kingfish; Haku </i>. ... Distribution: North island and northern coasts of South island (rarer farther south). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/117</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Seriola lalandi </i> Cuv. & Val. KiNGFiSH, YELLOWTAiL; HAKU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,34,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> KiNGFiSH (<i>Regificola grandis </i>), Haku of the Maoris, is an excellent sporting fish which readily takes the trolling hook or spinner. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,68</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> YELLOW TAiL or KiNGFiSH (AUCKLAND) (HAKU) <i>Regificola grandis </i>(Castelnau) Here we have another instance of a lack of uniformity in the common names of our New Zealand fishes. This fish has always been the Yellowtail of Otago, but in Auckland it is known as Kingfish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,238,2nd edn ed.</i>)

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> We caught several kahawai, some haku and a couple of small sharks. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,179</i>) [NOTES: Not for a Seagull]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> YELLOWTAiL or NORTHERN KiNGFiSH ... <i>Other names: Seriola grandis </i>; yellowtail (Australia); haku (Maori). The general adoption of the term yellowtail for this species would be more appropriate than the sometimes confusing term of kingfish. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,234</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> YELLOWTAiL or NORTHERN KiNGFiSH [Seriola grandis] ... The Maori name haku is still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,50</i>)

### hakuai
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [hakuwai](h-hea.md#hakuwai))

### hakuakua
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [hakoakoa](h-hea.md#hakoakoa))

### hakuwai
 
 <i>n.</i> A Maori legendary bird, said to be heard at night but never seen. (Suggested originals include frigate bird, Haast's eagle; the most probable being Miskelly below, i.e. NZ snipe in display flights.) See also hokioi. [19?? Miskelly in Notornis 34 get cit]

>  Many legends are associated with the mythical bird "hokioi"... in the South and in some parts of the North island it is identical, apparently, with the "hakuwai" which has seven joints in its wing, lives in the sky, and presages trouble... "its cry was Hokioi! Hokioi! Hu!" The last word represented the whizzing sound made by the bird in flight. <i>(Sep 11, 1912,Otago Witness,76</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> The Mysterious Ha-ka-wai Bird [heading] No-one has ever seen it, no-one knows what it is like. Mutton-birders who have heard the strange sound of its passing over the islands at night speak of the ha-ka-wai bird. A swish of wings close overhead, a sound like the rattle of chains when a boat lets the anchor down, again the sound of wingbeats and the cry floating earthward from the heavens, Ha-ka-wai! Ha-ka-wai! in a drawn-out singsong with accent on the first and last syllables. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,61</i>)

>  A strange bird in Maori eyes is the hakuai of the off-shore islands in the far south. ... Whole families go mutton-birding ... At night the fowlers gather round their camp-fires, and old songs are sung ... and ghost-stories retold. And in the darkness sometimes they hear the ghost-bird screaming its `Haku-ai, haku-ai, Ooh!' and then a hair-raising swoosh of great wings ... This bird, called the `hakuai' from its call, is spoken of as a spirit. ... The mystery of the hakuai may be dispelled by assuming it to be ... the frigate-bird (Fregata aquila). <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/340</i>)

>  The Maori word <i>hakuwai</i> (or <i>hakawai</i> or <i>hakuai</i>, or any of at least five other variant spellings, including the European <i>harkowhy</i>) was defined by Williams in his <i>Maori Dictionary</i> as 'an extinct bird heard at night'. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,No. 4: 5</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> it is a moot point whether the name <i>hakuwai</i> was derived from <i>hokioi</i>.. or whether <i>hokioi</i> has been adopted., as an imperfect rendition of <i>hakuwai</i>, to provide a name for the eagle legend. <i>(1989,Prodigious Birds,91</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He heard the harkowhy up in Canaan on the Takaka Hill, the ghost-bird never seen but many times heard by muttonbirders and the odd fisherman in the mist and fog in Stewart island, crying like a chain rattling. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,142</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> The Hakawai is the Maori mystery bird. <i>ibid.</i> 92 One of the party who had heard it before on several occasions called "Listen, there's the Hakawai". <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,91</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [bull](bri-byo.md#bull))

### half a dollar
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs.)] (See [half-dollar](h-hea.md#half-dollar))

>  Now, to become a member of the League the sum of 2s 6d has to be planked down, and as quite a number of ardent Chow-haters have `come to light' with their `half-dollars,' it would be interesting to know from Mr. Cameron himself where the money goes. <i>(Aug 15, 1908,NZ Truth,165/4/6</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Dealing with the tendency of the Maori to refer to all money as `dollars' - a practise persisting to some extent with the Pakeha today - ... [fn] Some New Zealanders use the term `half-dollar' for `half-a-crown'. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,63</i>)

>  Half a dollar - two shillings and sixpence. But your dollar is worth 6s 1d New Zealand money now. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> i took a half-dollar [double]. <i>(1946,That Summer,57</i>)

>  We use a colloquialism for a half-crown and five shillings and call them a half-dollar and a dollar even now. <i>(Oct 8, 1963,Dominion,4/4</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The present colloquial or slang use of dollar, usually in half a dollar, `half a crown' in New Zealand, but in Australia, where half-crowns are not in use, two shillings, should help the natural adoption of the new official term dollar. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,133</i>)

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Half crown [:] half dollar, half bull <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  The crown was called a bull, a cartwheel, a caser, five bob, and most familiar of all a dollar, ... The half crown was called a half-dollar, a half-bull, half-caser, two-and-a-kick, and two-and-a-buck. in the latter instance buck stands for sixpence, though apparently this term was rarely used by itself for that coin denomination, but almost always in association with a number of shillings. The florin seems to have had fewer English nicknames, only two bob being recorded as being in use in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> fifty cents [Since the introduction of decimal coinage, fifty cents or a fifty-cent piece.]

>  [uncertain cit] <i>(Mar 3, 1964,Evening Post,17/3</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS2 - omit.]]

 <i>n.</i>

### half a shake
 
 <i>phr.</i> (wait) a moment [-->1953 14 M A3 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 14 M A6 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 14 M A13 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 14 M A15 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 14 M A16 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 14 M A17 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 15 M A25 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 15 M A27 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 15 M A28 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE -->1953 16 M A30 Thames DHS 15 HALF A SHAKE; + men, when in a urinal, and asked to hurry up, say 'half a shake', 'in a shake']

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> i'll show you where they're wrong in half a shake <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,43</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Half a shake - any more beer? <i>(1934,Tides of Youth,117</i>)

>  Knock it off, pack a sad, have a nice one Get stuck in, boots and all, have a coke, Half a shake, how ya doin', have a drop, of mother's ruin Hell's bells, blow that for a joke. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,No 51, p19</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### half draught horse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BLAKiSTON</b> in those days (1883), there was a ferryman, named Jack Heney, whose job it was to convoy the coaches across the Orari and Rangitata rivers; thinking i would be frightened, he ranged alongside the coach, on a big half draught horse, grabbed me by the collar and carried me back to the south bank. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,14</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

 <i>n.</i> (historical) [WW1]

This from the fact that Nat Gould was the author of a book titled `Landed at Last'. <i>(Feb 23, 1918,Anzac Diary,87,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: WW1]

### half quid
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) ten shillings

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> Ten shillings [:] half quid <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  The ten shilling note, after its introduction into New Zealand in 1916, never gained a slang nickname of its own, but was generally `half' something, as for example half-quid, or half-nicker. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,16</i>)

### half savage
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka half-savage]

>  Coming to a country where there were no legal means of enforcing such rights, when objected to, did the first settlers expect to walk into every acre without opposition? or, if not, were the Company to provide an army to give possession by force? or, if not that, did they believe that the cupidity of the half-savage was not inextinguishable by feeding it, and that additional gifts and presents would not be building a barrier to keep them from their lands? <i>(Jun 3, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,258</i>)

>  it may have suited those who were favourable to the success of the "King movement" to conceal its real purport; while others, who have cherished so much affection for the natives or professed so much interest in their welfare, may have been blinded to the inevitable results of encouraging such dangerous aspirations among the half-savage chiefs and tribes ... <i>(Jan 4, 1862,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> Perhaps, also, he will help to remind them how good, how strenuous life was `in the good old days,' when their Dominion was still half-savage, still unsettled after the toughest of colonial wars, and before the fire and axe of succeeding generations of peaceful settlers had robbed her of so much of her incomparable possession, the native bush. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,7,(1933) H.E.L. Priday Preface 7 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1930 Andreas Reischek Yesterdays in Maoriland (1933) H.E.L. Priday Preface 7]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  He led for more than twelve years a half savage life among the Maoris of New Zealand. <i>(Oct 28, 1903,Nelson Evening Mail,1</i>)

>  The old leaders having acquiesced in the new state of things, and with no hope of succeeding in any attempt to alter them, were not easily moved into rebellion, but among half savage tribes there always remains a leaven of revolt ... <i>(Sep 21, 1870,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  it is humiliating to consider that a half savage chief with a few hundred followers, should have the power to cause the Colony to waste money to such an extent, and worse than all, to learn now that this extravagant expenditure is to be indefinitely prolonged. <i>(Feb 2, 1881,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### half sharemilking
 
 <i>n.</i> fifty-fifty sharemilking, where the sharemilker provides the cows and the implements and has an equal share in the profits with the landowner.

>  Half share-milking on a one-man farm with a non-strip herd and a tractor is not such a hard job for a strong man. <i>(Nov 2, 1950,Dairy Exporter (NZ),89</i>)

### half woolled
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] with six months growth of fleecewool.

>  Freshly shorn lines sold from $30-$32.50 and half woolled at $30.50-$34.75. <i>(Oct 1998,NZ Farmer,8</i>)

### half-axe
 
 <i>n.</i>

### half-banded (sea) perch
 
 <i>n.</i> A perch distinguished by six red bands that extend from the spine to just over half way down the fish's sides; found in Kermadecs and Three Kings islands to East Cape. [<i>Hypoplectrodes</i> Sp.B, previously <i>Ellerkeldia</i> Sp.]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] seaperch, half banded 1268, 1278, 1315 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <i>Hypoplectrodes semicinctus </i>(Cuv. and Val.). Half-banded sea perch. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/410</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  24. <i>Hypoplectrodes semicinctus</i> Cuvier and Valenciennes. Half-Banded Sea Perch <i>(1929,TrNZi,61/143</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Hypoplectrodes semicinctus</i> (Cuv. & Val.). HALF-BAND SEA-PERCH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,33,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Half-Banded Sea Perch <i>Ellerkeldia huntii </i>(Hector) <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,405</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HALF-BANDED SEAPERCH <i>Ellerkeldia huntii </i>(Hector) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,232</i>)

### half-bent
 
 <i>n.</i> the recess where the nose of the seer engages when the rifle is at half-cock

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> There is a coterie that likes to alter the half-bent (the recess where the nose of the seer engages when the rifle is at half-cock). <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,192</i>)

### half-bred
 
 <i>adj.</i> the sheep resulting from a cross between two breeds, usually in NZ a cross between merino and another breed.

>  <b>PEACHE</b> The half-bred ewes are looking well ... <i>(Aug 25, 1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 Aug 25 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 50]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,265</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Originally only applied to sheep by a longwool ram from a merino ewe, but now loosely applied to the type. Corriedale sheep were originally called in-bred half-breds. Now in-bred halfbred flocks are those which reintroduce merino or longwool blood, while the Corriedale flocks can only introduce Corriedale blood. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs</i>) [NOTES: (1890-1910)]

>  200 six and eight-tooth halfbred ewews at 10s. ... <i>(Mar 17, 1894,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Mar 17 Otago Daily Times in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 113]

>  The first cross, or, as it is called, the half-bred sheep, has been found so suitable to the country that several attempts have been made to perpetuate or form a breed of pure half-breds, and with some success. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,94</i>)

>  <b>MacDONALD</b> The Merino provides good wool returns in its natural quarters - the mountainous back blocks - and ... [furnishes] half-bred ewes as a foundation of the crossbred flocks of Canterbury. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,37</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Aug 1869 Best wethers 11/- Sept.23 2000 halfbred Romneys, mixed ages and sexes, 7/3. <i>(1938,High Country,110</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Before long he had begun experiments on fixing a breed of sheep we call to-day `Corriedale'. Hitherto merinos were pastured on the runs and a few cross-breds on the farms. How to dispose of the merino ewe lambs? Crossed by the Leicester or Lincoln, the half-bred progeny was disappointing in size and fattening quality. ... Little gave it the name of the estate he managed - Corriedale. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,57</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> For the uninitiated, the cross between a merino and a longwool sheep, usually Lincoln, Leicester or Romney, is called a half-bred. . . <i>(1980,Tussock Ranges,219</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> of Maoris, half-caste

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> At each place which i have visited, i have been assured that the Maories are dying rapidly - `perishing like rotten sheep,' these people often say; thus at Kurreroo or Molineux, where there are now half-a-dozen natives, they say that formerly, and at no remote period, about two generations previous to the present, there were above a thousand; and in many other places within the last sixteen years, which is about the extent of the experience of the older beachers, there has been, in many places, a corresponding diminution of numbers. if this is an unexaggerated statement, it is a very distressing one to reflect on. Since Dr. -- visited this settlement, one of the residents, Mr. --, an educated man, has opened a school for half-bred or any other children, and the progress which the children have already made is very encouraging. <i>(May 22, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 22 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 223]



 
 <i>n.</i> a half-bred sheep [(farming)]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> ... you must get a very considerable increase of price ... for fine wool [from pure Merinos], to compensate for the loss of weight of fleece and of mutton, and for want of the more ... hardy constitution of the half-bred. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,249</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Oct 22 in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 249]

>  <b>PEACHE</b> We cut and tailed the lambs from ... half-breds. <i>(Oct 7, 1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 Oct 7 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 52]

>  The average clips for the various breeds of sheep are approximately as follow: Merino, from 4lb. to 7lb.; quarter-breds, about 6 1/2lb.; half-breds, 7 1/2lb.; three-quarter breds, 8 1/2lb. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,122</i>)

>  <b>SWAiNTON</b> The class of sheep usually carried [on a sheep-farm] is what is called the `half bred', a cross between a Merino ewe and a Leicester ram. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,90</i>)

>  The average clips for the various breeds of sheep are approximately as follow : Merino, from 4lb. to 7lb.; quarter-breds, about 6-1/2lb.; half-breds, 7-1/2lb.; three-quarter-breds, 8-1/2lb.; ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,92</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The half-bred is produced by the above crosses, and by placing the same kind of ram again to the half-bred [51] ewes the cross-bred, so much prized for mutton-cum-wool, is obtained. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,50</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The [Leicester] rams are extensively used ... for mating with the Merino, for the production of the half-bred .... The Half-bred ewe is one of the most valuable sheep ..., and has played no small part in establishing the name `Prime Canterbury'. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,38</i>)

>  Halfbred sheep. Originally only applied to a sheep by a longwool ram from a merino ewe, but now loosely applied to the type. Corriedale sheep were originally called inbred hb. Now in-bred hb. flocks are those which reintroduce merino or longwool blood, while he Corrie- dale flocks can only introduce Corriedale blood. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  This term [Half-bred] covers: (1) the progeny of any distinct breed of long-woolled ram mated with merino ewes; (2) the progeny of merino rams mated with any breed of long-woolled ewe; (3) the progeny from the mating of half-bred with half-bred. The wool produced by sheep from the above crossing is termed `half-bred' and ranges from 48's to 56's quality. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,416</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> When the frozen meat trade started, the change from Merino to crossbred, or halfbred, as they are called, became more rapid; but there was another reason for it as well. <i>(1938,High Country,116</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> HALF-BRED: A sheep which originates from a Merino-Leicester, Merino-Lincoln or Merino-Romney cross. They are used extensively in the high country and drier down country districts of the South island. <i>(1949,High Country Days,195,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Some stations have stuck to Merino...and others have changed to `half-breds' as [Merinos crossed with a mutton breed] are called. <i>(1951,High Country,14</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> in this country, Merinos, Corriedales and halfbreds are classed as the fine-wool sheep, and all the other breeds in the long-strong wool or mutton class. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The [Merino] breed is still widely used to cross with long-woolled breeds to produce the half-bred. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,129</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> This breed [Lincoln] was used extensively for crossing with the merino to produce successive top-cross generations, colonial half-bred (wool 50s count), three-quarter bred (wool 46s), and crossbred (wool 40s to 44s count). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/236</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Half-bred: A sheep which originates froma merino-Leicester, merino-Lincoln or merino-Romney cross. They are used extensively in the high country and in the drier down-country districts of the South island. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,187</i>)

>  Halfbred: A cross between two breeds, usually an F1. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,15</i>)

>  a sheep containing between a quarter and three-quarters merino blood <i>(1982,NZ Almanac,248</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> `Our 45,000 halfbreds summer high on the Nevis block,' says Frank, `and winter far below on sunny spurs.' <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Halfbred Merino crossed with long-woolled breeds - Lincoln or Leicester or Romney. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> ... my style of breaking out the neck by turning the head and coming out behind the ears could not be applied on these loose, wrinkle-necked half-breds. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,97</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Halfbred. Breed of sheep developed from the Merino and one of the long-wooled breeds - Leicester, Lincoln or Romney. it is a dual-purpose meat/wool sheep with emphasis on wool production. Has a fine to medium wool that sells well. is mainly found in the South island foothills but is sometimes grazed with Merinos in the higher regions of the Southern Alps. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breed of sheep developed in New Zealand through the crossing of Merino with one or other of the long-woolled breeds: English Leicester, Lincoln and Romney. [1800s]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The half-bred sheep, being the various crosses between Merino ewes and long-woolled rams. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,265</i>)

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> Breeders quickly discovered that compared to the Merino the Half-breds also had improved fertility and meat, and today they are farmed for their good performance in adverse climatic conditions. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,55</i>)

>  After the great snowfalls of 1878 .. half-breds and crossbreds took the place of merinos. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,22</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The half-bred ewe is one of the most valuable sheep New Zealand possesses and has played no small part in establishing the name 'Prime Canterbury.' <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,38</i>)

### half-bred country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a low-country pastoral area where half-breds, less hardy than Merinos, can safely be grazed all year round.

>  Half-bred ewe country, which comprises downs and the best of the foothill country ... [half-bred ewe country] <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,100</i>)

### half-bred wool
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of wool grown by either Corriedale, New Zealand Halfbred, or similar sheep containing between a quarter and three-quarters Merino blood [(woolgrowers')]

>  ~ Type of wool growth by either Corriedale, New Zealand Halfbred, or similar sheep containing between a quarter and three-quarters Merino blood <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

### half-breed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sheep drafting, 4000, consisting of pure Leicester, pure Shropshire downs and halfbreeds of both sorts. <i>(Jun 11, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,269,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> Half Breeds ... Other Breeds ... Cross Breeds ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(rare)]

>  <b>HAY</b> Half-breeds, or Anglo-Maori men and women, form no inconsiderable section of the native community. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/135</i>)

### half-bull
 
 <i>adj.</i> half-a-crown, or a half-crown, see also bull.

>  (Loafer from Loafer, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson] Following are other local money-names...2.6d half -bull, half-caser or two-and-a-buck... <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>)

>  Half crown [:] half dollar, half bull <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,151</i>)

>  The crown was called a bull, a cartwheel, a caser, five bob, and most familiar of all a dollar ... The half crown was called a half-dollar, a half-bull, half-caser, two-and-a-kick, and two-and-a-buck. in the latter instance buck stands for sixpence, though apparently this term was rarely used by itself for that coin denomination, but almost always in association with a number of shillings. The florin seems to have had fewer English nicknames, only two bob being recorded as being in use in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)

### half-canned
 
 <i>adj.</i> Half = `fairly': OED a common formation with half : adverbial - tacked on when the sense suits.

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... most of the boys were half-canned by now on whiskey <i>ibid.</i>148 Most of the boys were half canned by now on whisky smuggled aboard the train at ismalia. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,135</i>)

### half-carriage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The unreality of the tunnel, of this half-carriage with people chatting or reading the morning paper ... as they are jolted along the cross-section of a volcano, drawn by an electric locomotive which would have astounded the pioneers who dug this hole through the hill. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,18</i>)

### half-caser
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Caser not recorded in NZ; half-a-caser ? esp in horseracing circles: obsolescent and rare by 1963).]

>  Following are other local money-names...2.6d half -bull, half-caser or two-and-a-buck... <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,Red Page</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from Loafer, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  Hubbub over Half-a-caser. . . Wrangle over sixpence. <i>(May 5, 1906,NZ Truth,46-5-5</i>)

>  He was knocked kite high, however, when the owner of the pocket-book, a man of means, sent along 2s 6d for the recovery of his pocket-book. He might have known that the reward would have gone to charity in any case, but half a dollar! Mother Mary Aubert is getting the half-caser, anyhow, and bad cess to him that it wasn't a guinea. <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,195/4/8</i>)

>  The crown was called a bull, a cartwheel, a caser, five bob, and most familiar of all a dollar ... The half crown was called a half-dollar, a half-bull, half-caser, two-and-a-kick, and two-and-a-buck. in the latter instance buck stands for sixpence, though apparently this term was rarely used by itself for that coin denomination, but almost always in association with a number of shillings. The florin seems to have had fewer English nicknames, only two bob being recorded as being in use in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)

### half-caste
 
 <i>n.</i> [(?now derog.)]

>  The majority herded together, eyeing the "gentlefolk" with a touch of distrust, but themselves remaining haughtily aloof from the Maoris and half-castes who joined their ranks. <i>(Dec 28, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,Magazine Section 1</i>)

>  Last week the half-caste told a native all about it. <i>(Apr 16, 1864,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  "My mother was a half-caste," she says, unselfconsciously, using a term that is taboo in these more sensitive times. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Sydney gave it as his legal opinion that the child was "the only legal half-caste in New Zealand." <i>(Jul 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> She contracted tuberculosis, then an almost incurable disease which was regarded as endemic amongst half-castes. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,45</i>)

>  it harks back to the days when the pejorative term "half-caste" was used to describe people of mixed ancestry and a Maori was defined in statutes as a person who had half or more "Maori blood". <i>(Sep 3, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),98</i>)

>  i always thought of myself as a half-caste until i got to university, and they said: "Oh no, that's a racist term." <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 43</i>)

>  Maori (other than "half-castes") were legally forbidden from standing as candidates in general electorates until 1967. Maori voters (other than "half-castes") were forbidden from enrolling on the general roll till 1975. <i>(Feb 11, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>DONNE</b> Some of the Anglos-Maori half-castes are no more superstitious than is the average white man. <i>(1927,Maori Past and Present,270</i>)

>  <b>SMiTH</b> There was a half caste there from Wanganui, who came to saw timber .. <i>(Jan 22, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 22 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 370]

>  Two half castes came on their way to Baldwins <i>(May 2, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> At Port Adventure there is a small settlement, now chiefly peopled by half-castes. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,138</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Feb 15 T.Heale in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 138]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> [Maori women] frequently form matrimonial alliances with Europeans, and the result of these marriages is the finest race of half-castes, perhaps, in the world. <i>(1866,Polynesia,144</i>)

>  We had Alex Shand ... on board, two women, two white men and six natives mostly halfcasts. <i>(Mar 19, 1875,Chudleigh Diary,234,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiNGSTON</b> it is only necessary to look at these half-castes in New Zealand to believe in the virtue and value of mixing blood - `miscegenation'. <i>(1879,Australian Abroad, The,367</i>)

>  <b>F.E.BAUME</b> Half-caste [book title] <i>(1933,Half Caste</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> His sister married a whaler known as Pratt, and their son Tame Parata, the M.H.R., married a sister of Bob ... Brown, so that Tame and his wife were halfcastes. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,21</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Several informants said they would like to be born full-blooded Maoris instead of half-castes. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,293</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The only European [in the shearing gang] was the classer, but, of course, some of the team were half-castes, and what a mighty job this team did, ... and Joe Gillies walked the board as boss. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>MEiKLE</b> i can remember only a few Maori children at school and a few who were overtly `half-castes' or who had `a touch of the tarbrush' - labels commonly applied. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>)

>  Describing himself as a half-caste, Judge Jurie said that there were many times he was tempted to publicly reject allegations of Maori bias. ... `But it is saying that Anglos can hear Maori cases and they will never be accused of bias ... it assumes that Anglo are fair but Maori are not and that hurt me a great deal.' <i>(Mar 14, 1989,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> Further along [sc. Tinakori Road].. lived an endless family of halfcastes who appeared to have planted their garden with empty jam tins and old saucepans and black iron kettles without lids. <i>(1947,Journal of Katherine Mansfield,504</i>) [NOTES: 1916 quote]

>  no one called me Manu off Playschool or darkie / i was a milk chocolate glass and a half / half caste / caramello enough to be safe from bitter dark accusations <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  Much as we admired the Maori race, we were even more struck by the half-castes. <i>(Apr 1884,Country Magazine (London),919</i>)

>  The controversial "half caste" writer and researcher, now working at Waikato University researching Maori customary law, mainly from Maori language manuscripts, says he acknowledges being half caste is problematic for some people. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),9</i>)

>  Half-casts [sic], they said, had gone there but no Europeans were allowed there yet. <i>(Sep 26, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  These novel tastes were inculcated by his [sc. Te Whiti's] half-caste son-in-law. <i>(Apr 2, 1904,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  Tom Ellison was a half-caste and accompanied the Maori football team to England. <i>(Oct 8, 1904,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  Mr Thorpe was stopped by an attendant with the remark, "No Maoris or half-castes allowed here." <i>(Jan 25, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  it's people like her and her husband that give rednecked "whities" the wrong idea about "cheekie darkies. (i'm allowed to say that because up until the age of 10 i was classified as a "half-caste" and other less kind euphemisms so i figured i'm owed some payback. <i>(Dec 29, 2004,Capital Times,p4</i>)

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> [The children are] much lighter in colour than i should have thought; it is probable ... that those i have seen are half-caste. <i>(Mar 28, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 28 Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 100]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Some [whalers] had native women & i noticed several fine half cast children. <i>(Apr 1843,Journal,16</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Apr Taylor Journal 1842-1843 [TS:ATB] 16]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> As the half-caste children usually speak both languages, they may be of great use [in instructing their mothers], if they can first be well taught. <i>(Feb 5, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Feb 5 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part iii (Church in the Colonies Viii) 27]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The number of half-cast children is, as yet, very trifling; probably little more than three hundred. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,77</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> in this pah there was a pretty little half-caste girl, as white as any European; ... <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,42</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> in this pah there was a pretty little half-caste girl, as white as any European; ... <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,42</i>)

>  There were 1,626 natives of Scotland, 715 of England, 141 of ireland, and 1,092 of New Zealand. These figures also include the half-caste population <i>(Aug 12, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>) [NOTES: [Report on the Otago census of 1857(?)]]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> With provisions and compass he followed the undoubted tracks of the [lost] party, those of Dr.Schmidt, a Maori boy, and a half-caste boy; ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,165</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The other day a half-caste woman friend of Oparure ... begged me ... to lay before the public ... the following distressing facts. <i>ibid.</i> 284 Our Half-Caste Population. [title] <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,107</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Some of the whalers having been early on the coast and having Maori wives, their halfcaste children were mates of mine, but Waikouaiti being one of the later whaling stations, many of the halfcastes were younger than i. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,24</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> A year later the half-caste claims were still as far from settlement as they had been in 1864, ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,169</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> But i cannot imagine that she displayed any emotion, certainly not to ... the Maoris squatting in the shade of pepper trees, or the ... half-caste fishermen ... yarning about the jetty. <i>ibid.</i> 16 i should have to share a tiny schoolroom ... with some thirty other Maori, half-caste, and pakeha children. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,14,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> Whether the half-caste children will take to the woods remains to be ascertained.] <i>(1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,232,1932 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> (fn) Rauna i katoa is a half-caste word, the English word round being here Maorified, and thus makes a more emphatic expression than any of their own. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,134</i>)

### half-cocked
 
 <i>adj.</i> esp. in the expression go off half-cocked, common in NZ since at least the 1940s.

### half-crowns
 
 <i>n.</i> Cuts in sheep when carelessly dagging; or holes carelessly cut in pelts when skinning [(farming) (usu. pl.) ca1930 at least: Plute Warren]

### half-cut
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  [?the big half cut (is this a mishearing or comps mistake for a bit half cut?)] Michael appeared bright and early at the Dunedin Police Court on Monday morning - and he was the big half cut into the bargain ... . As soon as he got there [into the dock], Sub-inspector Phair said, `i don't think accused is in a fit condition to plead.' <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/7/2</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> You can make an appointment any time. But don't come staggering over from the pub, half cut. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,146</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> half cut: What you are after half a bottle of brandy. More than half DRUNK. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,50</i>)

>  <b>NEALE</b> i was always waiting for her to bring it up first, for her to crack some smarmy comment about Marion being smashed, half-cut, pissed-as-a-fart, arse-faced as usual. <i>(1998,Night Swimming,8</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> "Must be still half-cut," agreed Curly Daniels. <i>(1964,Watersiders,77</i>)

>  Even parliamentarians can''t hire a taxi when they're half-cut, in case they're accused of showing off. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheepdog that does not last at work. (See [sooner](sho-spa.md#sooner))

>  .. backdoor pensioner (retired working dog) 10 o'clock dog (one that won't work during the heat of the day) ... [ten o'clock dog] <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### half-decker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We dragged my old half-decker through the shallow water to the channel, carried the girls aboard and hoisted the sails. <i>(Dec 21, 1918,NZ Herald,Suppl. : 6</i>)

### half-dollar
 
 <i>n.</i> Half-a-crown

### half-fat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal that requires finishing or fattening.

### half-fonged
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk (on methylated spirits).

>  <b>AGNEW</b> ... i watched three strangers, two of them already half-fonged, heating methylated spirits in a [98] pan while passing a piece of bread over it, the theory being that the bread would soak up the oil like a blotter, leaving a clear spirit. <i>(1974,Loner, The,97</i>)

### half-G
 
 <i>n.</i> a flagon, usually of beer, and orig. of half-gallon capacity; b) its contents; =flagon, =jar, =peter, =half-gallon jar.

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> A real shithot bloke. Jesus, can he sink the piss. Real hard case that joker. Yah should seen him knocking back those half Gs. Pissed as a fart. ... spewed his ring out. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,85</i>)

>  <b>MiTCHELL</b> half-G: Less than 50 per cent of a full-G. Also known in Dunedin as a peter, after the first missionary to land in that city, St Peter Speight. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  .. my brother throws a barbecue. After many (half)-g's have been drained, his lawyer friend dozes ... <i>(1973,islands,6/359</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Leeming (Back Home) in islands [spg 1/2-g]]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> The flagon-parties, the crates of half-gees on the sink, men in singlets queued up at the keg; ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,110</i>)

>  <b>GiFFORD</b> ... they're off home from the party as quick as a sheila who's copped a half gee down the front of her dress. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,34</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> ... Harrison Pona in his black singlet ... clamouring to borrow spare half-gees, ... <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,51</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> half g:Now superceded by its metric equivalent (2.25 liters). This is the standard sized bottle for taking home draught beer from the hotel. if there are only one or two of you, then 2-3 of these would be standard. Oh yes, a half g is HALF AN iMPERiAL GALLON bottle, almost always containing draught beer. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,50</i>)

>  (Goldie Brown Collection) 1/2 g. <i>(1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

>  [advert] <i>(Mar 18, 1982,Dominion</i>)

>  New Zealand's traditional take-away beer container - the flagon, also known as a `jar', `half-G' or `peter' - was yesterday defended by its manufacturer.... <i>(Oct 6, 1982,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Almost as traditional as half-Gs of beer at rugby club socials are those bowls of dip and potato crisps which grace the bars and coffee tables at New Zealand social gatherings. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,38</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> ... could've been the couple of half g's i had though, or that hunk of pork. <i>ibid.</i> 260 Have a mimi, grab a couple of half g's, and walk back. <i>(1938,Bone People, The,168</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Suzie ... [11] settled down for the day behind the changing sheds with ten men and a dozen half G's. <i>(1985,Secret Diary of a Telephonist,10</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> But five dollar will only get a half-g and a half-g'll only half-blur us. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,20</i>)

>  Depending on regional expressions, [draught-beer containers] are variously known as a `jar', `peter', `half-gee' (half-gallon) or the more modern `pub pet' two-litre plastic bottle. <i>(Jul 1988,Pacific Way,50</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> half-g flagon of draught beer; shortened form of imperial measure half-gallon. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,54</i>)

>  He was delighted to be offered a drink from what his host described as a "half-G." <i>(Mar 3, 1963,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  Hey. You wouldn't have a half-gee in your baggage, i suppose? <i>(Jan 2, 1993,Evening Post,6</i>)

### half-pie
 
 <i>adv.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> imperfect; half-way; (neither one thing nor the other); mediocre (sometimes used to express an unsatisfactory incompleteness), (cf. its neither my arse nor John Jackson's); or else (of mechanical things) merely = `half' cf 1950 quotation. [poss. from Ma. pai good]. Half-and-half; half-way; (neither one thing nor the other); mediocre (sometimes used to express an unsatisfactory incompleteness), (cf. its neither my arse nor John Jackson's); or else (of mechanical things) merely = `half' cf 1950 quotation. [(informal]

>  [The Government's] handling of this over the last 10 days has been ham-fisted. it has been a series of half-pie defences. <i>(Feb 27, 1999,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Anything less is half pie, half-baked. <i>(Sep 2, 1999,City Voice,2</i>)

>  But half-pai Kiwi won't cut it, he says. it's got to be full-bore. <i>(Dec 1998,Mana,61</i>)

>  <b>iRELAND</b> There were no half-pie solutions. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,146</i>)

>  The voting was of no `half-pie' order, 205 in favour of the proposal, and 22 votes against. <i>(Dec 8, 1911,Maoriland Worker,40/13/1</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Half-pie. insincere; little respected; (rather) contemptible: New Zealanders': C. 20. See pie. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,368</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> She would rather have a Maori who was a real man than a half-pie Pakeha who talked too much. <i>ibid.</i> 67 They came to the settlement - just a few straggling houses and a half-pie store and a packing-shed hall ... <i>ibid.</i> 78 a few straggling houses and a half-pie store. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,16</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Many of the middle generation also understand ... [this `deep' language] but most of them and of the younger generation of adults speak a sort of colloquial Maori (a `half-pie' Maori is the slang expression which the younger generation apply to it). <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,272</i>)

>  The gate was half-pie open. <i>(Sep 29, 1949,Dominion Post,29</i>)

>  <b>WADMAN</b> New Zealanders who go home on scholarships and come back half-pie Englishmen <i>(1949,Life Sentence,8</i>)

>  Got a half-pie farm out the north road. Doin well. <i>ibid.</i> 227 ... all the half-pie pannikin bosses ordering me about because there's no-one like the working man for putting the boot into the working man. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,147</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> <i>ibid.</i> 227 ... all the half-pie pannikin bosses ordering me about because there's no-one like the working man for putting the boot into the working man. <i>(1960,Word for Word,104</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> i don't trust these half-pie good times. They can't last <i>(1963,Coal Flat,7</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> ... if he shoots his expensive heading bitch after one half-pie mistake, what's he going to do to me when i get cracking? <i>(1964,Watersiders,72</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word half-pie means `incompletely realized' or `only half'. it often occurs in inarticulate colloquial speech, very frequently with some speakers, as an equivalent of `sort of' or `kind of' when exact words will not come. Some derive it from Maori pai `good'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,49</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> ... but you can't bring your pakehaness into my home unless i say so and i won't! This is a Maori home ... bringing up his children to be Maoris and not half-pie imitation pakehas. <i>(1969,Green River,56</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> `Whereabouts do you live?' `in a bed-sitter half-pie flat sort of thing.' <i>(1974,Maori Woman,109</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> The bach must be sitting right afterwards ... No half-pie work, you hear? <i>(1981,Fish Factory,157</i>)

>  half-pie adj. N.Z. inf. ill planned; not properly thought out: a half-pie scheme. [ < Maori pai good] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,505</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 339; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 223]]

>  The Maori Language Bill offered merely `crumbs' to the Maori people in its official recognition of their language, ... Professor Sid Moko Mead, said yesterday. The `half-pie' measures had to be strengthened to reflect the findings of the Waitangi Tribunal which had recommended allowing the use of Maori on all public occasions, ... <i>(Aug 20, 1986,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  She'd make delightful circular movements with her tiny, rubbery nose; occasionally letting off ... half pie grunts. <i>(Aug 7, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),B2</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> half-pie half-hearted or poorly performed; probably Maori 'pai', good; eg `The whole team had been on the booze the night before and were only playing half-pie.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,54</i>)

>  The odd swagger would still come up to the house, wanting his billy filled. One of them was a good old bloke who i think was half-pie related to Mum. She always gave him tea when he arrived, and one time Dad gave him a pair of old clodhoppers. But he never stayed in the house. There was an old whare down the road that he used to sleep in. <i>(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam,6</i>)

>  included in the book are over 300 words from jandal, formed from Japanese sandal, to the Maori-English blend half pai. it takes the Maori word pai for "good" and blends it with half to mean half good. <i>(Jun 26, 2002,Capital Times,8</i>)

>  He half pie knocked off after that, but i don't think he could see the other horse coming with his winkers on. <i>(Apr 2, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

>  Among them [sc. English sailors] was a Maori who had been at sea with them so long that.. he spoke with a genuine half-pi "Ay, Choom" accent! <i>(Aug 19, 1953,NZ Observer,14</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> She would rather have a Maori who was a real man than a half-pie Pakeha who talked too much. <i>ibid.</i> 67 They came to the settlement - just a few straggling houses and a half-pie store and a packing-shed hall among the cabbage-trees on a river-flat. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,16</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  i'd tell them what i think o'them ... . . There's no half-pie about this kid- -->i have never heard the Part. use, or heard of it. Half-pie =half -' used frequently, and perhaps has Maori origin (check Wms), quotes Finlayson - Maori Nthland, and i have heard it frequy in Hlock. Part cf pyah. `weak; paltry; inferior; useless.' mainly naut. (1864) pye A contraction of pariah-dog: Anglo - ind mily (-1886) Yule and Burnell. P: Sup. pie-ard straight fr. the Hindustani comes the R.A.F. Regulars' nuance `any dog' (Jackson) The Army in Nth Africa (1940-3) used pie-dog of any desert dog. [all of which is muddy and contradictory: possibly pie a corruption or derivn fr. Hindi - check] <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,11</i>)

### halfbeak
 
 <i>n.</i> garfish, skipper, ihi, garfish (from the name `reporhamphus', descriptive of the fish).

>  <b>HECTOR</b> This name is applied both to the SKiPPER (<i>Scombresox Forsteri</i>) and to the ihi or Half Beak (<i>Hemiramphus intermedius</i>) which are allied fishes representing those of the same names in the British seas. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,118</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> GAR FiSH (<i>Hemiramphus intermedius</i>). ... [34] This name is applied both to the skipper (<i>Scombresox Forsteri</i>) and to the ihi or half-beak, which are allied fishes representing those of the same names in the British seas. The representatives of the former genus, however, though common in England, appear to be rare in these seas, while the half-beak, which is of rare occurrence in the British seas, is common all round New Zealand, and is the ordinary gar-fish that is so highly appreciated, especially in Auckland and Dunedin. <i>ibid.</i> 40 HALF-BEAK (see GAR FiSH). <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GARFiSH or PiPER (TAKEKE) <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> Phillipps. This fish is known in Auckland as Piper, although in Otago it is called Garfish or Guard Fish. ... it is on account of this peculiarity [a long bottom jaw] that they are sometimes called Halfbeaks. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,157,(2nd edn) ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The name Half-beaks, which is generally applied to [garfish] in other countries, refers to their uniquely formed mouth, in which the upper jaw is normal, but it is the lower jaw which is produced into a long beak-like process. <i>ibid.</i> 169 Garfish <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> Piper; Guard-fish; Half-beak <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GARFiSH ... <i>Other names: Reporhamphus ihi</i>; piper, half-beak; ihe, takeke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,206</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GARFiSH [<i>Reporhamphus ihi</i>] ... Also called piper, halfbeak, ihi, and takake. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,16</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> HALFBEAK (a) Scientific name: <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Garfish; Piper. (c) Other common names: None known. (d) Principal localities where taken: North Auckland, Auckland and Cook Strait. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,29</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> The Garfish, or Piper, or Long-Tom (Australia) or Half-beak is distinct from the New Zealand Needlefish which has a beak on both upper and lower jaws, the Piper's is only on the lower jaw. <i>ibid.</i> 120 HALF-BEAK <i>See Garfish </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,115</i>)

>  [species name] garfish .. [scientific name] <i>Hyporhamphus ihi</i> .. [other name(s)] piper, halfbeak, needlefish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] halfbeak 958-69 <i>(1987,Bibliography of NZ Fishes</i>)

### halfway home
 
 <i>n.</i> a halfway house [reflecting the kinder, more euphemistic use of -home instead of the harsher -house, -hospital).]

>  ... . advocacy of half-way homes for persons suffering from psychiatric disorders. Many patients entering our mental hospitals today are voluntary boarders and, as such are in possession of their powers of reasoning ... they may be put in wards with patients suffering from all types of mental disorders. Half-way homes could overcome this problem by being used for these people ... <i>(Sep 3, 1952,Evening Post,8</i>)

### halfway house
 
 <i>n.</i> hotel (orig. cf OED halfway to one's destination, and so a convenient stopping-place); in NZ, a coaching term which is still extant in this extended use for a `country pub' among very old people (ca1947-8 at least).

>  <b>AMNEMA</b> We had just left a hotel or `half-way house' four miles distant <i>(1874,Trials and Travels of a Dominie,244</i>)

>  <b>CHAMiER</b> ... to pay for a week's spree at the half-way house. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,157</i>)

>  <b>KNiGHT</b> We went [from Wellington to Porirua] in a cart stopping at the `half-way house' for dinner, getting to Mr Cowper's in the afternoon. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,22</i>) [NOTES: [reference C.1849, Porirua] c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 22]

>  <b>JOSKEN</b> At a shelter called `Half-way House' we left the car and went the rest of the way [to the Hermitage] by coach. <i>(1968,Turn Back the Clock,77</i>) [NOTES: [Mackenzie Country, c.1910, from Pukaki]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an approximate halfway (stopping) place on a journey.

>  <b>McMURRAN</b> Timaru is the `half-way house' by railway from Christchurch to Dunedin <i>(1904,From New York to NZ,64</i>)

### halfy
 
 <i>n.</i> from halfwit. [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 1 HALFY (3) [silly person] -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 2 HALFY (3)]

### halibut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> We have, indeed, a wealth of fish in all the tidal waters. Sharks, schnapper, rock-cod, mackerel, mullet, herring, sole, halibut, albacore, barracouta, king-fish, and others. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/230</i>)

### haliotis
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Haliotis</i> spp. for paua in occas. (? educated) usage.

>  <b>WARD</b> We tried the haliotis this evening boiled. <i>(Jun 1851,Journal of Edward Ward,197,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The roof was well fastened down with ropes of the climbing fern (<i>mangemange</i>: Lygodium volubile) and the carved work of the gables was inlaid with haliotis (<i>paua</i>) shell. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,92</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The rocks leading to Houghton Bay and Terawhiti were a hunting ground for haliotis, or paua shellfish, a large black and uninviting shellfish. <i>(1952,Faring South,77</i>)

### hall
 
 <i>n.</i> any corridor, passage or extended landing in a house. [Jim writes NZPOD (but not DNZE) records this extended NZ use. In BritE it seems to be limited in this sense to what we would specifically call a <i>front hall</i> - an area into which the front door opens. I can support this by an anecdote of a minor domestic catastrophe. My wife (who grew up in England) asked my son to strip the wallpaper in the hall, by which she meant the front hall. He did this but went on to deal with a passage at the top of the stairs!]

### Hall's totara
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Podocarpus hallii</i>

>  55 Lithograph of `Hall's totara' foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> Hall's totara ... <i>Dacrydium intermedium </i> <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: (And) Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  The kahikatea stands next in abundance, and is closely approached by the kamai, while the miro, matai, totara, Hall's totara, are generally distributed; but the northern rata and pukatea scarcely occur south of Greymouth. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,330</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>DENNiS</b> Hall's totara ((i) <Podocarpus hallii</i>) is now uncommon in the west [of Westland]. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,159</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> (i) <Podocarpus hallii</i> HALL'S TOOTARA ... Thin-bark totara <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,52</i>)

>  Above 900 m hard beech is replaced by red beech which begins to dominate the canopy up to the summits of the ikawhenua Range. Also at these altitudes are Hall's totara and tawari. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,60</i>)

>  The block is considered important because it contains the largest of three remaining stands of matai forest in the Wairau Valley, as well as original beech forest and Hall's totara. <i>(May 2003,Forest & Bird,308/22</i>)

>  From this species there are some excellent ground covering varieties prized by landscapers. Another is the Hall's totara (Podocarpus hallii) which grows up to 21m and achieves a spread of about 1.8m. <i>(Oct 1, 2010,Waikato Times,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### halo hair
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long hair extending beyond the birthfleece in a Romney lamb.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> At docking time the special marking of lambs with excessive numbers of halo-hairs is a useful aid to later selection. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,27</i>)

>  Halo hair: Long coarse fibres which stand out from the birth coat of some lambs. Generally shed within two months of birth. Also called mother hair. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

>  There is good reason to believe that halo-hairs are the first constituents of the fleece to begin their development prior to birth. Halo-hairs from a central position on the britch are often noticeably long ... <i>(Jan 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  These [coarse fibres] are seen in the 'halo hairs' found in many lambs ... <i>(Oct 11, 1951,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Away back in 1929 wool manufacturers complained of a hairiness in New Zealand's Romney wool clip: large, straight and hairy fibres, 'halo hairs' projected above the fleece in the birthcoat of the lamb. Looking about for lambs with halo hairs, Dr Dry found a ram plentifully supplied with them. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,141</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> Among the progeny of ewes which had few hairy fibres were several lambs which had high halo-hair density like their sire. in contrast, others were almost devoid of halo hairs. [halo-hairs] <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,185</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Hamilton jetboat
 
 <i>n.</i> a boat propelled by a Hamilton jet unit, designed by Sir Charles William ("Bill") Fielden Hamilton [1899-1978].

>  The books deals first with the experimental years in the development of the unit and jet boat in Hamilton's work shop... The book also recounts the development of different makes of jet unit following the success of the Hamilton jet unit overseas, particularly in America. <i>(Jun 23, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),69</i>)

>  <b>BLOXHAM</b> A jet boat is a standard boat fitted with a car engine to drive a sophisticated pump or "jet unit". This draws water in through an intake in the bottom of the hull and forces it out at high pressure, jet-like, through a nozzle in the rear. Unlike a conventional boat, the jet boat has neither propellor or rudder. instead, it is pushed along, and steered, simply by the thrust of the jet stream <i>(1983,Jet Boat,1</i>)

>  <b>BLOXHAM</b> For 30 years Sir William Hamilton's 'jet boat' has given thousands of New Zealanders pleasure and excitement as well as useful commercial service <i>(1983,Jet Boat,xi</i>)

>  <b>bloxham</b> it was addressed simply: "Hamilton Jet Boat inventor, New Zealand." <i>(1983,Jet Boat,23</i>)

### Hamilton's frog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  All are nationally threatened, with the rarest, Hamilton's frog (Leiopelma hamiltoni) numbering less than 300. <i>(Oct 12, 2006,Wellingtonian,8</i>)

>  The rare Hamilton's frog (Leiopelma hamiltoni) confined to remnant populations on Maud and Stephens islands in Cook Strait, has been bred in captivity for the first time ...) <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,17</i>)

 <i>v.</i> [1951 16+ M 32 St Mary's, Wgton 30A HAMMER HOLY HELL OUT OF EACH OTHER -->1951 16 M 26 Wellington H.S. 30A HAMMERED -->1951 14-15 M 33 Wellington H.S. 30A HAMMERS]



 
 <i>n.</i> heroin [Newbold says this dates from the mid-1970s, and coincides with the involvement of the criminal element in the drug trade.]

### hammerhand
 
 <i>n.</i> a carpenter's labourer or assistant

>  Havelock North Construction Ltd require <B>Tradesmen and Hammerhands</b> <i>(Oct 22, 2005,Hawke's Bay Today,5</i>)

Form work and framing etc. Grade 6: Skilled, experienced hammer-hand. Form work, framing and finishing etc. All tools. <i>(Oct 27, 2004,Northern Courier,5</i>)

Employment opportunities are as hammer-hand, a trainee carpenter, or construction labourer - then progressing to leading hand, and later qualified tradesperson. <i>(2006,Life's What You Make it</i>) [NOTES: http://www.waiariki.ac.nz/content.aspx?iD=100006151 Accessed 09/02/2006. site updated 2005.]

### hammerhead shark
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of shark with a head that resembles a double-headed hammer; also called mangopare, see shark, hammerhead. [also as hammer-headed shark] (See [shark](sa-sho.md#shark))

>  NOW ON EXHiBiTiON <i>AT THE</i> OCCiDENTAL HOTEL ..The Hammer-headed Shark, the Porcupine Fish ... <i>(Jul 23, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>) [NOTES: Advert]

>  The following donations have been received during the past year at the Museum .. Mr. T. F. Cheeseman (Museum, Auckland), skin of a hammer-headed shark ... <i>(Mar 18, 1887,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  A huge hammer-headed shark, 11ft. long, was seen swimming about Arran Bay, Waiheke, for about two hours the other morning. <i>(Dec 29, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 122. ZYGAENA MALLEUS. Risso. Hammer-headed Shark. <i>(1872,Fishes,76</i>)

>  <i>Hammer Shark (Scyllum laticeps.)</i>> - A small specimen of this strange sea-monster was caught in a net at Port Ahuriri, in April. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,485</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> <i>Zygaena malleus </i>> .. Hammer-headed shark. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,115</i>)

>  On these are the bollards - not the great blocks of wood on the King's and other wharves, but curious cast-iron things with a top like a hammer-headed shark leaning outwards. <i>(Nov 27, 1909,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>mango pare Hammer-headed shark; Zygoena malleus.</i>> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,87</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Shark, ... The sharks of New Zealand are -- ...<i>Hammer-head S.- Zygaena malleus </i>> (Maori name, <i>Mangopore </i>> ) <i>(1898,Austral English,412</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>mango ... pare (Zygoena malleus, hammer-headed shark. </i>> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,207</i>)

>  Synonyms: <i>Squalus zygaena; Zygaena malleus; Cestracion zygaena </i>> . The hammer-headed shark, or mango pare (<i>Sphyrna zygaena </i>> ), appears for the most part to live in the open sea . <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/262</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHiL;LiPS</b> <i>Sphyrna zygaena </i>> (Linnaeus). HAMMER-HEADED SHARK; MANGOPARE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,7,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Sphyrna zygaena </i>> : Hammer-head. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> HAMMERHEAD SHARK (<i>Sphyrna lewini </i>> ), Mangopare of the Maoris, is well named, .. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,60</i>)

>  Hammerhead Shark <i>Sphyrna lewini </i>> (Griffith) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,397</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 397]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>mango-pare Sphyrna lewini, hammerhead shark.</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,178</i>) [NOTES: [?dateline] Zygoena, (mangepare), hammer-headed shark, a very dangerous fish, and held in great dread by the natives.]

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Hammerhead shark .. .. mangopare .. .. <i>Sphyrna </i>> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HAMMERHEAD SHARK ... <i>Other names: Sphyrna lewini </i>> ; mango-pare (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,180</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAMMERHEAD SHARK [<i>Sphryna lewini </i>> ] ... The Maori name mango-pare is still in use but is met with only rarely. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,12</i>)

>  [species name] hammerhead shark .. [scientific name] <i>Sphyrna zygaena </i>> .. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Hammerhead shark Sphyrna zygaena </i>> A widely distributed species in this family (Sphyrnidae), occurring in warm temperate seas, mainly in coastal waters. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,27</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] shark, hammerhead 266-76 ..... SHARKS, HAMMERHEAD 264-76 (32) HORN ... SHARKS, HORN 132-7 (33) LEOPARD SHARK =TiGER SHARK <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> TiGER SHARK ... <i>other names: Galeocerdo cuvieri </i>> ; leopard shark, toiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,179</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> They almost rolled their eyes out of their sockets, distending their mouths, like hammer-headed sharks, from ear to ear; ... <i>ibid.</i> 1/323 The mango or <i>shark</i> (Squalus); <i>pilot-fish</i> (Scomber ductor); <i>flying-fish</i> (Exocoetus volitans); <i>hammer-headed shark</i> (Squalus zygoena); frequent the coast, expecially the River Thames, in vast shoals, and are preserved by the natives as winter food. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/162</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called para[as-acute] <i>paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Mango pare (squalus zygaena)</i> hammer-headed shark. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,412</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Zygoena, (mangepare)</i>, hammer-headed shark, a very dangerous fish, and held in great dread by the natives. <i>(1870,Te ika a Maui,626</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Mangoo-pare</i>, n. 1.<i> hammer-headed shark. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,75</i>)</i>
