### hand-drafting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a selection and separation of stock, physically lifting them over a fence or rail.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> .. all the sheep at Glenmark were hand-drafted. McMillan himself had a fondness for hand-drafting. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,275</i>)

### hand-milk
 
 <i>v.</i> to milk by hand distinct from milking by milking-machine. [ca1932 GT Diary (Dannevirke) -->ca1940 at least, Havelock.]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to milk a cow without machinery, i.e. with hands and bucket.

>  i hand-milked for nearly 10 years, but i don't tell my country friends now - they think i'm scared of cows! <i>(Apr 11, 1960,NZ Dairy Exporter,124</i>)

### hand-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Not having the needful to pay for tea, bed, and breakfast in Dannevirke, i called at Mr. Knight's station, where i got permission from the manager to sleep in the wool-shed, and got what is called amongst the brotherhood a `Hand out' that is, six biscuits, a bit of meat, and a billy-can full of tea. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

### hand-set
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> You fill up the pens and the handsets are oiled,/ And the boys in the denims, as yet quite unsoiled,/ ... /Now the engine has coughed and the hand-pieces whirr,/ There's a thud on the board and an almighty stir,/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Stronach `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 185]

### hand-shearer
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer who uses blades, not machine.

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> Prior to that, sheep in the district had to be shorn by hand-shearers. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

### hand-shearing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This position is explained by the fact that hand-shearing is still practised on many farms. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,434</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> it had been built for hand or blade shearing, so was fairly cramped when converted to a three-stand machine shearing shed. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,65</i>)

### hand-spread
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to distribute powdered fertiliser using a sack pack or satchel tied around one's chest and waist.

>  <b>TAiT</b> The long wearying hours spent hand-spreading are now past hardships of most backcountry farmers. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol ii,463</i>)

### hand-strip
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to milk first few drops of milk, and last few drops of milk, from a cow's bag by hand, using a machine for the main milking.

>  The necessity to strip cows after machine-milking can be obviated without loss of yield if due care is taken. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,542</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> We children chased the cows into the bails and helped them with the stripping. (Nowadays, with better machines, the cows do not have to be hand-stripped.) <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,36</i>)

### hand-stripping
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the milking of small amounts of milk both before and after the main emptying of a cow's bag.

>  in the Taranaki area, hand-stripping was still being practised on 30 per cent of the farms in the survey. <i>(Mar 1, 1955,NZ Dairy Exporter,42</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> .. a Consulting Officer reported on a farmer who had abandoned hand-stripping. Hand-stripping was a waste of time. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,61</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> .. hand-stripping was soon the exception and herd sizes started to increase. [handstripping] <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>iRELAND</b> Hand-stripping was the order of each day, to get the last drop of cream from the withered udders. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,86</i>)

### handbag exchange
 
 <i>n.</i> (euphemistic) a punch-up

>  On the way to the lineout he paused to exchange a few punches with Dallaglio and Joe Worsley. Soon after, he was in the thick of a mass handbag exchange. <i>(Jun 20, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### handbrake
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who is constantly telling his/her partner to stop

### handie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  if you're called [by adolescents] a ... handie ... you've been insulted. <i>(Apr 22, 1990,Dominion Sunday Times,4</i>)

### handjetter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a jet gun spray used to clear fleecewool on sheep of lice and keds.

>  .. comparisons between shower and plunge dipping and handjetters favour the jetter. The jetters sell for $146 plus GST. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

### handjetting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the spraying of animals with a jet spray to rid them of pests and diseases.

>  The wool length with handjetting is not as vital as with traditional dipping methods. When Andrew Hide heard Wools of New Zealand was promoting handjetting to control fly and lice he was afraid he'd be out of a job. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  Handjetting can be concentrated on the areas most susceptible to flystrike ... <i>(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

### handle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Customers of hotels were wanting to know why 12-ounce handles were not served. <i>(Nov 29, 1943,NZEF Times,8</i>)

>  We used to go out...we started drinking beer, shouting alternately. The first one's effortless, especially if it's that handle size. <i>(1985,Sites,11/14</i>) [NOTES: (Hodges `Drinking Vernacular')]

>  The normal method of address is "two ponies", "two mediums", "two longs", or "two handles." <i>(Jun 27, 1951,NZ Truth,18</i>)

>  Ale is not commonly served in "pint" containers in this country, but in what is better known as a handle. <i>(Oct 20, 1937,NZ Truth,22</i>)

>  A few days ago Sandy, with two "handles" of beer warming him into action, arrived at the Smithers' haven. <i>(Jul 25, 1925,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,60</i>)

>  Sixpence has been fixed as the price for a 12 ounce handle of beer in the four main centres. <i>(Dec 15, 1941,NZEF Times,i/25 : 4</i>)

### handle the jandal
 
 <i>phr.</i> meet a challenge [also the name of a DYI Music Video contest]

>  "it's all about giving it a go. Can you handle the jandal?" Gibbon says. <i>(Nov 20, 2008,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  The Kiwi colloquialism, "can you handle the jandal?", plus finding a large jandal washed up on the beach, inspired British-born Gibbons to establish the Radio Active Handle the Jandal D.i.Y New Zealand Music Video Awards ten years ago. <i>(Nov 20, 2008,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  it would appear the Paul Holmes cannot, as they put it nowadays, "handle the jandal". <i>(Aug 11, 1997,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  When they lose it in here i try to make them talk about it, i tell them face it, handle the jandal ... talk to me boy ... <i>(Sep 15, 1997,Dominion,13</i>)

>  Radio Active is hosting "Handle the Jandal", the NZ Music Video Night and "Fair Suck of the Sav", a NZ amateur Music Video competition at the Paramount Theatre. <i>(Jun 8, 2000,City Voice,5</i>)

>  Audience participation on the night will nail the Favourite Handle The Jandal Video award winner. <i>(Sep 9, 2004,Wellingtonian,2</i>)

### handlebars
 
 <i>n.</i> horns (of merino ram).

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Brownie selected one [merino] with a good pair of `handle bars' (horns) <i>(1949,High Country Days,67</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc] horns on sheep or cattle.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Eventually, Brownie selected one with a good pair of 'handlebars' (horns) and wading into the water, dragged the bucking, fighting animal through to the far side. <i>(1949,High Country Days,67</i>)

### handpicking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The T wing of the shed, where the wool press and bins are located.. has a large cellar underneath, into which the daggs and such of the wool as requires handpicking is passed through the hatchway in the floor above. <i>(Sep 16, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### handpiece
 
 <i>n.</i> a shearer's cutting tool; the hand-held portion of shearing machine. [(shearers')]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> [advt for] Cooper's Little Wonder Shearing Machine ... The Shearing Attachments comprise ... two Latest Model Cooper Handpieces. ... COOPER COMBS AND CUTTERS will fit any make of handpiece <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,xv</i>)

>  <b>SMiTH</b> Then the handpiece used to get so hot as to blister the shearers' hands. <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool industry,69</i>)

>  And as he got close [to the woolshed] he could hear the pop chuff ... of the engine; and then the whirring of the hand-pieces; . <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,107,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> You fill up the pens and the handsets are oiled,/ ... /Now the engine has coughed and the hand-pieces whirr,/ There's a thud on the board and an almighty stir,/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,185</i>) [NOTES: 1949 Stronach `Shearing' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 185]

>  <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,351/1</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Remember that one hears some shearers called butchers. in fact it is not the shearer that does the butchering, it is the comb on his handpiece. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>) [NOTES: See also p. 79]

>  <b>MiLLS</b> The handpieces of these earlier machines, resembling somewhat those of modern barbers, caused much dissatisfaction. Through overheating and blistering the shearer's hand they were labelled `Hot Boxes', `Smokers' and similar uncomplimentary terms. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,19</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,162</i>)

>  <b>MiDDELTON</b> `it'll be crutching time. ... Or have they taken their handpieces and mocs and gone to chase better money elsewhere?' <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,243</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> On the board the noise changed. There was the high whirr of the shearing hand-pieces. Ten stands, ten bodies bent over the sheep, bring off the fleeces with deft, sweeping blows. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,3</i>)

>  Handpiece: Hand-held portion of shearing machine . <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

>  `You're wrong,' i said. `it's going to be a scorcher of a day and we're set to do a record shear.' `Not a hope,' he said. `not [sic. l.c.] with these new handpieces. ... The shearers over at Fairview won't have a bar of them.' <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,33</i>) [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> The drone of handpieces. The bustle of women on the board. The cry of `Sheepo!' Hot dust. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,107</i>)

>  handpiece n. NZ & Austral. part of shearing machine held in the hand. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,341</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 224]]

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> None of them had ever shorn a sheep before, nor had they ever seen a handpiece correctly set up with comb and cutter. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,55</i>)

>  Shearing hand pieces worth nearly $7400 were stolen from a Palmerston North stock agency firm. Wrightson-NMA's ... shop lost seven hand shearing pieces, and many combs ... <i>(May 26, 1986,Manawatu Evening Standard,1</i>)

>  "Handpiece it is," Forde explained succinctly. "Something that fits in your hand." <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hand-held part of a shearing machine, used to comb and shear off fleece wool.

>  <b>MACiNTOSH</b> How i welcomed an unexpected stop for a change of cutter or comb on the handpiece. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,113</i>)

>  <b>SQUiRE</b> Handpiece - That part of the shearing machinery, held in the hand by the barrel, which, by the action of a cutter moving across a comb, shears the wool off the sheep. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,22</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> One hears some shearers called butchers. in fact, it is not the shearer that does the butchering, it is the comb on his handpiece. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> He 'breaks out' with a movement of wrist and forearm as they follow the handpiece. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,10</i>)

### handy dog
 
 <i>n.</i> a non-specialist working dog; a general-purpose sheep dog that will head, hunt, back, and perform. [(farming)]

>  Sheep dog.- Dog to work sheep; collie (q.v.). S.ds. run naturally either to bring sheep towards you or to drive them away; and when a puppy shows his preference he is broken in for that work. The dogs that bring sheep are called heading ds. (q.v.) and most of these can be taught to lead (q.v.) if desired. Dogs which take to driving sheep away are broken in to huntaway and are called htwys (q.v.). Some dogs will both hd. and htwy and are called handy ds. They are not generally high-class heading dogs but very useful about hand and sometimes very useful mustering dogs. Some men have a special slewing d., but a well-trained, hdg. dog or htwy. will usually slew (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Occasionally one sees dogs that will both head and hunt equally well. They are known as handy dogs. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,13</i>)

>  HANDY DOG: A dog which can do both heading and huntaway work. A good one is a champion indeed. <i>(1949,High Country Days,NEWTON,195 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> So the musterer of to-day, with his pack of five or six dogs, began to take shape. One or two of the border type - `heading dogs' as they're called now and three or four huntaways and `handy' dogs. <i>ibid.</i> 46 . . .Wait, the most wonderful dog i ever saw, a true `handy' dog. He would bring sheep out of any place where dog or sheep could go, and all his master ever said to him was `Here Wait! Here Wait!'; the rest was left to his own judgment and initiative and the sheep would be got. <i>(1951,High Country,5</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> My dogs were undoubtedly the worst in the gang - as a team. i had no heading dog, no decent handy dog, and a rough assortment of huntaways. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,15</i>) [NOTES: [musterers']]

>  <b>SHARPE</b> A sheepdog is either a huntaway, a heading dog, or a handy dog. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,97</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Handy Dog: A dog which can do both heading and huntaway work. This `all-round worker' is probably the most useful of all sheep dogs. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Handy dog: A dog which, endowed with the qualities of both the heading dog and the huntaway, is proficient at both jobs. A good one is a valuable asset and is usually the mainstay of the team. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,187</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Handy dog: One which can lead and pull sheep if required or stand and bark and hunt as well. Frequently heads when required to hunt and hunts when required to head! all the duties required in sheep yards and woolsheds <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Handy dog: A sometimes unpredictable combination of heading dog and huntaway. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  Handy dog: General-purpose dog that will head, hunt, back, and perform all the duties required in sheep yards and woolsheds. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

>  handy dog NZ dog possessing a variety of skills for the mustering of sheep. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,341</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### handy paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area close to a woolshed, cowshed, stable or homestead where stock can receive ready attention. (See [safe paddock](sa-sho.md#safe-paddock))

>  Cows about to calve should be brought into a handy paddock so that they can be kept under supervision. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Journal of Agriculture,26</i>)

>  During May most of the cows are dried off and the carryovers are grazed in a handy paddock near the shed. <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,35</i>)

>  <b>PEAT</b> Beyond the handy paddocks the Redan property appears to my urban eye to be an austere place for sheep. <i>(1992,Falcon and the Lark, The,50</i>)

### handy/handy dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cross between a hunting, heading and perhaps a backing dog in its functions. (See [shandygaff](sa-sho.md#shandygaff))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Handy dog: one which will head and pull, or hunt away and slew, or can be run out to a strategic spot and bark up. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,39</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The common source of the handy dog is a cross between a huntaway and a heading dog, known as a shandygaff. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,88</i>)

>  Like 'Speed' the only handy dog i have any use for is the heading dog with natural noise. The handy dog events one sees at trials are not won by this class of dog. <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,231</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Occasionally one sees dogs which will both head and hunt equally well. They are known as handy dogs ... <i>(1947,Wayleggo,13</i>)

>  The handy dog is used for all heading and pulling, and to be of real value, must get close to the sheep he is working ... <i>(Jan 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  Cross a header with a huntaway and you get a 'handy.' <i>(Jun 14, 1980,Southland Times,18</i>)

>  Handy - a dog that heads a bit, hunts with a bit of noise and possibly backs as well. Many true handies are from a huntaway-heading dog cross. <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> in those days a few of the big stations had head shepherds who relied on handy dogs to head with. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,82</i>)

### hanea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Panapana, Hanea, Nau, (Cardamine) New Zeal. cress. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),157</i>)

### hanehane
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The usual remedies were kokomuka (i.e., koromiko), a preparation of the shrub hanehane (<i>Geniostoma ligustrifolium</i>) <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/70</i>)

### hang
 
 <i>v.</i> (joc.?) be married

>  The wedding [of Abner] is to be at the survyiers hut in the bush ... 21st. ... [We] went to the bush together and saw Abner hung. i do not think a common man has had such a swell breakfast or, in this case, dinner, ... They had turkeys, fowls, hams, beef ... with all the etcs. <i>(Sep 20, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,103,1950 ed.</i>)

### hang by/on its reputation
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1949 Kaitaia, of physical objects (R. Mason) ca1949 (Wellington) A man describing a nearly-amputated finger as `hanging by its reputation'. ca1972 July The JBL industrial group, which had gone into receivership, described as `hanging on its reputation for more than a year before the crash'. (The last usage perhaps illustrates the acceptance of the phrase in N.Z. idiom, when its literal meaning can be revived to counterpoint its traditional idiomatic use, in a kind of quasi-pun. A number of years ago, during a discussion of an English murderer's appeal to commute his death sentence, I recall someone remarking drily that the condemned man `had no show. He was hanging merely on his reputation').]

### hang of a
 
 <i>adv.</i> (intensive) as adv. very; also as adj. very great, very fine; = hell/heck of a [-] (See [also hangashin](../A#also-hangashin),[heckashin](../H#heckashin))

>  <b>BAKER</b> in constant use by our youngsters ... hangava, hangashun <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Equally meaningless .. . are ... the intensives hanguva, hangershun - modified forms of helluva. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  Gosh, Dad's hangava crabby with you. <i>(1949,Landfall,11/145</i>) [NOTES: (B. Mason)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> They [children] got down in a hang of a hurry <i>(1945,When the Wind Blows,14</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> And they got down in a hang of a hurry and ran, ran until they were across the railway line and out of sight of the saleyards. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,10,(1974 ed.Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  it's a hang of a wet day <i>(1950,Our Street,33</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> it's a hang of a wet day <i>(1960,Maori Girl,64</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Children are notable users of slang ... and in these good old days the most current included hang of a, hangashun, heck of a and heckashun, all intensives. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hang of a/hanguva intensive regarding something big or forceful; euphemistic version of `helluva'; a fervent intensive favoured by juveniles; eg `That was a hanguva good flick, eh? NZA. - like hang euphemistic phrase for `like hell'; eg `That was a hang of a good movie, eh?' `Like hang it was!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,54</i>)

### hang one on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to strike with the fist [1984 16 M E84 Pakuranga Coll. HANG ONE ON (A PERSON)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There is some discussion about hanging one on each other bu the roar of the crowd diverts their attention to a Welsh forward ... <i>(1970,On the Ball,135</i>)

### hang out
 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> a development of 1.

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> `Going to hang out long?' `i beg your pardon.' `Going to stay long?' <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,204</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> to last out - to hang on; to hold out; endure. [ca1953 Jan. A typist says: `I hope his ulcer hangs out, I'd hate to see him retire.']

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> i've been pretty crook for the last hour. But i wanted to hang out till we saw a house. <i>(1944,Troop Target,87</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i>

>  When there is a job hanging out. <i>(1927,Transport Workers' Song Book,69</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>v.</i> to have one's tongue hanging out for. [(drug users') (Phs from 3.),]

>  `Hanging out' means feeling like an old man who has been worked over with an electric cattle prod ... going through hot and cold sweats, streaming eyes, nose runs, ... <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> As the price of normally cheap local gear inflated, junkies began hanging out for their normal supplies. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,14</i>)

>  There is a big difference between being stoned and `being held'. Addicts have to be at least held for their body responses to be `normal'. This is how the methadone programme works. it ... holds addicts, depending on their dosages, for about 24 hours, before they start hanging out...Peter's early-days addiction routine started with a `ping' in the morning and a cup of coffee. ... The time of the day he started `hanging out' depended on a number of factors, including work pressure. He would have a minimum of three pings a day, a maximum of about six... When addicts are hanging out they will hassle around, do and pay anything to get a ping. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)

### hang out for
 
 <i>v.</i> crave [(to have it hanging out for somebody, is a fairly common slang expression; = `to be carrying a torch for; be obviously and invitingly smitten by (a woman)')]

>  <b>BEATON</b> [?have tongue hanging out; ?hold out for] GiNNY pours...Come on, Ma. ...You know you're hanging out for a drop [of liquor]. <i>ibid.</i> 110 Hanging-out : longing (for something). <i>(1984,Outside in,64</i>)

### hang up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE</b> if the preparation had been scamped the drive might be spoilt by a `hang-up', due to some of the bigger trees not having been properly scarfed. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,49</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  hang up ... NZ & Austral. colloq. tether (a horse). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,341</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### hangashun/hangashin
 
 <i>adv.</i> var. (esp. children's) of the intensive hang of a, hanguva.

>  <b>GASKELL</b> 1978 editorial note 191: `hellashin'. Originally in the expression `hellashin hot' to match `perishing cold'. The more aceptable variations were `hangashin' and `heckashin'. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,191</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> in constant use by our youngsters ... hangava, hangashun <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Equally meaningless ... are ... the intensives hanguva, hangershun - modified forms of helluva <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Children are notable users of slang ... and in these good old days the most current included hang of a, hangashun, heck of a and heckashun, all intensives. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

### hangehange
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori privet (<i>Geniostoma rupestre</i>); a native shrub, similar to privet [Maori]; New Zealand privet. [[Check that cits. do not include those re angiangi.]]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> A'ngi; Name of a certain tree; a native oven; ... <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,134</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> [?] [?shrub; or ?Hinau 1/286 has angi angi] There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the <i>30 Warangai - 31 Pata -32 - Niho - 33 Tangio, - 34 Maihoi - 35 Nene [e's-acute] - 36 Taraiti - 37 Kohihihu 38 Waihupuku - 39 Kaikamoko - 40 Pukapuka - 41 Karanghu - 42 Utuhutu - 43 Angiani - 44 Akkas</i> of various kinds. 45 <i>Parekireki</i>, &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/400</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Hange-hange, <i>Col[enso]. Genistoma ligustrifolium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>) [NOTES: (List of Names)]

>  <i>Coprosma baueriana</i>, angeange. - Common on the coast. <i>(1873,TrNZi,6/49</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>POTTS</b> ... women are bringing water in calabashes (taha), or boys riding in horses laden with fodder, consisting chiefly of leaves and boughs of the hange-hange (<i>Geniostoma ligustrifolium</i>) ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,16</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>G[eniostoma] ligustrifolium</i>, A.Cunn. ... A perfectly glabrous much-branched shrub 4-12 ft. high; ... <i>Hangehange. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,444</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Privet. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,228,1961 ed.</i>)

The waxy-surfaced, apple-green leaves ... might commend this as a desirable garden shrub were it not that the small greenish flowers, ... have an odd smell that gives rise to the name `pigwood' in some districts. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,118</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Geniostoma ligustrifolium</i> A. Cunn. Common name: Maori<i> privet</i> Maori names: <i>Hangehange, papa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,63</i>)

### hanger
 
 <i>n.</i> [(dredging)]

>  <b>KiTTO</b> Before letting down the ladder you should examine the bottom tumbler and hangers to see they [sic] are all right. <i>ibid.</i> 25 Should one of the ladder hangers come off, the ladder will tip over to one side ... <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,16</i>)

### hanger-on
 
 <i>n.</i> a good-for-nothing; supernumerary or toady; camp-follower; ?bludger. [ca1947 (slang) `a bludger'; one who hangs around with others for what he can get out of them.]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The people of Auckland, consisting of a few mere land-sharks or hangers-on. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> ... some of the hangers-on of the diggings, - the thieves, the good-for-nothing portion of the community - were not at all unlikely to make a raid on [the sheep]. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,194</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(mining)]

>  The driver takes the trucks through the mine races, and deposits them in lay-bys, places similar to ... railway sidings. Here the trucks are received by the hangers-on, boys who attach the trucks to an endless rope. <i>(1901,NZ illustrated Magazine,iV/614</i>)

### hangi
 
 <i>n.</i> Native oven, consisting of a circular hole in the ground, in which the food was cooked by heated stones; the contents of a hangi; a) an earth oven, or its contents; b) a communal meal cooked in an earth oven:1979 HNZD; =copper Maori, =umu.

>  Proves the bludgers who run this country are two kumaras short of a hangi. <i>(Nov 26, 2002,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  For those whose rural knowledge is confined to Footrot Flats or grumbling about TV ads for drench or dog biscuits, silage is the end product of a self-drive grass hangi, made by chopping up paddocks of grass and squashing it into a giant pit. <i>(Jan 14, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  hangi: cooking oven. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  Hangis and Hash-houses [heading] `Maori hangis are classed as `eating-houses' in the new eating-house regulations ...' [reported to the Rotorua Borough Council by the borough inspector] <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,13</i>)

>  ~ A Maori oven, a hole in the ground in which food is cooked by heated stones. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),192,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> Dad killed the biggest pig for the hangi. [fn.] hangi - earth oven. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,45</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> hangi-Food cooked in a Maori oven. Such an oven, or umu, consists of a pit dug in the ground, part filled with large rounded river stones. <i>(1963,in the Sticks,181</i>)

>  <b>KEANE</b> hangi: Maori oven consisting of a hole in the ground in which stones are heated by a fire. Branches and leaves (or, nowadays, sacks) are placed on top, sprinkled with water, and then the earth is piled on and stamped flat. The food is then steamed for several hours. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,190</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> <i>(1963,Coal Flat,372</i>)

>  hangi, haangi n. Maori earth oven. See also copper Maori [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> [spg haangi] KANi. ... Hey, Pera! How deep the haangi? <i>(1969,Awatea,16,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The farmers lost one or two sheep in a hangi <i>(1979,Collected Poems,529,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> haangi: an earth oven in which the food, having been covered with wet sacks and earth, is cooked by the pressure of steam from water poured over heated stones at the bottom of the pit. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> [Glossary of Maori words] hangi earth oven, or, by common usage, a feast <i>(1979,Collected Poems,631</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hangi: A MAORi BARBECUE, except that the food isn't barbequed. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,50</i>)

>  (Goldie Brown Collection) hangi <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

>  hangi earth oven; by extension, a feast <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,ADCOCK ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> hangi earth oven, or, by common usage, a feast <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> There are countless ways of preparing food Maori style, but basically there were only three ways of cooking it. The first, which involves steaming in a subterranean oven called an umu or hangi, is known as tao cooking. it was the preferred method of cooking for groups of people, from family size up, in a village setting. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1</i>)

>  hangi n. N.Z. 1. an open-air cooking pit. 2. the food cooked in it. 3. the social gathering at the resultant meal. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,508</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 341; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 224]]

>  A haangi is being held at the Quad Centre on Thursday 31st July at 12.00 noon. All tickets will be pre-sold at $5.00 each. Proceeds go to the opening of the new Wharenui. <i>(Jul 25, 1986,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular</i>)

>  [also the pit] i heard about a hangi which was organised ... last weekend ... First one person climbing out of the hangi fell on the cinders, burnt his arm and had to be taken to hospital. <i>(Nov 14, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),4</i>)

>  Hangi food cooked in an earth oven <i>(1989,Chosen Place,104</i>)

>  i'd always wanted a portable hangi and, when i got hoha with the permits, i decided to build my own. <i>(Jul 17, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> it would also be an unhappy thing to cook titi in a hangi. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,13</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Twice, cousin Joe and Sean had done a hangi, out at Pukepoto, driving the steaming baskets into town. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,62</i>)

>  She says the name of her show is a reflection of her Jewish, Celtic and Maori ancestry. "One of my sisters called us 'a bagel short of a hangi with a bit of haggis on the side'". <i>(Mar 2, 2004,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  [Hdg] Unearthing the takeaway hangi... His hangi device can cook up to 600 hangi packs in less than two hours. This surely beats the "old way" of cooking a hangi, which can take half a day, he said. <i>(Jun 17, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  ...just like the Maoris where we grew up in the Bay of Plenty with their hangis and tangis, their poi songs and mock fierce ways. <i>(Apr 19, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> A'ngi; Name of a certain tree; a native oven; ... <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,134</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR & HARREX</b> The flesh [of the kiwi] was quite dark, and very tender when it was cooked in a hangi. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,109</i>)

>  The first episode closed with a musical number called "Throw Whitey on the Hangi and Cook Him Like a Pig." <i>(Oct 3, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  He then laid into "the elements within our leadership" who, he says, are prepared to sell Treaty rights for cash - "mixing the kai with the tapu", as he put it, "having their hands in the hangi". <i>(Jan 14, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  During the night a young chief dreamt he had killed all the people in one house and made the fire and cooked them in the shangi (hangi) or oven. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,257</i>) [NOTES: 1824 Aug 5 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 257]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The hangis (ovens) were kept constantly supplied. <i>(May 31, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,132,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The only method of cooking formerly in use [by the New Zealanders] was by the kapa [a's-acute] or angi maori, the native oven, already described. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/399</i>)

>  <b>WiLY & MAUNSELL</b> Twelve whole pigs, cooked in the one hangi, and borne on sticks, were laid in the middle of the company. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,71</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 30 in Wily and Maunsell Robert Maunsell (1938) 71]

>  i found also two Hangis or ovens which had shortly before been used for cooking food without the usual process of heating stones. <i>(May 8, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,305,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> A small native village is here [at Ohinemutu], with the usual appurtenances of a native steam kitchen at the hot springs; namely, hot plates, made of large slabs of stone, laid over boiling water ... steam hanghis, or native ovens, always in readiness ... A native swing completes the equipment of this fashionable watering place ... <i>(Oct 30, 1843,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Oct 30 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part ii (Church in the Colonies Vii) 32]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> The native oven, hangi or kohua, made in the well-known manner with heated stones, is situated either in the open air or in a house (te-kauta) constructed of logs at a small distance from each other. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/43</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> ... a mat of muka flax is then made. it is then filled with berries and is placed in a hangi (oven) underground on heated stones. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,99,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> A small pig may be from time to time purchased, which if cooked in a hangi, or native oven, ... produces a very savoury dish <i>(1847,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from Jrul in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 119]

>  <b>GRACE</b> We had not been long here before my boys had dressed the pig in the Maori fashion, and the old man had dug up some potatoes and prepared a hangi - an oven of hot stones. <i>(Mar 1851,Journal,10</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Mar T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 10]

>  <i>(1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/697,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> The ducks were soon cooked in ram hangi or native oven. <i>(1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Meade Ride Through Disturb Dists. (ed2.) in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 178.]

>  <b>MEADE</b> The ducks were soon cooked in ram hangi or native oven. <i>(1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Meade Ride Through Disturb Dists. (ed2.) in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 178.]

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> Good, nay delicious, as the kora is when boiled, or steamed in a hangi au naturel. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 478]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> The Pakeha was much too valuable an animal in those days to be killed and eaten; that game did not pay at all. Cannibal feasts did come off now and again on the sly, but the Pakeha was too dear a morsel, and moreover, was far too salt to be put into a Hangi (native oven) for epicurean Maories. <i>ibid.</i> Bk2/6/107 ... the steam was just arising from their hangis as these were being uncovered, and we were all soon served, each with a little freshly-plaited flax-leaf basket filled with most deliciously cooked kumeras, potatoes and peppies. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/2/61</i>)

>  <b>26/579</b> When the bodies placed in the hangis were cooked they were calabashed, and formally handed over to the Ngatitoa as payment for the children of Te Rauparaha who had been treacherously killed by the Muaupoko. <i>(1894,TrNZi</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> `Coppa Maori,' `Maori oven,' etc. - these are the slang names given by those who know no better. By the natives it is called an `Umu' and universally a `Haangi'. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,102</i>)

>  THE HANGi, OR KOPPA MAORi ... An hour is long enough for a moderate sized hangi ... An uncooked hangi is considered by the Maori as a disgrace, a ... sign of ... disaster ... it is not too much to hope that some day Hangi parties will become fashionable. <i>(1908,Souvenir of All Nations Fair,11</i>)

>  [spg] The best camp oven ... is the haangi, or earth oven, of the Maoris ... These fronds, called pikopiko, were excellent when steam-cooked ... <i>(Jun 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> [spg] Then in the days of the cannibal chief, Rauparaha, ... at the time he was eating his way from Kawhia ..., `the thousands of Hawkes Bay' took refuge ... to escape the inevitable haangi. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,17</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> [spg hoangi] Te Whero, talking across the cooking-hoangi to Te Rauparaha, was not very sure why he did allow it. <i>(1938,Promenade,121</i>)

>  h&amacr.ngi, Maori earth-oven, in which the food is steamed, not baked: sounded like ha(r)ngi - the `r' suppressed: syn. umu. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... hangi: earth oven; also known as copper Maori <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> in modern urban contexts [get cits: to hangi a pig].



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  HANGi length firewood, peach, plum, apple, unique flavour, $50 the lot. Ph 307 1244. <i>(Dec 13, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> it was an appetising display ... a mound of potatoes, haangi steamed, ... alternated with stacks of eels enclosed in wrappings of `raurekau' leaves (coprosma grandifolia), baked on a framework of rickers over a clear fire, ... delicate of flavour. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,253</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> [spg (in this edition) haangi] From afar they could see rising smoke at the back of the whare manuhiri, or guest house, where haangi fires were being kindled. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,10</i>) [NOTES: `Christmas Day']

>  Here [on the Ruatoki marae] women organize monthly `bring and buys' or hangi dinners at half-a-crown a time to collect parcels for Ruatoki boys overseas. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,52</i>) [NOTES: (from Korero, AEWS magazine)]

>  <b>KEENE</b> And the hangi lunch! <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,46</i>)

### hangi pit
 
 <i>n.</i>

### hangi stone
 
 <i>n.</i> a large stone, composed of rock which heats and holds heat quickly in an hangi or uinderground oven.

>  <b>BURTON</b> The ideal hangi stones are porous volcanic rocks, about the size of a fist, which are light and easily heated. Next best are the variety of metallic-coloured bluestone found in the Auckland area, or the dense riverbed boulders which are often found in deep mud. ... in some areas deposits of good hangi stones are rare, and in former times a set of stones was a welcome gift. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1</i>)

>  They don't have hangi stones and they mainly steam the food, which ends up tasting insipid. <i>(Jul 17, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

>  And the Maori view tends to be, why the bloody hell should we go through some rigmarole to continue to do what we have been doing on the beach since before 1840? One example that has been quoted is the gathering of hangi stones. <i>(Apr 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  in places the wind sweeps away sand dunes to expose the broken shells and hangi stones of old middens. <i>(Mar 2003,North & South,93</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> one who prepares hangi

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>adv.</i>

### Hanging Bailey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Civilization is saved, for example, when instead of trying to torture a classmate a child says, `Guess what happened to Bailey?' `What?' `This,' and the child pulls out the fold in a piece of paper on which Bailey has been drawn with his neck in a noose, When the fold is pulled out his neck suddenly appears as stretched several inches in length - a nice little game known as Hanging Bailey. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,259</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### hanging wall
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Hangingwall: The uppermost or overhanging wall of the reef opposite the footwall within which the reef was confined. As the reef was usually [324] inclined at an angle, when it was taken out one wall was overhanging. in the upper workings the hangingwall tended to be unstable <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),323</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a hard case; dag, a character [HNZD2]. [ca1946 very popular especially for a hard drinker, a devil-may-care fellow. Always used good-humouredly]

### hangman's blood
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TUCKER</b> in our ranks was a very hard-headed gentleman by the name of Jensen who, on arriving at the pub, breasted up to the bar and ordered a `hangman's blood'. The barman confessed total ignorance of just what this drink might comprise. He was told to produce a beer handle, which he did, and was then ordered to go along [17] the entire length of the bar, putting in one nip of whatever spirits were on display. Now at that time spirits were very hard to come by, but Paul Rabone, the proprietor of the Mangatara Hotel, seemed to have an inexhaustible supply. Doing as he was bid the barman proceeded from one end to the other, putting in such things as scotch, gin, bacardi, rum, creme de menthe, and so on. He finally returned to the prospective quaffer of this incredible beverage with the beer mug about quarter-full of a purplish looking liquid. `Now,' said Jensen, `Fill it up with beer.' <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,16</i>)

### hani
 
 <i>n.</i> a taiaha

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Halbert Hennee <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/331</i>) [NOTES: (English-NZ Vocabulary)]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> H|ane, s. A war instrument so called; Also a fish so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,152</i>)

>  <b>CRAiK</b> These [spears], or rather the shorter sort, are also sometimes called by English writers Patoos, or Patoo-patoos. Lastly, they often carry an instrument somewhat like a sergeant's halberd ... This they call a Hennee. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,266</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Three of the chiefs stood up erect in the canoe, brandishing a hani [a-acute] spear, or the tomahawk. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/158</i>)

>  i bought a quantity of fishing hooks made of the Paua shell a mat and a Hauni. [fn] Hani or taiaha ... <i>(Oct 29, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,250,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the six hearts of the murdered people were thrust on the heads of spears, (hani's), placed upright in the centre of the canoe. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/29</i>)

>  in afn. went to White's and planed a bit of manuka, to make a hani as a paper-knife for Alice. <i>(Jul 13, 1861,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/715,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  They carved handsome staves (Hani and Taiaha), out of the hard variegated wood of the Ake ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/16</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> They used a variety of manual weapons, such as the mere, the hani, the hoeroa, the patu, the taiaha, the toki, etc. ... <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,249</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... whilst here and there a hani (or taiaha, a chief's staff of rank, &c.) was seen erected, adorned with the long flowing white hair of the tails of the New Zealand dog and crimson cloth and red feathers. <i>(1890,Treaty of Waitangi,10,Brombie ed.</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The quarter-staff or sword (taiaha or hani) was made of heavy hard wood. it also served the purpose of a spear ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,312</i>)

### Hanmer
 
 <i>n.</i> the Queen Mary drug and drink rehabilitation Hospital at Hanmer Springs

>  A lot of things have been wonderful about Queen Mary Hospital - or just "Hanmer", as it's invariably called - from its early days as a place of healing for traumatised World War i soldiers through to its iconic role as an alcohol and drug addiction treatment centre. <i>(Nov 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### Hansard
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ often referred to as NZPD, the official published reports of parliamentary debates.

>  He just kept on subscribing to pink-covered Hansards, milking too-few cows, avoiding screwing roosters' necks, going to the footie and giving her plenty of space to do her own thing. <i>(Aug 20, 2003,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  Hansard takes its name from the Hansard family, which ran one of the many private-sector companies producing reports on House of Commons debates in the early [60] 19th century. At the time, no official record was kept of what was said in debates, only of decisions made. Where the Hansards made their name was by producing their reports more quickly than their competitors did. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,N o. 63/58</i>)

### hao
 
 <i>n.</i> silver-eel

>  CONGERMURAENA HABENATA (Richardson). <i>Silver-eel</i>. Called `hao' by the Arahura Maoris. Rare in Auckland and Wellington markets, and not greatly utilized as a food fish. Distribution: New Zealand coasts (not recorded from Bay of islands). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/119</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

### happy as Larry
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> The first thing to do [on the land to be cleared] was to prepare a camp - a very easy matter in most parts of New Zealand. [A description of building sod walls, and sapling struts, and thatched roof, and chimney piece.] [133] We would be happy as Larry if it were not for the rats. The first night in camp, as soon as we blew out the slush-lamp, these started to burrow in the sod walls, ... <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,133,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> We did not vex ourselves ... with wailing or complaint. ... on the contrary we were as `happy as Larry' <i>(1910,Reminiscences,21</i>) [NOTES: ca1935 Havelock -]

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> is he still in the asylum?' `Oh, he's as happy as Larry,' says Uncle Ted. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,91,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WHiTWORTH</b> i was a regular tomboy, happy as larry with my brothers <i>(1950,Otago interval,29</i>)

>  <b>ARCH ACKER</b> The once common `Air-piece Larry' was dropped from polite usage after Larry's death ... in 1957. <i>(1966,New Zild,9</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ...although too big and heavy to be a good walker he was happy as Larry on that job. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,110</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> There is simile: `as mad as a snake', `as bald as a bandicoot' (an embroidery on the English `bald as a coot'), `as happy as larry', `rough as bags' (which i know better in the variant `rough as sacks'), `as wet as a shag'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,115</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> JAMESON. ... Everyone happy? WHA. Oh, sure Sarge. Happy as Larry. <i>(1969,Awatea,45,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RAE</b> `is she all right?' ... `Happy as Larry,' said Ryan, ruefully. `Smashed out of her mind on Bell's best, mind you, but glad to be that way.' <i>(1972,Shooting Gallery, The,272</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> And i'm happy as larry here, chopping up wood in the shed on a wet day. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,12</i>)

>  ... his [Larry's] origins are shrouded in mystery. All that we can be certain of is that he first flourished in Australia. `Happy as Larry` made an early appearance in print in the pages of Joseph Furphy. He seems to have his literary debut in New Zealand in the 1940's in the pages of Frank Sargeson. <i>(Aug 20, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),68</i>) [NOTES: (i.A. Gordon)]

>  <b>HUNT</b> The Watkins were happy as larry way up there. <i>ibid.</i> i left [sc. school] at thirteen, went to the land, and was as happy as larry. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,87</i>)

### hapu
 
 <i>n.</i> sub-tribe, clan, "family" [[Ma. <b>hapu</b> sub tribe, clan] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 45.]

>  Maori society was, in the 18th and 19th centuries, a mosaic, or, better, a kaleidoscope of hundreds of hapu, politically autonomous descent groups, which were forming, disappearing, dividing and forging numerous alliances with each other as population pressures increased... <i>ibid.</i> 8 indeed, Maori society was, and to a large extent still is, primarily a hapu-divided society. <i>(Mar 1999,NZ Books,7</i>)

>  These included.. the wives of all the principal men of the hapu. <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 30</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> After food today the great Korero commenced ... soon the space in front of Hikurangi was crowded with an expectant audience seated in due order according to their respective hapus. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,21</i>)

>  Unfortunately the next nearest hapu viewed us with no friendly eyes. <i>(Aug 28, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  The opposing hapu.. had made sure of victory by carefully makutu-ing the playing-ground beforehand. <i>(Jul 26, 1913,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'i'm hapu too,' Hoheria told Sean one day. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,223</i>)

>  Even the poorest hapus among the Natives.. can.. raise enough for the rude subsistence their wants require. <i>(Mar 5, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  THE Wairak-based Wharepaia hapu wants to establish a centre in Wairaka to administer its affairs for hapu developmement. <i>(Jul 22, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a division (of a board) [ORIGIN: Maori]

<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> pregnant [ORIGIN: Maori]

### hapua
 
 <i>n.</i> a shallow lake

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> From the spot where Huruhuru left us our path lay along a pebble ridge, with the sea on one side, and the lake Waihao on the other. This `hapua,' as all similar pieces of water are called, has no outlet to the sea except at times of floods, when one is made by the bank of pebbles breaking down at its weakest point: at other times its water oozes through the pebbles and sand. After leaving Waihao, which was nearly three miles long, we passed several small `hapua' similar to it. <i>ibid.</i> 307 HAPUA, a shallow lake, the termination of a river, separated from the sea by a bank of sand or shingles. Syn. hopua. <i>(Jan 16, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,219</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 16 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 219]

>  ... the material of which it was constructed being only cabbage trees, korari (flax stalks), and raupo; both of the latter grew plentifully round the Lagoon, or hapua, as the Maoris called it, which then contained nice clear water ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,9,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 9]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Waitarakao, a large `hapua' or lagoon or sheet of water. <i>ibid/.</i> 64 Koareare is the edible part of the root of the raupo, and was got in the hapua (lagoons) and re (swamps). The name runs off the tongue glibly when given its southern pronunciation, `ko-a-ray-a-ray'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,45</i>)

### hapuku
 
 <i>n.</i> an important commercial fish =Cod 3b3 2b and grouper 2c. 1844 W Wakefield 1944 W & T Mod Jun Dice ed7 1960 Doogue & Moreland NZ Sea Angler guide 209 1946 JPS 1491966 Turner.] [Maori hapuku[a-micron].] Polyprion oxygeneios; 1. hapuku [`ha:puku] becomes English [ha:`puk],and a mishearing of Maori [p] phoneme gives the commonest pronunciation [ha:`buk] 2. whapuku [` a:puku] [`wa: puku] gives similarly [hwa:`pukd] [wa:`buk] etc. 3. Forms with no initial aspirate may be due to a) de-aspiration in vulgar English b) Maori dialectal de-aspiration (cf. Whangaroa - (North island) Akaroa (South island) initial. in more Maori districts the stress is often on the initial syllable, as in Maori, but in NZ colloquial it is more usually on the second syllable; the Eng. plural can have an -s inflexion; Obsolete (and sometimes mistaken) names include: (New Zealand) cod, cod-fish, rock cod; formerly called New Zealand cod, =groper. [These illustrate anglicisations and corruptions of main Maori dialectal pronunciations: spelling with final a as frequent in English vernacular as that with final u. Williams h&amacr.puku, wh&amacr.puku, kapua. Hapuku is the chief use in the North Island and in Marlborough; in Wellington and the South Island (esp Otago) groper is the chief use. 1895. `Oxford English Dictionary' (s.v. Cod): `In New Zealand, a serranoid fish <i>polyprion pronathus</i>, called by the Maoris hapuku.']

>  <b>REES</b> The livers of these and other fishes are rejected, and a strong prejudice exists against that of the hapuka, a cod, which is considered poisonous, but it has been partaken of by Europeans without inconvenience. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (irish Univ. series),1779/28</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> He could keep an eye on her while he was fishing at the hapuku grounds near Danger Rock. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,124</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> 1846 Alex Marjoribanks NZ 82 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 267 <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,267</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The Hapuka is an excellent fish of the cod kind, but more gelatinous and of richer flavour. <i>ibid.</i> 303 The <i>habouka</i> is taken [in Otago] ...; flat fish and oysters in all the bays. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,160</i>)

>  <b>McKiLLOP</b> ... the harbouker, a large fish, even superior to the cod (more resembling salmon), ... <i>(1849,Reminiscences,250</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> Among them the Hapuka and Kawai deserve particular mention: the former varies from ten to seventy pounds in weight ... Many people prefer them to the cod, which they somewhat resemble; ... <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,76</i>)

>  (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5) English Name Native Name Rock Cod Whapuka Snapper Tamuri ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,(Vol. iX) 125</i>)

>  the habouka, or abuka ... Whapuka - Rock cod <i>(Oct 15, 1849,Anglo-Maori Warder,?15</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Fish are plentiful ... Eeels [sic], sometimes Habouka, a large fish (which i tasted at Capt. Simeon's) something between Cod and Grey Mullet, but not so good as either. <i>(1853,Journal,1/158,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACKiE</b> On the [Nelson] beach were lying a quantity of large fish - the hapuku - which had been caught by the natives. <i>(Apr 28, 1853,Traveller under Concern,103,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Hapuku</i> or <i>hapuku</i> commonly called the cod, but a much richer fish in flavor: externally, it more resembles the salmon, and is known in New Holland as the Dew or Jew fish; it attains a large size, and is considered the best sea fish of New Zealand. <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,411</i>)

>  .. .the Karoa Reef, which is the best fishing ground for hapuku, ... <i>(1856,TrNZi,17/421</i>) [NOTES: (Goldsmith)]

>  <b>THOSE CHAPMAN</b> Hapukus (N.Z. Cod). <i>(1856,Letters & Journals,3-621</i>)

>  <b>COOPER</b> hapuka, pronounced Habooka or wabooka. <i>(1857,NZ Settlers' Guide,30</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Hapuka in every tide;/ And as for fruit, the place [Taranaki] is full/ Of that delicious bull-a-bull. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/217</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Next to the shark, ... the Hapuku is the largest New Zealand salt-water fish. <i>ibid.</i> /62 ... and sea fishing even the hauling out of a hundred-pound hapuka, was poor sport ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/30</i>)

>  ... Wahpuka or Hahpuka ... <i>(1862,From The Black Rocks,21,1950 ed.</i>)

>  `My friend' .../ You like hapuku drying;/ Kapai the smelt, the duck, the eel,/ And shark when putrifying./ <i>(Jul 1860,Canterbury Rhymes,85</i>) [NOTES: (The Maori's Choice)]

>  The Hapuka, Baracouta, Ling, Snapper, Kawai, and Moki, ... and a few varieties of Rock fish, are the sea fish chiefly obtained ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/325</i>) [NOTES: (Travers)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> The first on the list of edible fishes is the Hapuku or Whapuku of the Maoris (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>), or Habuka, as the name is generally pronounced by Europeans, who in the South apply the name Groper to the same fish. it is also occasionally called the Cod fish, which is altogether erroneous ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,102</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 1. OLiGORUS GiGAS. Owen. Hapuka. <i>(1872,Fishes,1</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON & HECTOR</b> The Hapuku or Whapuku of the Maoris (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>), or Habuka, as the name is generally pronounced by Europeans, who in the South apply the name groper to the same fish. it is also occasionally called the Cod Fish, which is altogether erroneous. <i>(1872,Fishes,102</i>)

>  <b>BAiNES</b> Truly ... presently in the bottom of the boat lay Hapuku, Tamuri, Tarakiki [sic], Rock-cod, Yellow tail, young shark, crayfish ... <i>ibid.</i> 164 Although i always made my own `hapuku' hooks, ... yet i feel that a lengthy written description would only give a feeble idea of the appearance of a Maori `matiku'. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,163</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Hapuka, Groper .. [Scientific Name] <i>Oligorus gigas</i> <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> There were also a few bones of Oligorus gigas, hapuku. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,417</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Hapuku (also spelled hapuka) - the groper. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 61]

>  <b>HAY</b> in the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. in the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>ibid..</i> 2/ 231 We prefer the substantial schnapper, the goodly whapuka or kanae, or the luscious poraki. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  The fishing off the island is very good, there being abundance of hapuku, kokiri, kohikohi, maumau [sic], schnapper, kahawai, tarakihi, in addition to plenty of shell-fish such as koura or crayfish, crabs, paua, etc. The mako shark, so well known for its beautiful teeth, which are highly prized by the Maoris as ornaments, is found off this island and nowhere else in the world i believe, ... <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Goldsmith)]

>  The fish for eating are hapuka,<i>Oligorus gigas</i>; rock cods, <i>Percis colias </i>; tarakihi, <i> Chilodactylus macropterus </i>; moki, <i> Latris ciliaris </i>, etc.,etc. <i>(1885,TrNZi,17/198</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> HAPUKU (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>). The first on the list of marketable fishes is the hapuku, or whapuku of the Maoris (<i> Hectoria gigas </i> of Count Castelnau), or habuka, as the name is generally pronounced by Europeans, who in the south, apply the name groper to the same fish. it is also occasionally called the cod-fish, which is altogether erroneous, as it is more properly the representative of the sea-perch of European seas. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,40</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  2. <i> Mokohinou islands </i>. - Hapuku (or groper), hiwihiwi, barracouta, maomao, spotty, parrot-fish, leather jacket, korokoro-pounamou. <i>ibid.</i> 24. <i> Puysegur Point </i>- Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i> Hapuku </i>... Maori name for a fish, <i>Oligorus gigas</i>, Gunth., called later <i> Polyprion prognathus </i> (see quotation, 1895), pronounced hapuka, frequently corrupted into habuka, the Groper (q.v.). it is variously called a <i> cod,</i> a <i> Perch </i>, and a <i> Sea-Perch </i>. See quotations. <i>(1898,Austral English,192</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Lowing herds on every side,/ Hapuka in every tide.' <i>(1845,New Zealand (various authors/titles),217</i>) [NOTES: 1845 (about). `New Plymouth's National Song,' Hursthouse's `New Zealand,' p. 217: -->`]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `Hapuku, or whapuku, commonly called the cod, but a much richer fish in flavour: externally it more resembles the salmon and is known in New Holland as the dew or jew-fish. it attains a large size and is considered the best fish of New Zealand.' <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,411</i>)

>  <b>ANON</b> `A kind of codfish called by the natives whapuku or hahpuka.' <i>(May 17, 1862,All the Year Round,No. 160:</i>) [NOTES: 1862. Anon., `From the Black Rocks on Friday,' `All the Year Round,' May 17, 1862,]

>  <b>COLENSO</b> `The second (Oligorus gigas) is found in the sea, on the coast of New Zealand, and called by the Maoris and colonists `Hapuku' ... >  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  `How big's the hapuka? As large as a schnapper?' ... `. . .Why the ' arpooker comes in to eat the schnapper. He's the biggest deep water fish we have ... .' <i>(Mar 26, 1910,NZ Truth,248/8/1</i>)

>  At the Portobello Marine Fish - hatchery the swimming form has been taken from the stomachs of the following fishes: Smooth-hound (<i>Mustelus antarcticus </i>), Spined dogfish (<i> Squalus fernandinus </i>), Warehou (<i> Seriolella brama </i>), Red cod (<i> Physiculus bacchus </i>), Hapuka (<i> Polyprion prognathus </i>), ... <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/111</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson and Thomson)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie never struck trouble but once, when he asked [on the transtasman boat] for some <i> hapuka</i> at breakfast. A very young English steward commanded: `You cut out that language in front of the women.' `i asked for <i> hapuka. Hapuka</i>. Just plain <i> hapuka</i>.' `There you are,' yelled the steward. `He said it again. Said it three times.' ... `For Christ's sake,' said the chief steward wearily: `Get him some fried fish.' `Fish? is that all it means? i thought , in front of the women . . .' `in front of the women or behind their backs, <i> hapuka's</i> still fish and fish is still <i> hapuka</i> fathead,' explained Starkie, ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,234</i>)

>  <i>h&amacr.puku</i>, a sea-fish (Oligorus gigas), groper, vulg. harbuka. <i>ibid.</i> 55/150 <i>h&amacr.puku</i> is not the cod, which is p&amacr.kirikiri (Percis colias): groper is the common name, or hapuka corrupted to harbuka. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>hapuku</i>: edible fish (groper). <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Groper is known more generally in the North island by the name `Hapuka', and by Europeans this name is often spelt `Whapuka' or `Habuka'. <i>ibid.</i> 169 [whapuku, -->whapuka] Groper Polyprion oxygeneios Hapuku; Hapuka; Habuka; Whapuka <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,70</i>)

>  <i>hapuku (ha|-pu-ka)</i>. The Maori name for a large edible fish, also called `groper'. Often mispronounced <i>ha-pu-ka. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),193,8th ed.</i>)

>  <i>whapuku n</i> -s [Maori] : a large edible marine fish (<i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i>) of New Zealand waters that is closely related to or perhaps identical with the stonebass <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,2599</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by ... the adoption of Maori words and phrases. ... Examples of the native words now in general use are the names of birds, fish, plants, and trees: <i>kea</i>, tui, hapuku, tarakihi, koromiko, raupo, <i>and </i>kauri. <i>ibid.</i> 3/705-12 Hapuku (groper).. .. hapuku, kapua, whapuku .. .. <i>Polyprion oxygeneios </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GROPER or HAPUKU ... <i>Other names: Polyprion oxygeneios</i>; whapuku (Maori). Groper has a wide usage in the South island, while hapuku is largely used in the North island. The word `hapuka' is a corruption of hapuku. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,224</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAPUKU or GROPER [<i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i>] ... Hapuku is a Maori name widely used by North island fishermen, while groper has a predominantly South island usage. in some localities both names are used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

>  <i>hapuku ... n. </i>N.Z. fish, the groper [Maori] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 342; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 225]]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>GROPER</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (ii) <i>Polyprion moeone</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) New Zealand Groper; Hapuku (ii) Bass. (c) Other common names: (i) Hapuka, Stone-eye, Blue-nose, Bonita (ii) Deepsea Groper. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Mainly easten coasts, Foveaux, and Chathams] ... [27 Groper and Bass] should not be confused with the Australian and tropical groupers which they resemble superficially. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,26</i>)

>  ~ n. New Zealand fish, the groper (q.r.) [Maori] <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  [species name] hapuku .. [scientific name] <i>Polyprion oxygeneios </i> .. [other name(s)] groper, puka <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Hapuku Polyprion oxygeneios</i> ... Widely known throughout New Zealand as either hapuku (often pronounced hapuka) or groper, the former generally in the North island, the latter in the South. The species also occurs around southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,82</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>BLUE-NOSE WAREHOU Hyperoglyphe antarctica</i> (Blue-nose grouper, griffins silverfish, bonita, deepsea trevalla) ... [302] They are caught regularly on hapuku long lines along with hapuku and bass and are sold as hapuku. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,301</i>)

>  On Monday the fish were biting freely, and the 20 boats on the bank made great hauls of hapuka. <i>(Nov 28, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  When you look at a fish, particularly a hapuka, cut a slit above the tail, down to the narrowest part. and if it is in good eating condition there will be a layer of fat next to the skin; if not, it will be lean and dry. <i>(May 8, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  <b>TOWNSEND</b> There are very fine fish - sole - haddock - white herring fresh - Harbouka - a large tender fish - conger eel 10 feet long. <i>(Nov 16, 1842,Letter,9</i>) [NOTES: in <i>Absolutely Positively Wellington</i> 0/9/2004, p. 9]

>  Two hapukas at a time, 600 pounds each. <i>(Apr 9, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> ...later in the week he'd go with Arch to deep water to fish for hapuku for the Friday night fundraising dinner. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,105</i>)

>  For the main course there may be crayfish,.. pan-fried hapuku or fillet steak. <i>(Aug 1991,Cuisine,87</i>)

>  How big's the hapuka?"... "The 'arpooker comes in to eat the schnapper." <i>(Mar 26, 1910,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  Curiously southern hapuku, sorry, groper reverse these movements and in winter head out into deep water to spawn. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B10</i>)

>  The reason for this is that in winter, northern hapuku move into shallower water (40-50m in places) from their summer haunts of deepwater rocks and reefs off the edge of the continental shelf. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B10</i>)

>  Typical hapuku tackle is a ledger rig with two large circle hooks on droppers off the main trace. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B10</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Wap|uku, s. The cod-fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,225</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Some deep banks lie off the east coast, on which the <i>kanai</i>, or mullet, <i>wapuka </i>, or cod-fish, and the kahawai [a1,a2-acute], or colourless salmon, abound. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> [<i>Abuka </i>, (Chatham islands) -->apuko] Amongst those esteemed the most, the <i>Abuka </i> ranks first. it is generally of great size, but the flesh is of much delicacy of flavour. A considerable quantity of it is cured at the whaling stations for winter provision, and is very fine. i have seen this fish being carried along the beach of Wellington, on a pole, borne by two natives, who found its weight fully equal to their united strength. [124] The same fish that are found on the coast of New Zealand may be caught in plenty in Port Wakefield [Chatham islands] ... The great apuko, the snapper, ... <i>ibid.</i> 124 The great apuko, the snapper, and the crayfish, are very numerous ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),48</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Polack mentions `cod-fish', bearing the native name of `Wapuka,' but we do not know the fish he alludes to. The `polach' he speaks of are, perhaps, the young of the <i>Percis colias </i>, the adult of which are known to the settlers as the `cole-fish'. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/222</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/222]

>  <b>W.WAKEFiELD</b> The habouka is taken in great quantities ... <i>(Aug 31, 1844,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 31 W.Wakefield letter to Secretary of NZ Co. in Hocken Contributions 267 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Capt. Smith The fish, which at Port Nicholson is known by the name of habooka, but which is called groper to the southward.- <i>(1843,NZ Journal,98-263</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> in the offing [of Moeraki] is the reef Taki-a-maru, a celebrated fishing ground for the `hapuku' or rock cod, as it is called by the whalers - the finest fish of these seas. <i>(Nov 4, 1843,Southern Districts of NZ,131</i>)

>  <b>J.B.WiLLiAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species i will name a part, viz. ... Wapuku [Hapuku], species of codfish; ... [<i>Glossary </i>, 118 hapuku, groper (<i>Polyprion Oxygeneois </i>)]. <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> in a moment the little population [of Stewarts island] was alive - children dragging up cod-fish [30] (<i>hapuka </i>) nearly as large as themselves; ... all sharing the joy. spg horbockers [Jan 16 previous: groper] <i>(Feb 7, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Feb 7 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part iii (Church in the Colonies Viii) 29-30]

>  Simson fishing: caught 18 horbockers - gave me one. ..[Jan 25] Men out fishing: ... Horpocker. <i>(Jan 22, 1844,Piraki Log,147,(ed. Anson 1911) ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Two fine fresh <i>Wapuku </i> (a species of <i>Gadus </i> . . .) each weighing more than 20 lbs. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern island of NZ,7</i>)

>  : got 11 gropers Jan 22.:caught 18 horbockers <i>(Jan 1844,Piraki Log,16</i>)

>  <b>BARNiCOAT</b> fine habuka <i>(1845,Journal,173</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### hapuku-fishing
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  in pre-European times there were several famous hapuku-fishing rocks outside the Heads. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/268</i>)

### hara
 
 <i>n.</i> sin, offence [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> After a dispute with one dear Maori friend/ i walk all night ... / `Pain in my feet; pain of my hara?' <i>(1979,Collected Poems,567</i>) [NOTES: (1972) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 567]

### harakeke
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ flax <i>Phormium tenax</i> [also known as harakeke, whararahi or swamp flax] (See [also swamp flax](spa-sys.md#swamp-flax))

>  There are two species: Phormium tenax, also known as harakeke, whararahi or swamp flax; and Phormium cookianum, also known as wharariki or mountain flax. ... Harakeke can be differentiated from wharariki by its larger size and seed pods. <i>(Apr 22, 2006,Dominion Post,indulgence (supplement) /20</i>)

>  Traditional kakahu are made by taking the fibre out of the harakeke [flax leaf] and washing, pounding and drying it in a process that takes months. The weaving in of the feathers then begins. <i>(Mar 10, 2000,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,14</i>)

>  <b>CROOKES</b> But, alas, from her came no godlike son, but only the Hara-keke, the flax plants, the stalwart dwellers of the swamp lands and the stiff sentinels of the river banks. in the summer Hara-keke sends up a rigid stalk bearing on each side of it dull red flowers, flowers which are even yet `sweet with the honey of a god's vain love.' <i>(1926,Plant Life in Maoriland,99</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The two most characteristic plants of New Zealand are the Kauri and the Harakeke or the New Zealand Pine ... and the New Zealand flax plant ... <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),140</i>)

>  How to do four-plait round, how to make harakeke stick boats, how to impale old net corks on the dorsal spikes of the kokiri, the leatherjacket, for races in the bay. <i>(Jan 10, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Ara Harakeke - Porirua's City's pathway alongside Taupo Swamp - has won this year's Best Cycle Facility Project award... Ara Harakeke (the flax pathway) appears to be contributing to the city's economy and health as a survey of bike shops in Porirua has shown that cycle sales have significantly increased over the last few years. <i>(Dec 18, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),10</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Phormium tenax is a flag-like plant, the sword-shaped drooping leaves of which the natives call Harakeke;the flower-stalk, bearing pink blossoms and resembling agaves, is called Korari. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),150</i>)

>  Outside the home of every self-respecting irish peasant in days gone by would be a small patch of flax, grown to be spun and woven into linen for household use. We're not talking about harakeke (New Zealand flax), of course, but the totally unrelated European flax (Linum usitatissimum)... When Europeans arrived in New ZXealand, they called harakeke "flax" because its fibres are similar to those of Linum. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  .. [p]lanted the harakeke plants and helped with the preparations for the opening. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,p 55</i>)

>  He just looked out the window and saw a stationary flax leaf... An unmoving blade of harakeke seemed like a good omen. <i>(Feb 13, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),76</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> hard drugs [(drug users')]

[sic] opium tincture, and so on ... <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,14</i>)

### hard as the hobs of hell
 
 <i>phr.</i>
