### hard ask
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As they are fond of saying in league commentaries, it could be a "hard ask". <i>(Oct 22, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

### hard bar
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  The country rock separating two veins approximately parallel with each other has, in certain instances, been found to grade laterally from the vein-wall, where it assumes a highly propylitic facies, to a median rib of dark and only slightly altered rock. These ribs or dyke-like masses are the `hard bars' of the miners. in some cases, however, these `hard bars' are probably due to dykes. <i>ibid</i> 111 The `hard bars' of mining terminology are in most cases merely remnants of less-altered rock, but occasionally as in the case of the `bar' mentioned in the Golden Pah mine, they owe their existence to intrusives. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,4(NS):101</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  Further, the `hard' bars most probably interrupted the shoot, more of less horizontally. <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,42(NS):39</i>) [NOTES: (Gage, Reefton)]

>  We have now passed through the hard bar, and re now in good reefing country. <i>(Nov 21, 1912,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  The manager telegraphed to-day:- "Low level tunnel. Cut a break. Appearance of being through the hard bar." <i>(Jun 25, 1894,Auckland  Star,3</i>)

>  A hard bar then came in and pinched the reef up, but at the date of his report he had got through it and into soft ground, and the reef seemed making again. <i>(Aug 18, 1875,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### hard beech
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus truncata</i>, tawhai-raunui.

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>), Kahikatea or white pine ... red and hard beech (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i> and <i>Nothofagus truncata</i>), silver beech (<i>Nothofagus Menziesii</i>), black beech (<i>Nothofagus Solandri</i>) ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>N. fusca (The Red Beech</i>)... North island: mountainous districts; South island: abundant. The <i>Red Birch</i>. A somewhat similar species is <i>N. truncata</i>, the Clinker Beech, often called the Hard-Beech. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,139</i>)

>  <i>(Aug 1957,NZ Timber Journal,59/1</i>)

>  Beech ... Hard .. tawhairaunui <i>Nothofagus truncata</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/710</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>NOTHOFAGUS TRUNCATA</i>. Hard beech, the only <i>Nothofagus </i>native to north Auckland, is absent from the east and south of the South island. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,80</i>)

>  <b>DENNiS</b> ... red beech (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i>) and hard beech (<i>N. truncata</i>) are regular components of the forests on the crest of the scarp above. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,157</i>)

>  Above 900 m hard beech is replaced by red beech which begins to dominate the canopy up to the summits of the ikawhenua Range. Also at these altitudes are Hall's totara and tawari. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,60</i>)

>  <b>A.L. POOLE</b> `Hard' probably because timber is hard and brittle, and of all the beeches is probably the most difficult to cut: probably a bushman's name <i>(Apr 25, 1987,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Five species of <i>Nothofagus</i> were formerly recognised in New Zealand, but more recently two of these have been combined as varieties of one species. ... <i>Nothofagus truncata</i>: hard beech (the wood has a high silica content making it hard). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,121</i>)

>  <i>Nothofagus truncata</i> (Hard Beech). <i>(1933,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,14/233</i>)

### hard case
 
 <i>n.</i> a witty person (NZ-US Dictionary); a good-natured reprobate or hoon; Likeable, amusing, conventional, often adventurous, but tough-minded morally tough person; a character; a good sport; a dag; also hard doer, hard lot, hard nail, hard shot, hard thing. [Mary Caldwell of Australia suggests that in NZ the meaning is that one's hard to understand; not the generally accepted NZ meaning but perhaps a regional or special situation meaning, e.g. psychiatric nurses might use this meaning 1984 16 F E30 Pakuranga Coll. 1 HARD CASE -->1984 16 F E38 Pakuranga Coll. 34 HARD-CASE]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> One of the most remarkable attributes of the Hard-case Bastard is his memory for jokes, and when he's not actually engaged in some other kind of hard-casery he keeps up an almost continuous running patter of jokes from his seemingly inexhaustible fund of them. <i>(1971,Bastards i Have Met,59</i>)

>  <b>WRiGHT</b> i thought of old tramps in the summer to stations far out at the back,/ When i met with the hardest of cases that live on the wallaby track -/Men like him, full of tricks and devices, .... <i>(1897,Station Ballads,24</i>) [NOTES: `The Hard Case']

>  <b>McKEE</b> The Hard Case [title] He was as big and as rough as they make 'em .... <i>(1900,Wisps of Tussock,50</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> The termed `hard case', purly colonial is one, is one of the most widely inclusive and general serviceable appellations imaginable. it may be employed in so many senses, from that of contemptuous pity, of ordinary condemnation, even to that unwillingly extorted admiration. <i>(1911,in Maoriland Bush,282</i>)

>  Shrewd or humorous person <i>(1916,Sentimental Bloke, The</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection) [Aust]]

>  Bill's only enemy was Bill himself. A hard case - yes! As hard as anything in the Army. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,171</i>)

>  They had in their ranks a fair number of dare-devil `hard cases' <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,15</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> `Nonsense, he is nothing of the kind, he is a real hard case, a first-class card sharper, and can do exactly what he likes with the cards. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,161</i>)

>  <b>MARKS</b> i don't suppose that in any business factory ... there are more `hard cases' than are to be found in a printing office. <i>(1934,Memories of the Past,68</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... a hard thing, a man noted for his outspokenness, his hardihood, or his kindly if abrupt eccentricity, with which compare the more widely known hard case; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

>  <b>TREADWELL</b> On another occasion ... there was a very `hard case' in the quarter-guard awaiting trial. <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,203</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> He's a hard case, that one ... i reckon he's a real shrewdy. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,123</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hard case. 2. in Australian and New Zealand, a person morally tough but not necessarily incorrigible; also a witty or amusing dare-devil,- one who loves fun and adventure; a girl ready for sexual escapades: all coll. from ca. 1880 <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,374</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> When they got back, John was encouraging Sandra to roll down the grass bank ... Over and over flashed Sandra's fat legs ... A lean man at John's elbow said, `That's a proper little hard case you've got, Hannay,' and John laughed his best-pleased laugh. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,24,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> One hard case, called Yorkey, ... is credited with eating two swan's eggs for breakfast- a good effort, even for a hungry man. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,101,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> [?a character] This set him thinking, and i must say it took something of the kind to do so, for he was one of the hardest old cases i have ever had the pleasure of collecting a good cheque from, .. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,86</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Cliff was a hard case. He knew lots of yarns too. <i>(1985,Some Other Country,78</i>) [NOTES: 1950 Gaskell `All Part of the Game' in 1984 Some Other Country 78 [a character] Also 1950 LF 13-18]

>  ~ n. an amusing witty person. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  hard case n.in Australia, tough or roughly amusing character; female of doubtful morals; in N.Z., mischievious person, hard liver. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Tank Tarrant had been one of the hardest cases that ever passed through Ruamahanga College. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,160</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> Geoff Dawson, the winger and a well-known hard case, was a bit tight. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,28</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> A real shithot bloke. Jesus, can he sink the piss. Real hard case that joker. Yah should seen him knocking back those half Gs. Pissed as a fart. ... spewed his ring out. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,85</i>)

>  hard case: used to describe someone who is regarded as a bit of a devil or rough; e.g., `Old Albert's a real hard case.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>HUNT</b> We were hard cases, too right; we weren't criminals but we had to live like criminals to survive. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,18</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> Some of the army men stationed at Whekenui must have been rather hard cases who liked their grog. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,85</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `And you keep on doing it? You're a bit of a bloody hard case, boy.' <i>(1983,Bone People, The,19</i>)

>  <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 510, HARD, sense 24; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 225]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hard case resolute, independent, wild, amusing or sexually available; eg `Boy, that Morrie's a hard case when it comes to impressing the sheilas. i've never seen him dip out yet.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)

>  ... the inconceivable dislike manifested for Australians by Maori landers (the hard cases for whom the innocent had to suffer) ... . <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65-5-6</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> i, the dissolute, the `hard case', as they termed me in their vernacular. <i>(1908,Anchorage,76</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> They were simply remembering Turei as he had been. The hardcase, always ready with a joke, the lovely boy who had once lived among them. <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,236</i>)

>  They are a pair of hard cases. <i>(Feb 12, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  My little brother is a very hard case. <i>(Jun 17, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a sexually adventurous or willing woman

>  it appeared from the record that Lydia [a prostitute] was a hard case, and had been to gaol before. <i>(Sep 9, 1905,NZ Truth,12-6-1</i>)

### hard cheese
 
 <i>n.</i> [also as exclamation]

>  hard cheese: bad luck <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection) hard cheese: bad luck]

>  <b>MASON</b> Hard cheese, eh. <i>(1970,End of the Golden Weather, The,93</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

### hard cott
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep fleece that is very badly matted.

>  <b>MAF</b> A very badly matted fleece is termed a hard cott. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> Grading - remove hard cotts ... <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,110</i>)

### hard country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) steep, hilly land with a difficult contour and poor pasture sole.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Their biggest day was Sykes 203 and Hart 202 - and that was in merinos off hard country. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,38</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Hard country: country running up to a height above which there is, or appears to be, nothing that can be grazed and which appears to be only rock and running scree. Nevertheless some of the highest quality mutton and wool come off such country. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,39</i>)

>  They [Rakahuri Corriedales] are bred on hard country. <i>(Feb 1, 1930,NZ Farmer,211</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. it had been performed on 'hard' country where courage must rank high in the many qualities essential for such work. <i>(1949,High Country Days,31</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Hard country: The harsher South island high country - largely "running" shingle and rock - is referred to as hard country. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,164</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> Most of us have a something we simply must do before life leaves us. For him, it was farming which we did the hard way on hard country. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  This gives a depth of 220 feet from the surface, the last two or three fathoms being through a belt of very hard country. <i>(Dec 15, 1869,NZ Herald,3</i>)

>  The andesites occur in bars, varying from 20ft. to 300 ft. in width, and are termed by the miners "hard country" or "blue bars". <i>(1896,AJHR,C.-3/50</i>)

>  The decomposed andesitic lavas generally possess a characteristic yellowish-brown or grey colour, and form the "kindly country" of the local miners; while the solid andesites and tuffs possess a greenish or dark-blue or purple colour and are generally known as "hard country." <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/38</i>)

>  After indefatiguable work for about 15 or 16 months, sinking a shaft through hard country, the shareholders came on to a very fine leader at a depth of 150 feet, and on following it up for a short distance the water broke in on them and drove them out. <i>(Mar 24, 1870,NZ Herald,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(farmers' and musterers')]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The fact that fine-wool sheep are usually run on hard country is another reason why these sheep are slower to shear. They do not suit good country because their feet go quickly on heavy or wet land. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Hard country: The harsher South island high country - largely `running' shingle and rock - is referred to as hard country. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,187</i>)

>  <b>PiLLiNG</b> Of the hard-doers of the Thirds, some lie at Anzac <i>(Jan 26, 1916,Anzac Memory,78,1933 ed.</i>)

### hard doer
 
 <i>n.</i> a hard case, a good sport (see doer, do it hard; and hard case for discussion).

>  <b>J.V. TWiDLE</b> Thursday 18th i was a hard looking doer covered with mud, thigh [gum]boots and steel helmet. <i>(May 18, 1916,Diary,No. 1 page 33</i>)

>  <b>ANZAC</b> He is, as a rule, a `hard-doer' <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,122</i>)

>  <b>iNGRAM</b> My companion is Stan Allen ... a real hard `doer', good pal and champion scrounger. in Ruahine Company we found several of our old `cobbers' who had joined up whilst we were still in the segregation camp at Etaples. <i>(Aug 29, 1917,Anzac Diary,39,1987 ed.</i>)

>  Of all our fearless hard doers he is the only one who has ever been known to march past a General ... with his `shell dressing' tied around his neck. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,96</i>)

>  Life was so simple when i was with the old Hard-Doers in Gallipoli and Armentieres. You carried on until you got a smack. <i>(Oct 1, 1918,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>WEBB</b> So 'ee laughed and said i was a 'ard doer all right, and 'ee married us. <i>(1925,Miss Peters' Special,49</i>)

>  ... 'struth ... a real hard doer. <i>(Sep 3, 1947,Landfall,166</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> He believed her grandmother practically lived on the mat and her mother was something of a hard-doer; ...<i>ibid.</i> 171 ... her mother was something of a hard-doer. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,142,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> `Struth,' Mr Garbers said. `A real hard doer!' <i>(1949,it Was So Late,46,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The younger one, a bloke about thirty-five, looks like a hard doer and wears a tweed hat all squashed on his head but he seems more steady on his feet ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,165</i>)

>  doer, hard doer n. a tough character, hard case. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),165</i>)

>  doer, hard doer ... n. tough character, hard case. <i>ibid.</i> 10 hard doer n. sophisticated person, tough character. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> A hard shot is `a hard case', `a hard doer' or `a hard nut', all these terms conveying an idea of eccentricity or lack of concern for conventions with a tone of tolerance or approval. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,121</i>)

>  hard doer NZ & Austral. = hard case. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,342</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 225]]

>  <b>McgiLL</b> hard doer incorrigible person, wag; eg `Cliff's a hard doer, take my word for it. He's written off 15 stock cars, never won a race, no prize money. Now he's mortgaged his house to get another.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)

>  ~ n. sophisticated person, a tough character. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  it doesn't seem to have struck anybody that the Engineer Corps, "Tunnellers", which has been trained in Auckland, is an absolutely unique body and will be known to history as "The Hard Doers". <i>(Dec 24, 1915,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> "Struth," Mr. Garbos said. "A real hard doer!" <i>(1949,it Was So Late,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hard-case or stoic character.

>  <b>KERR</b> Andy was a hard doer, immensely capable and ran Glentanner to the great satisfaction of its owners ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,16</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> John Harris, a hard doer, was sending his dog off for the Long Pull at the Waitaki club grounds.. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,158</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an ill-thrifty farm animal.

>  .. these ewe lambs retained being the hard-doers or culls, of the lambs so bred.. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,402</i>)

### hard feed
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mix of chaff and oats given to working horses when they are yarded early in the morning.

>  Such [colic] cases are common in the annual military camps with green horses coming on to hard feed and work, unless precautions are taken. <i>(Jun 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,524</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to place stock on low feed rations.

>  <b>MACFARLANE</b> if you hard-feed a Merino ram well, he will have no trouble in letting you cut off 30lbs and if you starve a Southdown ewe, she will be thankful to get rid of 3 pounds. <i>(1931,Farming: Facts, Faults & Fancies,55</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to feed farm stock on dry feed such as hay, barley, straw or silage.

>  They [the cows] are being kept on neighbouring leased land and hard fed. <i>(Jun 7, 2001,Press, The,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) dry stock feed such as hay or proprietary food.

>  if put on hard feed these teeth easily come out, and leave the animals 'gummies' in the course of a few weeks. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,15</i>)

### hard fern
 
 <i>n.</i> any of the many <i>Blechnum</i> ferns with thickish and harsh-textured fronds; also called pig fern. [a1927 Ver. (A). =Vernacular names supplied by B.C. Aston (And)<i> Paesia scaberula </i>]

>  <b>LOGAN</b> (And) <i>Blechnum penna marinum</i> alpine hard-fern. <i>(1875,Ferns of NZ</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Blechnum penna marinum</i> alpine hard-fern. <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Blechnum penna marinum</i> alpine hard-fern. <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Blechnum penna marinum</i> alpine hard-fern <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>WODZiCKi</b> Here [on the dry, rocky and open spurs on Stewart island] ferns were first slowly removed [by deer] except hard fern (<i>Blechnum discolor</i>). <i>ibid.</i> 223 ...while the common fern (<i>Blechnum discolor</i>) <i>(1950,introduced Mammals of NZ,219 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The most common stock-induced associations on the deforested areas are manuka, hard fern, and piripiri. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,247,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Those ferns having the generic name (<i>Blechnum</i>) are popularly known as hardferns by which is really meant hardy ferns, as indeed most species are. <i>ibid.</i> 106-107 This fern (<i>B[lechnum] penna-marina</i>) occurs at all levels but is much more plentiful at subalpine levels [107] whence the old name <i>B. alpina</i> and the popular name ALPiNE HARDFERN. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,105,(1929) ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNEiSH</b> The native, easy and fluent, slipping quietly through the hardfern and lawyer vines that wired the tunnels of the beach. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,14</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Blechnum</i> HARD FERNS ... They are easily recognized also by their hard texture and simply divided fronds. in some species young fronds are striking red or pink. <i>Blechnum</i> is a predominantly southern hemisphere genus of some 200 species; Stewart island has 12, which is about three- quarters of the New Zealand total. <i>ibid.</i> 460 <i>Blechnum</i> sp. `brown scale' Common hard fern ... Fronds more or less tufted but commonly covering extensive areas of open forest floor ... Frond scales uniformly brown. (ii) <i>Paesia scaberula</i> pig-fern, ring-fern, silver fern. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,456</i>)

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HARD FERN (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>), called also pig fern [ring fern], and silver fern, is abundant in both islands, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,19,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Paesia scaberula</i> (hard fern, ring fern, scented fern, carpet fern) is considered one of the most troublesome weeds of the wetter hill farms of New Zealand. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/113B</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> [=ring fern] Attuned to the geographical region were some particularly troublesome growths, and of these, the hard fern (or ring fern) became a special pest difficult to control and to guard against. ... This hard fern and piripiri were greatly encouraged by set grazing with sheep, ... <i>ibid.</i> Appendix ii 355 Hard fern - <i>Paesia scaberula</i>. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,15,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>hard fern [=] ring fern [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of the worst of the regrowth problems on this country [near Taumaranui] is the hard fern, or `ring' fern as it is sometimes known. ...Ring fern ... got the name because after burning the regrowth came up in the form of a circle outside the burnout area. <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,112</i>)

>  Hard ferns (<i>Blechnum</i>) are the next most diverse group, with 23 species. Several species produce young fronds that are tinged pink when first formed, and a bank of young <i>Blechnum</i> plants in spring can be a glorious sight. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hardy fern that readily occupies land cleared of bush. [also known as pigfern, bracken, ring fern, carpet fern or scented fern.]

>  in the case of hard fern and water-fern and felled wineberry, fuchsia etc early summer burning after a good dry spell would be advisable. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,102</i>)

>  By forcing cattle on to fern and other secondary growth .. it will become immediately open to invasion by herb-like weeds .. or by hard fern. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,92</i>)

>  the experiments included .. burning and sowing of hard fern and patches of water-fern. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,98</i>)

>  There is sometimes some problem from invasion by hard fern or water fern ... <i>(Mar 23, 1967,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

>  <b>HiNDMARSH</b> The sweet fern, (also known as hard fern, ring fern and carpet fern) was burned and grass sown on about 250 acres of the hardiest country. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,136</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Paesia scaberula (See [also silver king](sho-spa.md#silver-king))

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HARD FERN (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>), called also pig fern [ring fern], and silver fern, is abundant in both islands, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,19,1967 ed.</i>)

### hard graft
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [see graft](go-gra.md#graft))

>  For ian Ferguson and the other five Olympic canoeists, the preparation for the Games has been hard graft twice a day, seven days a week. <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

### hard grafter
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grafter](go-gra.md#grafter))

>  He was a hard grafter, was Murray .... <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hard grafter: The man you want to hire! Gung-ho all the way - this is the Stakhnovite of New Zealand. A HARD WORKER. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,51</i>)

### hard grazing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heavy or complete grazing of pasture to stimulate sward growth. (See [hard-graze](h-hea.md#hard-graze),[hard-grazed](h-hea.md#hard-grazed))

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFiELD</b> After hard grazing in the spring, a fresh supply of clover may be sod-seeded along with phosphatic fertiliser. <i>(1961,Agriculture,315</i>)

>  Tekapo - Densely tillered, prostrate cultivar suitable for hard grazing and dry environments. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,20</i>)

>  Hard grazing is also a common tool in promoting and maintaining a leafy sward. <i>(Mar 11, 2002,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming Livestock</i>)

### hard head
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Aythya australis</i>, white-eyed duck

>  White-eyed Duck <i>Aythya australis</i> (EYTON, 1838) ... OTHER NAMES: Karakahia, hard head, Australian white-eyed duck. ... Eastern and south western Australia. Vagrant to ... New Zealand. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,151</i>)

### hard heads
 
 <i>n.</i> knapweed

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> KNAPWEED (<i>Centaurea nigra</i>), called also hard heads, is found in waste places in both islands, but is not at all common. it is perennial, with a stem up to 3 ft. in height, though usually about a foot. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,194,1967 ed.</i>)

### hard knocker
 
 <i>n.</i> hard hitter?

>  <b>SARGESON</b> My uncle wears a hard knocker. <i>(1936,Conversations with My Uncle,1</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> The drummer, who has lost his uniform cap, ... [is] wearing a hard-knocker hat. He glares hard and bangs out the bumpy waltz time good-oh. <i>ibid.</i>136 he's wearing a hard-knocker hat. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,100,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... and standing there, all dressed up in his good Sunday suit and his hard-knocker hat, home from church, ... - father! <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,35,(1974 ed.Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> He rode through the capital city ... ., dressed as usually in bo-yangs and hard knocker. <i>(1962,Linkwater,124</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He takes off a squarish small-brimmed hard knocker, and hangs it on one of the pegs. <i>(1964,Wrestling with the Angel,70</i>) [NOTES: [State direction-reference early 1880's]]

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> ... i saw one of those hansom cabs with the cabby perched up behind with his hardknocker hat on. [reference ? c1920] <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,128</i>) [NOTES: `Great Times Ahead']

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> My father always wore a bowler hat, or hard knocker as they were called. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,8</i>)

### hard lines
 
 <i>n.</i> Common esp. in constr. it is (was etc.) hard lines on (a person, thing), to connote `hard going'; `hardship, hard times', be hard on = `fall heavily on' (hard = `difficult') rather than OED Line `6. ill luck, bad fortune (Prob. nautical in orig; now often associated with 4c).' 4c. Appointed lot in life (in echoes of Ps. xvi 6). OED quots for 6 (1824) are similar to the NZ quots, given below cf COD lines 2. [1984 16 M E67 Pakuranga Coll. 7 HARDLINES]

>  <b>BAiNES</b> After mother died, i left and went to be hut-keeper to a pair of sawyers. it was `hard lines' then i can tell you. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,53</i>)

>  it is hard lines for [Australian] Commonwealth citizens. <i>(Sep 15, 1906,NZ Truth,65-5-6</i>)

### hard lot
 
 <i>n.</i> = hard case (but phs more pejoratively used, in the sense `bad lot') [ca1948 [OEDS omit.]]

### hard milker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cow that does not readily yield milk during milking.

>  Most of the nurse cows are dairy culls, some of them good producers but hard milkers. <i>(Dec 16, 1954,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  i had to axe one or two hard milkers when i first started but non-stripping has certainly speeded up the whole job. <i>(Mar 1, 1947,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)

### hard nail
 
 <i>n.</i> ? A hard bargainer, a shrewd business man; ? a hard case, cf also hard as nails. [1855 Canterbury Papers: Letts to Godley 2/251 (J.W. Hamilton Christchurch)]

>  Creyke by the bye is what the old Australians and shagroons all call `a hard nail' <i>(1855,Canterbury Papers,2/251</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Canterbury Papers: Letts to Godley 2/251 (J.W. Hamilton Christchurch)]

### hard nuts
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This landless slog was going to be hard nuts by comparison. <i>(Oct 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

### hard shot
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ since ca1930 (Slatter).] a) (informal) an unorthodox, amusing, or incorrigible character; a dag. b) a person hardened by experience; ?Transf. from shot `one who shoots; an expert in shooting', often qualified by aj (good, bad shot) (cf good, bad sport) cf OED 22, esp 22b. (a queer shot) dial., an `odd customer'. good shot occas. used in NZ for `good sport', good boy: e.g. (a grandfather speaks) `Be a good shot now and get me my slippers'; =hard case.

>  <b>HYDE</b> `You get a divorce, Opal, eh? Fred's a hard shot, but he wouldn't stand in your way.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,184</i>)

>  <b>VAiLE</b> The girl's father was a real hard shot. <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,58</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... he looked a bit of a hard shot. <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,1/5</i>)

>  <i>(1943,Penguin New Writing,17/16</i>)

>  <b>CHERRiLL</b> He's a hard shot, i'll grant you. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,56</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's a hard shot. Yeah, he's a woopkacker all right <i>ibid.</i> 147 -Gone into partnership with old Snow. He's a hard shot. Hard as the hobs of hell. <i>ibid.</i> 183 Fitted bags of propellant to the two-inch mortar and stonked that sniper out of the church tower at Felisio. Real hard shot he was.Bags of guts. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> hard-shot: a 'hard-case joker' who is rather a wag. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>TURNER</b> A hard shot is `a hard case', `a hard doer' or `a hard nut', all these terms conveying an idea of eccentricity or lack of concern for conventions with a tone of tolerance or approval. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,121</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> They were hard shots all right, tough as boots. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,104</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hard shot law unto self, fiercely independent; eg `Frank's a real hard shot, no question. Lost everything in the fire, wasn't insured, won't accept help from anybody.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `He was the hard shot boy of the town. But all was forgiven when he became an All Black.' <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,231</i>)

### hard tack
 
 <i>n.</i> orig. ship's biscuit, used occasionally for any hard, dry, plain biscuit such as cabin bread; fierce liquor.

>  At Taumarunui recently a man from Raurimu was prosecuted for sly-grog selling. He admitted receiving 21 bottles of the hard tack per week, but said that he had procured the liquor for himself and friends, and they subscribed the cost between them. <i>(Jul 22, 1911,NZ Truth,317/1/5</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> As a youth on the fields, the writer knew an old husk of a man with red whiskers fringing a cadaverous face, a real type of a drinker of hard tack, as they called the fearful brand of whisky in vogue. <i>(1952,Faring South,158</i>) [NOTES: [c1880s]]

### hard thing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... a hard thing, a man noted for his outspokenness, his hardihood, or his kindly if abrupt eccentricity, with which compare the more widely known hard case; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hard thing. A C. 20 New Zealand variant of hard case, 2. Only of men. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,375</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> Hogan lay back on his bed, ... roaring with laughter. `You're getting a bloody hard thing,' he said at last. <i>(1976,Travelling Man,84</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hard thing resolute, outspoken or abrupt loner; eg `Polt's been up in that old whare for years, never spoken to a soul, except to buy his provisions, when he has to. Hard thing, that Polt.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)

>  Without a smoke, he was a hard thing. <i>(Jun 7, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iV/45 : 204</i>)

### hard thinker
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for a later batch of volunteers or reinforcements, see tourist. [WW1]

>  <b>M & N ROSS</b> There followed [after the `Dinkums' and `Tourists'] `The - War Babies' and `The Hard Thinkers', the latter having thought a great deal before they came. <i>(1916,Light and Shade,177</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The new arrivals in the 7th, the 8th and the 9th Reinforcements were not exactly despised, but were considered to be `hard thinkers.' That was early in 1916. However the late-comers lived down this unjust imputation and the seasoned warriors and the new arrivals were soon moulded into one united body. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,232</i>)

### hard tucker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) pasture feed that is short and dry.

>  All the ewes that are going to mate in the first 10 days will be in Mob A, will be identified and will go on hard tucker early on with a chaser to catch any stragglers. <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

### hard tussock
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Festuca novae-zelandiae</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native tussock (Festuca novae-zelandiae) that is similar to silver tussock.

>  The least edible plants - hard tussocks such as <i>F. novaezelandiae</i> and <i>F. matthewsii</i> - survived for a time under burning and grazing... <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/32</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFiELD</b> Hard tussock (Festuca novae-zelandiae) is similar to and often associated with silver tussock on low tussock country .. Hard tussock differs from silver tussock in ts leaves feeling rough and its seed-head having short awns. <i>(1961,Agriculture,271</i>)

>  Less grand and altogether less fortunate are the short tussocks. Growing up to half a metre in height, they include such hardy species as blue tussock (<i>Poa colensoi</i>), silver tussock (<i>P. cita</i>) and hard, or fescue, tussock (<i>Festuca novaezelandiae</i>).... [29] Rub your hand down a leaf-blade of hard tussock, and you'll find it prickly and rough. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/28</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> <i>Festuca novae-zelandiae</i> (hard tussock) ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/486</i>)

>  Higher up the valley, where the main grasses are both silver and hard tussock, danthonia is not yet an important element of the pastures. <i>(Jun 29, 1911,Marlborough Express,5</i>)

### hard yacker/hard yakker
 
 <i>n.</i> hard work; toil, graft (See [yacker/yakker](y-yc.md#yacker/yakker))

>  it would seem to be hard yacker standing for parliamentary seats if you're in there with a chance. <i>(Dec 3, 1992,Contact (Wgtn),6</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> Big money entailing hard yakker,. Hard yacker that carried a touch of frenzy. <i>(1964,Watersiders,85</i>)

>  Salvarmy's `ome of `ope soap, and hard yacker. <i>(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>FANNiNG</b> The forwards do all the hard yacker ... and don't catch the reporter's eye. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,20</i>)

>  Yes! it is THE life `selling fish to the public,' as the old engineer was wont to observe at frequent intervals, but to those who are not prepared for a rough time and hard solid `yacker' in wind and rain, i would say `Keep away from it.' <i>((12/0/1912),NZ Truth,381/6/4</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `There are a lot of things i should've done,' i reply, ...[44] `Like getting down to some hard yakker,' he goes on. -<i>ibid.</i> 91 You'll haveta shake ya shirt and get down to some hard yakker. Cunning as a Maori dog. Too right he is. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,43</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> [sp yacca] A tough job: Or as one member, Reg. Petrowski put it, `Hard yacca'. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,109</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> i'd blown all me chips so it was a case of getting down to some hard yakker for a while. <i>(1960,Word for Word,253</i>)

>  [Leader] ... education is not play but, to employ a colloquialism, `hard yakka'; ... <i>(Sep 29, 1960,Dominion,10</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Hard toil. .in 4;`Plenty hard yakker, working on the pipeline, they have to break in the afternoon, the men can't handle the pipes in those temperatures.' <i>(1964,Australian & NZ English [TS],1112</i>) [NOTES: 1962-63]

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> (Not for a Seagull) `But it's not all hard yakker with nothing at the end.' <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,241</i>)

>  ... he ... had to bash through the lawyers and windfalls. it was hard yakka, ... <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  ... it was so rough couldn't you let go anything on the boat i was on. `Then we had to get this fellow onto our boat. it was hard yakker. <i>(Nov 1977,Public Service Journal,Vol. 64. No. 10 5/5</i>)

>  DEViSiNG methods to counter cervical injuries in rugby was yesterday described as `hard yakker.' `Trying to get messages across to all concerned is hard yakker.' <i>(Jun 7, 1980,Dominion,24/4</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hard yacker: HARD WORK <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,51</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... and they got me a job. Pretty hard yakker too, packing in stuff to the mine from the sawmill ... <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,24</i>)

>  `How do you like the place?' [Tongariro Corrective Training Centre] `Hate it, Sir.' `What, the work?' `Yes, Sir. its bloody hard yakka.' <i>(Sep 19, 1982,NZ Times,16</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> (Bill Henderson) i've seen my cousin carrying two four-gallon pails of water on a yoke on her shoulders ... Hard yakker for a woman, living those days. <i>(1985,i'm Ninety-five,34</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLiVAN</b> Last week i was down the river there with my lot filling sandbags. Hot as hell, sandflies, hard yakker like that, ... <i>(1985,Shuriken,53</i>)

>  <b>RiCKETTS</b> [Keri Hulme speaks] it was hard yakka. Then i became a postie. i shifted over to the West Coast because there was a senior postie position over there. <i>(1986,Talking About Ourselves,22</i>)

>  There was a few days' work for me though, doing the hard yakka while the joker in the pure white overalls put the things together like a Meccano set. <i>(Jul 13, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),C12</i>)

>  He was kept back by nasal trouble for a long while and bravely tackled cruelly hard yakker to support his sweet young wife and baby girl. <i>(Nov 14, 1914,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  For part of that time i was a single parent. it was bloody hard yacker. <i>(Jul 11, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### hard yakka
 
 <i>n.</i> hard work

>  Simple hard yakka is an essential element in his story. He has been working since starting a milk run as a nine-year-old schoolboy at Foxton's St Mary's convent. <i>(Jun 10, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  The road so far has been difficult - but the path ahead is still paved with hard yakka and difficult decisions. <i>(Aug 25, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  Picture the innocent sweetie as she trotted into the lawyer's office, hankie in hand, sobs stifled, to hear the good news about the year of hard yakka she'd put in giving the old boy his moment of joy. <i>(Nov 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Not these guys and gals; pick and shovel and months of pure hard yakka. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,36</i>)

>  His father.. made no allowances. A full day's work. Hard yakka for a four-year-old. <i>(Feb 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B11</i>)

>  But for 30 years i've devoted myself to work, and it's been hard yakka, you know. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  it wasn't an easy job. it was hard yakka. Everything i did was hard yakka. <i>(May 2004,Wel-com (Wellington),12</i>)

>  After that, until war broke out, he worked at a series of hard yakka jobs, such as high country mustering and general farm work, with time off for a bit of boxing, running and rugby. <i>(May 31, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  <B>WANTED!</b> 2 young, fit men for 2-3 consecutive days hard yakka moving sand with wheel barrows. <i>(Dec 24, 2006,Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle,22</i>)

### hard yards
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes Not known to me personally and not in any dictionary I have seen, It seems to have suddenly become popular. I believe the term derives from gridiron football and is thus likely to be international. DEH notes: I couldn't find any US traces but think it more likely to be from Australian League or Aussie Rules.]

>  After putting in the hard yards contemplating the assets of the fab 15, she was obviously feeling the need to take a look at something a little more impressive. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

>  We've all had to do the hard yards, it's just part of the equation. <i>(Jun 13, 2001,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  Cindy was there to show that perfection is within our grasp if we're prepared to do the hard yards. <i>(Sep 5, 2000,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  [Hdg]Hard yards needed, says NZ selector. <i>(Oct 4, 2000,Evening Post,36</i>)

>  investigators do hard yards on Blakie inquiry. <i>(May 20, 2000,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Let us do the hard yards, <i>(Jun 27, 2000,Wairarapa Midweek,00</i>)

### hard-backed snapper
 
 <i>n.</i> [toheroa]

>  <i>hard-backed snapper </i>: toheroa <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### hard-case
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  They're some hard-case fellas," he says with a giggle, "and i"m just learning so much." <i>(Oct 4, 1998,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)

>  Citation missing <i>(Sep 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>J.V. TWiDLE</b> Spent the day building Bivvies. they are hard case affairs. <i>(May 23, 1916,Diary,[1/37]</i>)

>  <b>iNGRAM</b> Stan Allan, my hardcase pal, who is forever [48] prowling, ... came across a dead Jerry's leg sticking out from the side of a large shell hole. <i>(Oct 2, 1917,Anzac Diary,47</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> After an inspection of our hard-case looking crockery as they went back through the kitchen, the ladies refused my tentative offer of afternoon tea ... <i>(1924,Follow the Call,55,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... his hard-case old face <i>(1945,When the Wind Blows,36</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> `Pair of Socks' ... he was the hardest case bloke you ever came across <i>(1946,That Summer,31</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He followed the trail of a `hard-case bloke called Sam.' <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,178</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> hard case: a hard-case joker, a morally tough and irrepressible character who has had wide experience of people and places; a regular hard case to talk to, an interesting and entertaining fellow; a hard-case old sheila, an amiable and amicable woman with experience of the world and of men. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> Pat Hanley is remembered as a `hard case' mill owner from Gore ... <i>(1966,in the Lee of the Hokonuis,388</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Complete with a long whip and a hardcase old straw hat, the [child] `bullocky' looked the part. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,96</i>)

>  Rose is the hard case one in the family, the kamakama one and the one with brains. <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories iii,185</i>) [NOTES: >1972 Grace `A Way of Talking' in 1975 NZ Short Stories iii 185]

>  <b>AGNEW</b> `A hard-case bastard, that Whippet,' Buffalo Bill said. `He seemed okay.' `He is, but he's a hard-case all the same.' <i>(1974,Loner, The,67</i>)

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> [?Maori usage] This was my first night back in Gisborne and naturally my sisters were as hardcase as ever - the Maori version of the Three Stooges, Ko, To and Wo. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,40</i>) [NOTES: [in Maori English cf hatakehi--> transliteration (Mason Awatea 99)]]

>  "it's a bit of a hard case look at the birth of bicultural Ao Te Aroa," says Terei. <i>(Jan 31, 2000,Evening Post,TV Week : 3</i>)

### hard-casery
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Crump `Bastards' 1971 p59.] Prob. manufactured (Griffiths Collection).]

### hard-earned
 
 <i>n.</i> constr. the, money; (concretely) a sum of money.

>  <b>MacCALLUM</b> i have to separate myself from another couple of the hard-earned [pound notes] for these [cartridges]. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,39</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Let's face it, in the present economic clime anybody who relieves you of the old hard-earned is worthy of notice. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,6</i>)

### hard-feeding
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the feeding of stock with hay, chaff etc.

>  This month's hard-feeding which was necessitated on many of the stations and upland farms has run into a big bill. <i>(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,5</i>)

### hard-graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to graze a paddock or pasture closely or heavily to stimulate growth.

>  Similar treatment during November after the area has been hard-grazed with cattle (not sheep) will account for most of the older and mature plants. <i>(Jan 1961,NZ Journal of Agriculture,241</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Paddocks to be conserved, usually those near the homestead, should be hard-grazed early in the autumn to permit good recovery before winter. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,57</i>)

### hard-grazed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a paddock, crop or pasture] heavily eaten out.

>  To be successful it is imperative that oversowing be attempted only on a hard-grazed pasture ... <i>(Apr 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,359</i>)

>  Wet ewes will require watching carefully, and any hard-grazed paddocks eased up if there is any better feed to put the ewes on to. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,221</i>)

>  After the lambs are got away in December the ewes are held in a few hard-grazed paddocks to prevent them becoming over-fat and then flushed on some of the recovered growth prior to tupping. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,501</i>)

>  .. results have been quite satisfactory from the practice of oversowing first on to a hard-grazed pasture and then harrowing fairly drastically. <i>(Apr 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,361</i>)

>  Some control [of barley grass seedheads] could probably be achieved by keeping pastures fairly hard-grazed in late spring. <i>(Dec 1976,NZ Journal of Agriculture,18</i>)

### hard-hitter
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The old man in the hard-hitter hat/ walks his dog in the early morning sun/ <i>(1982,Coming and Going,16</i>) [NOTES: (`Starting')]

### hard-labour man
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The first and only improvement of our public ways for years in New Zealand was effected by what are called Hard labour men, viz., the inmates of the gaol, and little as they effected, for their name was quite a misnomer, still it was all the colony had to depend upon for local improvements, ... <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,184</i>)

### hard-out
 
 <i>adj.</i> loyal, staunch

>  ...i'll still be a hard-out Poriruvian. Don't you know what a hard-out means? it means loyal. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Dominion Post,E2</i>)

### hard-stock
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mob stock an area of land, in order to make it bare of vegetation. (See [hard-graze](h-hea.md#hard-graze))

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> This paddock had been hard-stocked to make it bare of grass. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,67</i>)

### hard-up
 
 <i>adj.</i> ??? [SOED hard-up: tobacco from cigarette-ends etc.]

>  No swagger passes his place without giving him a call, and how many free feeds he gives away in a twelve-month it would be hard to say, besides never refusing to [sic] a hard-up tobacco which must run him into a pretty stiff sum as they all `bite his lug' for it. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

### hard-up social
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a social to which guests come dressed as if they were `hard up' or poor (cf. a shipwreck party or ball).

>  A `Hard-Up' Social took place at Greymouth. <i>(Sep 2, 1905,NZ Truth,11-1-8</i>)

### hard-walking country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] steep, high country with shingle slides and bluffs, inaccessible on horseback.

>  <b>KERR</b> .. they [North islanders] didn't like hard-walking country or free-running merinos ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,168</i>)

### hardbelly
 
 <i>n.</i> blue maomao (?Aust.).

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLUE MAOMAO ... Other names: <i>Scorpis aequipinnis</i>; hardbelly (Australia); maomao, hui (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,244</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BLUE MAOMAO [<i>Scorpis aequipinnis</i>] ... The species also occurs in Australia where it is known as hardbelly. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,40</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] hardbelly [TS] 1513 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### hardcase-looking
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>LETHBRiDGE</b> A rattle of pebbles ... announced the arrival of five or six / hardcase - looking sheep. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,49</i>)

### hardcore
 
 <i>adv.</i> intently?

>  You were watching him so hardcore the other day... <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

### harden
 
 <i>v.</i> drag a boat up onto the hard.

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Once inside [the inlet] you can harden your dinghy and go up a good sandy beach to a dry cave floor. <i>(1982,in the Grip of an island,118</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] to dry out and be incapable of producing facial eczema.

>  <b>EDGAR</b> When only the skin is affected, recovery usually takes place rapidly when weather conditions 'harden' the pasture. <i>(1959,NZ Farmers' Veterinary Guide,136</i>)

>  With the advance of spring, feed tends to harden and to run to seed. <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Pasture is judged to be safe when the soft lush growth, produced by the first rains of autumn, matures or 'hardens' as a result of a marked change in the weather ... <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,121</i>)

>  it should .. be rationed to the milking cows to make it last until spring feed comes away and hardens. <i>(Aug 15, 1960,NZ Journal of Agriculture,142</i>)

### harden up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [pasture and crops] to strengthen or mature.

>  Although somewhat laxative when the growth is very young and short, it [lucerne] soon hardens up if sunshine prevails. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,387</i>)

### hardhead
 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf hardyhead.]

>  The Hardhead (<i>Kathetostoma monopterygium</i>) is also seen occasionally, but its forbidding appearance is sufficient to prevent its being eaten, though wholesome enough. <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/486</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> <i>Kathetostoma monopterygium</i> Bleeker Cat-fish, Hard-head, or Ngu <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,301</i>)

>  <i>Genyagnus maculatus</i> Forster. The cat-fish or hard-head. <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/549</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] hardhead 1824, 1840 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### harebell
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Wahlenbergia</i> spp. [<i>Oligosoma hardyi</i> aka pre 2009 <i>Cyclodina hardyi</i>]

>  several veronicas of great beauty, wild thyme, dwarf yew, Alpine rose (<i>consanthenum</i>), white violet, a ribes, several heaths, gentianella, harebell, a lily, and several varieties of ranunculus. <i>(Feb 21, 1851,NZ Government Gazette,34</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 34 ...]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The chief plants i noticed [on Ruapehu] were a beautiful everlasting, the harebell, 2 kinds of gentian, a pretty speedwell, an epacris ... a wild carrot also abounded. <i>(Jan 30, 1853,Journal,8/70</i>)

>  <i>Wahlenbergia gracilis </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Wahlenbergia albomarginata</i> Harebell <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,250</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Wahlenbergia gracilis.</i>

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Wahlenbergia ... gracilis</i> ... Blue Harebell. RiMU-ROA (tall moss). <i>(1952,Chatham islands,44</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Wahlenbergia matthewsii.</i>

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Very choice rock-frequenting, floriferous herbs native to Marlborough include the KAiKOURA BUTTERCUP (<i>Ranuculus lobulatus</i>), ... the MARLBOROUGH HARE-BELL <i>(1929,Flora NZ,254,1961 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Wahlenbergia gracilis.</i>

>  Everywhere the graceful native harebell [fn <i>Wahlenbergia gracilis</i>.] shows its blue or white flowers, and occasionally a pretty white orchid is to be met with, or a tuft of the grassy-leaved, small-flowered native iris [fn <i>Libertia ixioides</i>.]. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,183</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Wahlenbergia gracilis.</i>

>  <i>harebell, New Zealand [s] Wahlenbergia gracilis</i> (Forst.f.) Schrad. <i>ibid.</i> 52 <i>New Zealand harebell [s] Wahlenbergia gracilis</i> (Forst.f.) Schrad. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> NEW ZEALAND HAREBELL (<i>Wahlenbergia gracilis</i>) Spindly but graceful native ... with a few deep-blue bell-shaped flowers. ... <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,103</i>)

>  Like our indigenous frogs, the Harlequin gecko is extremely long-lived and thought to live 20 - 30 years. <i>(May 2009,NZ Geographic,97/18</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  [Also eaten were] several Fungi [especially] ... four ... which grow on trees, - the Harori (<i>Agaricus adiposus</i>), the Hakeke, and the Popoiahakeke (<i>Polyporus</i> species, and the Pekepekekiore (<i>Hydnum clathroides</i>); also, three terrestrial ones, - the Paruwhatitiri (<i>ileodictyon cibarium</i>), the Pukurau (<i>Lycoperdon Fontainesii</i>), ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: -->1869 TrNZi (Colenso Botany North isd)]

 <i>n.</i> [(Cant. station)1952 Apr 21 Newton letter ... after a famous Christchurch firm of bootmakers]

>  <b>McCASKiLL</b> The best boots [1907-14 period] came from Pannells in Christchurch and Ford in Rangiora, but even they might last only six week or seven [mustering]-) <i>(1969,Molesworth,101</i>) [NOTES: Harry Cave was a NZ cricketer in the 1950s. He had an unusual bowling action.]

### HART
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Halt All Racist Tours [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  in 1981 he was a prime figure [sic] in the protest against the Springbok tour (it was Poata who in 1969 thought up the name hart). <i>(Dec 1, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  The group liked the idea but not the name - the National Organisation against Hypocrisy. The meeting [in 1969] chose instead Tom Poata's suggestion - Halt All Racist Tours... Hart was not the only New Zealand organisation to support the outlawed African National Congress and seek to ban tours by South Africa's all-white sporting teams. <i>(Jul 18, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  HART (Halt All Royal Tours) will have to be revived, to provide inspiration and leadership. <i>(Nov 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  Anti-apartheid groups split last night over the controversial visit of the South African golf team. The Citizens Association of Racial Equality (Care) entered a pact not to disrupt the tourney featuring the South Africans in Auckland. But Halt All Racist Tours (Hart) pledged to go ahead with plans to disrupt the event. <i>(Sep 23, 1971,Otago Daily Times,3</i>)

>  Tama Poata coined the name : Halt All racist Tours... Richards describes Hart as "a typical child of its time: rebellious, internationalist, confident, optimistic ... the creation of a hard-headed pragmatic brand of idealism." <i>(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  A number of us became involved with Halt All Racist Tours (HART) when it formed on campus. <i>(2000,Lemon,3 : 28</i>)

>  <b>Grimshaw</b> i said i'd seen a TV statement from the anti tour group HART, denouncing her stunt, calling for restraint. <i>(2007,Opportunity,169</i>)

>  The chairman and main spokesperson for Hart believes the Rugby Union will not persist with the South African Tour if it means sacrificing rugby itself - and Hart intends the stakes should be that high.... On our TV sets his face is a perfect complement to the darker side of Hart's reputation - pale with intensity, piranha mouth, features a shale-slide of sharp angles, overall effect a combination of the fanatic's aggression and the martyr's masochism. <i>(Jun 22, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

 <i>n.</i> usu. humorous or ironical for inferior food badly-prepared or badly-served, not necessarily chopped up chiefly in compounds in NZ : dry hash, hash-foundry, hash-house, hash-me-gandy; hash-up; hash is Std E. (cf OED sb1, and COD), and specific uses may be dialect : cf EDD. from Nhb `sheep's lights boiled then minced small and stewed with onions'.; or NZ (Dry Hash) chopped or minced left-overs, heavily seasoned or spiced, and baked without liquid; food.  <i>n.</i> a Hash House Harriers' event

The name Hash House came about because it was what these Britons called the boarding house where they lived. <i>(Jun 4, 2003,Capital Times,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> boarding house (cheap, inferior) (also used attrib.); cf hash @n.

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> restaurant, hash-foundry [ca1940- general among Services, Students and schoolboys.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And the proper form and ceremony after having a few wets was to have a binder and they stumbled by instinct to the hash house ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,165</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### hash-me-gander
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [hash-me-gandy](h-hea.md#hash-me-gandy))

>  Monday's "Hash-me gander" was a mystery to us all. <i>(Sep 6, 1915,Stratford Evening Post,6</i>) [NOTES: From a poem about a station cook]

### hash-me-gandy
 
 <i>n.</i> hashmagandy.Station stew (?transf. to freezing works, etc.). [ca1940 - Canterbury rural workers', and trampers' (Les Cleveland)]

>  Hash-me-gandy.-Station stew. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hash-me-gandy. Station stew: New Zealand and Australian rural: since ca. 1920. B., 1942, suggests: ex Mahatma Gandhi's frugal meals. An elaboration of hash <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1072</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> in the early eighteen-nineties digesters were installed for `boiling down' the offals. ... This was essentially a stewing process which took four to six hours' cooking, and separated the fat by partial breakdown of the animal tissues. The residue, or `ashmegandy', was dried in a machine for fertiliser material, as was the blood. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,7</i>)

>  hashmagandy, hash-me-gandy n. bush stew. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  hashmagandy, hash-me-gandy n. bush stew. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Hash-me-gandy. Station stew. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  hash-me-gandy sheep station stew; ref. meagre eating habit of Mahatma Gandhi and English `salmagundi', spiced meat and eggs; c. 1920. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station stew, made of mutton.

>  .. no matter what meal it was, the killers would have contributed ... tea: cold mutton or 'hash-me-gandy' - stew. <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Terms bequeathed to us by shearers and tramps and farmers, that appear to have originated this century .. hash-me-gandy, station stew. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,54</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Hash-me-gandy, station stew. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,381</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> A bad cook was a "blacksmith" and the regular mutton stew was a "hash-me-gandy." <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  <b>MCGiLL</b> hash-me-gandy: sheep station stew ... <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)

### hatch tent
 
 <i>n.</i> [(watersiders')]

>  <b>DAViS</b> `Right, up we go and put the tent on. The rain's gathering a bit of weight.' A hatch tent consists of a large, light tarpaulin which is lashed down round the hatch coaming while its centre is held up by a wire strung fore-and-aft across the hold. it provides effective temporary cover for an open hold, saving the time and labour required to replace the more cumbersome permanent hatch covers. <i>(1964,Watersiders,97</i>)

<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

### hatrack
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a thin cow.

### hatter
 
 <i>n.</i> a goldminer who works alone [probably f. `a hat covers all his family' (OED), `has all his property under his hat' etc. [goldmining]

>  <b>GRACE</b> "The game is to go mates with him - Scarlett, the 'hatter', and myself." <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,35</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [Persons who work holes alone] are usually called `hatters.' <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,66</i>) [NOTES: [get fuller cit]]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> "i don't know that it was anything particular that made me take to being a 'hatter'". <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,140</i>)

>  There was no doctor of course within hail and no hospital handier than a "hatters" 6 by 8 tent. <i>(Oct 23, 1890,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  Here i met a couple of "hatters" working away in Maori Gully. <i>(Aug 4, 1899,Auckland Weekly News,4</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> They [sc. miners] dug and washed for gold in the creeks with the most primitive of outfits, amidst much toil and privation; and the solitaries came to be known as 'Hatters', the only explanation i ever got of the term being, that if they had nothing else to wash in, they washed in their hats! <i>(1911,Through South Westland,19</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRiGG</b> Shepherds watching the sheep on distant parts of an estate often went made from loneliness or food poisoning - such men were often known as 'hatters' <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,52</i>)

>  [Gold] Diggers for 'hatters' as they were often termed persisted, a few into the last decade. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,110</i>)

>  <b>DAViES</b> A regular rum old stick, a Swede or a German, i believe; he mostly works as a "hatter". <i>(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,267</i>)

>  in 1883 two claims.. were taken by a well-known "hatter" - solitary miner - named Edwards. <i>(Sep 21, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  <b>FARJEON</b> i was working as a `hatter'. <i>(1865,Shadows on the Snow,76</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Some of the men [are], in mining phraseology, `Hatters', that is men who live and work by themselves content with the solitude of well kept huts, a few books [etc] ... .<i>ibid.</i> [144] hard work, their dog as companion, perhaps some poultry, and a weekly visit on Saturdays to the townships to sell their gold and see friends. Whence the term `hatter' i have never discovered; maybe because such solitaries are regarded as `mad as a hatter.' <i>(Jul 10, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,143,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> By one of these [West Coast] `beachers,' a countryman of mine, we were most hospitably entertained, the table being covered with several luxuries of the season which i did not expect in the abode of a `hatter' who are generally of a morose character, and do not indulge in any comforts of life. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,529</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 529]

>  <b>PYKE</b> He was what is termed in mining phraseology, a `hatter', - meaning one who works by himself. ... Alone and unaided he scooped the wash-dirt into his cradle, which he vigorously rocked with his left hand, whilst with the right he ladled water from the river into the `hopper' or seive [sic]. ... He cast aside the `dipper', and hastened towards the eddy. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/vii/95,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Hatter: Whatever the origin of the phrase, `as mad as a hatter' can have been, i have often unsuccessfully endeavoured to discover. But doubtless it is from this phrase that the name of hatter has come to be applied in mining parlance, to one who works alone or without a mate. There are always a few of these unsocial miners about every digging, and their solitariness arises from every imaginable cause. Frequently they are misanthropes, sometimes placid contented creatures, who, so long as they can make a living by `fossicking' about, are happy. / [139]. My host was a hatter and he always kept a spare stretcher for any chance passer-by. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,138</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> These [holes] frequently form the grave of some unfortunate `hatter', as a man who works alone and has all his property `under his hat' is called [in Central Otago]. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,189</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> By one of these `beachers,' a countryman of mine, we were most hospitably entertained, the table being covered with several luxuries of the season which i did not expect in the abode of a `hatter,' [[fn] in gold-mining communities those men who work for themselves are usually called hatters, amongst them many originals are found.] who are generally of a morose character, and do not indulge in any comforts of life. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,159</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `i wonder what can be the origin of [the term, hatter]?' `i don't know, i'm sure. Oh, i'll tell you. Those men who work alone are, i should think, more or less cranky, and you know the phrase `as mad as a hatter.' So i suppose they came to be styled `hatters'. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,88</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> Miners who work alone are called `hatters', one explanation of the term being that they frequently go mad from the solitude of their claim away in the bush, exemplifying the proverb `as mad as a hatter.' <i>(1889,New Zealand After 50 Years,165</i>)

>  The Waikeri goldfield [near Alexandra], where a good deal of ground has been turned over, was little better than a `hatter's' diggings in its best days. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 2. (N.S.) 26</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  Even up to the present day a few lonely `hatters' eke out a scant and uncertain livelihood [near Karamea, Nelson]. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 3. (NS) 16</i>) [NOTES: (Bell)]

>  ... mates, from their `clearing' on the other side of the Maori-Dog range; Sam Barriball, the `hatter' whose `claim' was supposed to be somewhere on the Glenroy river; ... <i>(Dec 11, 1911,Triad, The,21</i>) [NOTES: A.A.Grace (The Fate of Morland)]

>  <b>BELL</b> At times one comes across [at Karamea] an old hatter ... [fn] Back-blocks prospector. <i>(1914,Wilds of Maoriland,135</i>)

>  ~ n. a miner who works alone; any one who works by himself. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> An old `hatter' living [West Coast, 1890's] just above the beach, and looking out for patches of gold. ... i don't know the origin of the name, but some say it was because the man worked alone under his hat. <i>(1946,Memories of Mountains,113</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> An old `hatter' living [West Coast, 1890's] just above the beach, and looking out for patches of gold. ... i don't know the origin of the name, but some say it was because the man worked alone under his hat. <i>(1946,Memories of Mountains,113</i>)

>  <b>HEiNZ</b> Hatter: A solitary miner, working on his own account. <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> ... indeed, i have known hatters or gully rakers, as men fossicking up back creeks were called, who relied very largely on ... their dogs' hunting success. advertised for a housekeeper. <i>(1952,Faring South,103</i>)

>  ~ An Australian term for a solitary miner. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),194,8th ed.</i>)

>  ~ n. a miner who works alone, a solitary eccentric bush- dweller. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> HATTER: a solitary, a digger who works alone. The derivation of this New Zealand expression is obscure. Archdeacon Harper described the hatters as `men who live and work by themselves, often old sailors and much travelled men, content with the solitude of well-kept huts, a few books, hard work, their dog as companion, perhaps some poultry, and a weekly visit on Saturdays to the townships to sell their gold and see friends. Whence the term `hatter' i have never discovered; maybe because such solitaries are regarded as `mad as a hatter'. Another possibility is suggested by the remark that the hatter `kept his whole world under his hat'. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,527,1962 ed.</i>)

>  hatter n.prospector who works alone. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Hatter: miner who works alone. Probably derived from the habit of such men of leaving their hats at the surface to indicate that they were working down the mine shaft. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> At that time, only a few old bachelors and hatters were left living at Moonlight. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,89</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary shepherd or out-station shepherd suffering from loneliness and,often, psychosis.

>  <b>ELDRED-GRiGG</b> Shepherds watching the sheep on distant parts of an estate often went mad from loneliness or food poisoning - such men were known as 'hatters'. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,52</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> extended to other occupations, a solitary person

>  Hatter. One who works and camps by himself. Originally a miner's expression, no doubt from the saying of a man without relations that he had `all his family under his hat'. Archdeacon Harper derived the word from `mad as a hatter' (Letters, p. 143). The word is in general use on stations. H.'s billy, tent, etc., one just large enough for one man. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Hatter, ... (1) A solitary miner - miner who works without a mate partner: sc. one who has everything under his own hat. <i>(1898,Austral English,193</i>)

>  <b>BROUGH SMYTH</b> <i>(1869,Goldfields Victoria,613</i>)

>  He has been living as a `hatter' in the weird and whirling of Awhitu. But, tiring of playing a lone hand, and yearning for home comforts and luxuries, he <i>(Nov 13, 1909,NZ Truth,229/7/4</i>)

>  <b>Gibson</b> The skipper [of the Auckland timber-scow] i seldom saw, for he was a hatter and kept to his cabin and keg. <i>(1924,That Gibbie Galoot,124</i>)

>  hatter ... NZ & Austral. man who lives alone (orig. miner), solitary bushman. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,344</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a homosexual male accoster (?or prostitute; a brown hatter. [ca 1945 (Wellington)]

>  <b>iRELAND</b> So far as he was concerned 'old Frank' may be a decent enough sort of chap, but there were rumours i probably hadn't heard that he was one of the 'brown hatters'. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,152</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> A criminal that works alone and is very secretive: Australian late C. 19-20. in, e.g., Morris, 1898; Partridge, 1938 Baker, 1942. Also hatting (-1890): such a way of working and living. Ex the Australians. hatter, a miner working and living alone: he may go as mad as a hatter. <i>(1941,Dictionary of the Underworld,322,1961 ed.</i>)

### Hauhau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He castigates me for employing the term "Hauhau". Maori, he says, did not refer to themselves as Hauhau. He overlooks that i qualify the word. i describe it as a "misleading label". <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)
