>  <b>MONEY</b> Right in front of us, under the shade of Mount Egmont and far in the bush, we could see the smoke daily from a large village called Pungarahu, said to be a nest of Hau-haus. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,137</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> Kirimata.. a noted chief of the extinct How-Hows, was a formidable customer. <i>(1905,Return of Joe,310</i>)

>  [He] did not worry himself over the chances of retribution [in the King Country] for evading the `Aukati', the Hauhaus' `Thou-shalt-come-no-further.' <i>(Mar 3, 1915,Canterbury Times</i>) [NOTES: (VUW Fildes Clippings 421/58-59)]

>  His courage won him fame in Opotiki after the Hau Haus had been squashed. <i>(Mar 8, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  The natives treated us with great kindness and hospitality, as indeed we invariably find to be the case with all the Hauhaus we visited. <i>(May 10, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The Hauhau experienced their first flush of victory after ambushing troops caught destroving Maori cultivations at Ta Ahuahu, Oakura. <i>(1983,illustrated History of Taranaki,47</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> How did they regard the Queen's Maori soldiers? They had a name for them - Kupapa, a derisive term used by Kingite and Hau Hau to identify those they believe "to be crawling on their bellies to the pakeha. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,44</i>)

>  There was also another word of importance which they received from the angel [Gabriel], after the fight at Ahu Ahu, namely Hauhau. <i>(May 17, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>WiTTY</b> in the month of October, '66, / Sir Donald McLean was in a fix / For the Hau Haus were going to fight like bricks / At a place called Oamarunui. <i>(1912,Colonial Songs,[3]</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. uses]

>  The cantankerous Nanny Smack is a 19th-century Hauhau "witch"... <i>(Dec 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> The Hauhau call to prayers is sounded by the beating of the pahu, a sounding piece of wood ... it is made (when procurable) of the wood of the Poro Kaiwhiria (Hedycaria dentata), an aromatic tree. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,16</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> 'Hauhau bastard!' <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,15</i>)

>  Perhaps Bishop Neligan knows nothing about.. the Hau-hau adaptation of the Christian faith. <i>(Nov 26, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  in other words she backed the Government against the Hau Hau movement. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/25</i>)

### Hauhau pole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> At the principal villages in that rugged region ... the Hauhau poles, the sacred niu, were set up and round them marched the half-crazed people ... chanting their newly learned hymns ... the prayers of the `Kororia,' Maorified English phrases and scraps of English and Roman Catholic church services. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,85</i>)

### Hauhauism
 
 <i>n.</i> [Respecting Hauhauism, he said that there were three things that give rise to it: ... Ibid. 193 Hauhuaism is fast dying out.1868 Jan 4 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 177]

>  The Maori... having pondered over and searched until he had created those Scriptures to his own destruction, finally settled into Hauhauism. <i>(Jan 25, 1933,NZ Tablet,15</i>) [NOTES: Respecting Hauhauism, he said that there were three things that give rise to it: ... Ibid. 193 Hauhuaism is fast dying out. 1868 Jan 4 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 177]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the worst of them [Maoris] thought there could be no real good in our faith, when its professors could thus pay so little regard to its commands; they therefore invented another, and Hauhauism arose. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> From the successes achieved in Wairoa in 1865 ... may be dated the first break-down of Hau-hauism. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 572]

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> ... i found that the meeting was to be held, and that a large party of Hauhaus was present with the King's great teacher of his new form of Hauhauism, a man named Hanauru - `The boisterous Westerly wind.' <i>(Aug 1877,Grace Pioneer Missionary,262,1928 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Aug T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 262]

>  <b>GRACE</b> ... the national movement under Wiremu Tamihana, the King-maker, and the wave of religious enthusiasm called [149] `Hau-hauism' which followed it. <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,148</i>)

>  <i>(1908,Speeches & Documents,176,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PiNFOLD</b> Hauhauism may be regarded as the aftermath of the Maori War. <i>(1930,Fifty Years in Maoriland,102</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [1865] was the end of Hauhauism in these parts; for, though the name `Hauhau' has been in use ever since to denote those who have been disaffected towards the Government or towards Christianity the peculiar quasi-religious practices prescribed by Patara were heard no more of. <i>(1932,East Coast Historical Records,48</i>)

>  <b>BABBAGE</b> Hauhauism was the result of several different features, which became welded in 1863 in the form of a fanatical religion. ... The progess of Hauhauism corresponded largely with the progess of the Maori King Movement; and with the defeat of the Kingite tribes the religion became a ... negligible force. <i>(1937,Hauhauism,9</i>)

>  <b>H. WiLLiAMS</b> [introd] in this new war [in 1860s] the spirit of nationalism expressed itself in a revival of pagan religion, this time under the name of Pai Marire, generallly known as Hauhauism. <i>(1961,Journal,25,1961(ed) Rogers ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> ... he averted a battle which would have cost the lives of many Ngati Porou converts to Hauhauism. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/235</i>)

>  Hau-Hauism n. 19th-c. Maori religion founded by Te Ua Haumene, promising eternal salvation from the pakeha. [M incantation rire rire hau hau (peace, peace, strike, strike) or pai marire blessed peace) hau hau] <i>(1966,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,344</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 226]]

>  <b>BELiCH</b> On this basis, some of the campaigns of 1864-8 can be seen simply as a product of the new anti-Pakeha fanaticism. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,204</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> The neighbouring <i>pa</i>, Utapu by name, was a hotbed of <i>hauhauism</i>. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,258</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Hauhauism was a cult which combined aspects of Biblical teaching with the traditional culture and priest-craft of the Maori. <i>(1983,illustrated History of Taranaki,47</i>)

>  The only points at which the Ringatu doctrine resembles the Hauhauism or Pai Mairire is in the name Ringatu - "The Uplifted Hand." <i>(Apr 20, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

### haui
 
 <i>n.</i> ?A misprint for (?misconceived) hani; or a taiaha

>  <b>ANGAS</b> in battle, each chief always carried a staff of very hard wood with a carved head, the sharp point of which, designed to resemble a human tongue thrust out in defiance, was urged forward as a mark of insult to the enemy; ... This staff, called `e haui,' was also carried in the cirle of debate; ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,147</i>)

### haul
 
 <i>v.</i> always so used rather than drag etc in logging

>  When the natural supply of water is not sufficient to float these [logs] they are forced along by driving-dams or hauled out of the bush, either by bullocks or steam-engines. <i>(1904,TrNZi,36/464</i>)

### haul in
 
 <i>v.</i> to arrive unexpectedly; to blow in. -->ca1949 He hauled in for a feed as he was passing through Havelock.

### hauler
 
 <i>n.</i> [(forestry) Cf also log-hauler, timber-hauler, =donkey.]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> SNiGGiNG-TRACKS. ... Good straight tracks make hauling easy. A few pieces of corduroy placed across the track are a great help ... A good snigger will see that a log is not jammed before signalling `Right ahead' to the hauler, and will use his snigging-shoe ... to lift the log out of bad places. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,13</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> it has however been a painful decision, and as i stand today among the litter of logs waiting for the hauler to take them to the mill i wonder if my conduct has been civilized. <i>(Aug 1, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,42,1961 ed.</i>)

>  HAULER A portable engine equipped with drum and cable, and powered by steam or internal combustion. syn DONKEY (A) - <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> (facing) [a stationary log-hauler] [caption] Halliday's Hauler at Waitane <i>(1966,in the Lee of the Hokonuis,45</i>)

>  With the cost of a hauler running close to a millon dollars, and much of the other heavy gear costing around a quarter of that per machine, it is almost impossible for a younger logger to set up on his own [108] without this sort of support. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/107</i>)

### hauling-ground
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At the same date all the young stock ... went to still more exposed places by themselves, and spent the moulting-season, ... when, having acquired the new fur coat, they proceeded to sea. The last of these `hauling-grounds,' as they are called, i have known in New Zealand was at Cape Foulwind, but formerly they were all round the coast. <i>(1893,TrNZi,25/258</i>)

### haumakaroa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[anax] simplex, Forst. ... Haumakaroa. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,229</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The other associated trees and shrubs [of the Auckland islands] are the haumakaroa (<i>Nothopanax simplex</i>), ... the inaka (<i>Dracophyllum longifolium</i>) .... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,116</i>)

>  <b>WODZiCKi</b> Miss Mason identified the leaves of the following plants [Egmont National Park]: ...both leaves and fruit or seed of haumakaroa (<i>Nothopanax simplex</i>), ...raurekau (<i>Coprosma grandifolia</i>), ...and broadleaf (<i>Griselinia littoralis</i>), ... <i>(1950,introduced Mammals of NZ,164</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> 5. Haumakaroa (<i>N[othopanax] simplex</i>) as for 4 but leaflets serrated and smaller. <i>ibid.</i> 165 7. Haumakaroa (<i>Nothopanax simplex</i>) ... usually subalpine. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,159,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Pseudopanax simplex</i> HAUMAKOOROA ... Small tree, sometimes a shrub. ... (=<i>Neopanax simplex</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,82</i>)

### haumakoroa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>31</b> 768. Lithograph of haumakoroa foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers</i>)

### haumata
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Carex</i> spp., Maorihead, niggerhead, red tussock; <i>C. rubra</i>, snowgrass.

>  <i>Haumata</i> was the name of what are now called Maori-heads... <i>(1921,TrNZi,52/72</i>) [NOTES: (Beattie)]

>  <i>haumata [=] red tussock [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)

>  <b>GiBBS</b> i have found larvae on <i>Chionochloa rubra</i> the large `red tussock', or haumata so it is very likely that it utilises other species of snow tussock as well. <i>(1980,NZ Butterflies,91</i>)

### Haumurian
 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology)

>  The Haumurian is now suggested to equate with all late Santonian, Campanian and Maastrictian time, some 18.5 Ma. <i>(Jul 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,113/88</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Geology) . [rock classification]

>  The Cretaceous-Tertiary boundary in New Zealand is generally placed at the boundary between the Teurian and Haumurian Stages (Hornibrook 1962). <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/13</i>)

>  There is a strong suggestion of a relationship to South American in the Haumurian (Maestrichtian). <i>(Nov 1959,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,7/20</i>)

>  G. Warren: The Haumurian and Piripauan type sections, Haumuri gulf. <i>(Jul 1967,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,23/3</i>)

### haunga
 
 <i>n.</i> [?dateline `Parengo. You stink Parengo! Who told you to put your haunga seaweed in my drier?'; see also short story by Patricia Grace 'The Dream' 1975]

### hauora
 
 <i>n.</i> total wellbeing [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Exhibitions of Ta Moko (body art), Hauora (health in massage), Taonga Puoro (Maori music), Raranga (weaving), Korero Purakau (storytelling). <i>(Sep 27, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  We are talking about health from a concept of hauora [total well being]. the notion of hauora is stronger than just health. it's about spiritual, mental, physical and emotional wellbeing.' <i>(Dec 22, 2005,Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori health body contracted to the Government [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Ever since the hauora took over tiny Te Puia Springs Hospital in 1999 and saved it from closure by the government, it has been a critical part of East Coast life. From the birthing facility to palliative care for the elderly, the hauora provides cradle to grave healthcare and employs 160 people. <i>(Jan 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

### Hauraki Declaration
 
 <i>n.</i> a statement from a national Maori hui in 2003 regarding the foreshore and seabed

>  Some Maori simply argue that the foreshore and seabed are owned by Maori with the corollary that they can do what they like with it: the Hauraki Declaration resulting from the Paeroa hui in July includes both propositions. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### haurangi
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... then i would give the Natives, a dram of Rum to wash it down. it used to be called Why Pirah [waipiro] or Stinking Water. Now they call it Why Pie [pai] or good Water. They like now to get Showrangy [haurangi] or drunk if they can. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),45,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> in his absence [at the public house] the child died, and the stigma of the offence clung to him in `Haurangi' (drunk). <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,97</i>)

>  We had been drinking that night. Those stupid haurangi bastards, Josh and Hone too. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,126</i>)

### Hautawan
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Geology) . [rock classification]

>  Lower Pleistocene deposits (Hautawan to Nukumaruan) show cyclic vertical changes in depth of deposition which are attributed to glacio-eustatic oscillations of sea level. <i>(Jun 1961,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,10/3</i>) [NOTES: rock classification]

>  The implication for the Hautawan stage (pre-<i>Gephyrocapsa sinuosa</i> according to Edwards 1976) are uncertain since we do not yet know enough about its planktic micro-fauna at the type locality, but the indication of a longer survival of the <i>Chlamys</i> in Wairarapa and the southern Dannevirke district suggest that the stage, if recognised by the presence of <i>C. delicatula</i>, is not a reliable correlation unit. <i>(Nov 1978,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,46/4</i>)

>  Hautawan and Nukumaruan palynofloras may be dated given a rich palynoflora. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/51</i>)

### hauture
 
 <i>n.</i> Ma. hauture W.42 Hauture, n. <i>Trachurus novae-zelandiae</i>, scad, horse-mackeel; =haature, =horse mackerel, see also h&amacr.ture.

>  The horse mackerel (Trachurus trachurus) or hauture of the Maori, is a fish which has a very wide range, being common to the seas of Britain and New Zealand. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Daily Times,18</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> HORSE MACKEREL ... This is the Hauture of the natives, and the Scad of the northern hemisphere (<i>Trachurus trachurus</i>). <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,110</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> HAUTURE (see HORSE MACKEREL). <i>ibid.</i> 46 HORSE MACKEREL (<i>Trachurus trachurus</i>). ... This is the hauture of the Natives, and the scad of the Northern Hemisphere. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,45</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HORSE MACKEREL ... <i>Other names: Trachurus novaezelandiae</i>; mackerel; hauture (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,232</i>)

### have
 
 <i>n.</i> a surprise

>  He had placed the parcel there to give somebody a "have". <i>(Nov 11, 1897,Otago Witness,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> have a beer in, have a fly, have a look-in, rat (in one's garret), have a set on, have a spite on, have for breakfast, have had it in a big way, have one's photograph taken, have on toast, have the goods on, have tickets on, have whacked; see beer, fly, look-in, rat, set, spite, breakfast, had it, photograph, toast, goods, ticket, whacked.

### have a baby
 
 <i>phr.</i> be angry; cf. have kittens

>  [Hdg] Tua 'has baby' over car-park prang.... The number plate on Tua's new purple Lotus was knocked off when the cars collided in the car park of a central Auckland gym at lunchtime. "He had a baby about it," Ms Evans said. <i>(Aug 30, 2000,Dominion,3</i>)

### have a beer in
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be tipsy or drunk.

>  The well-dressed blackguard had a beer in and commenced patting the shoulder of the pretty girl beside him in the car on Courtenay Place section. <i>(Dec 7, 1907,NZ Truth,129/6/3</i>)

### have a cow
 
 <i>phr.</i> as have a baby q.v.

>  "if my mum finds out about this," says Vanessa, "she'll have a cow." <i>(Feb 17, 1999,Evening Post,22</i>)

### have a cramp in one's kick
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be short of money (in the pocket) (See [kick](kee-kiw.md#kick))

>  <b>WHiTWORTH</b> i was down there with Old Ashmore's lot, doing the trampoline, and so on; but things was very quizby. Everything went crooked somehow, and the gorger had a hawful cramp in his kick. Well, ramp was the word, and vamose we did, the lot on us; some here, some there. i was reg'lar beached, and i tell you when a cove 'as to spout his smish it's about time to look out. However, i got a job at a livery stable, but although that kep' me in grub, and i slep' in a spare stall, the billet was very multy, and i kep' on the kivvy for something better. [fn] 1. [trampoline] Vaulter. 2. [quizby] Bad. 3. [crooked] Wrong. 4. [cramp in his kick] Emptiness in his pockets. 5. [ramp] Clear out. 6. [vamose] Go. 7. [beached] Hard up. 8. [smish] Shirt. 9. [multy] No good. 10. [kivvy] Look out (qui vive.) <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,5</i>)

### have a death adder in one's pocket
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be very mean or slow at paying one's share

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> There is all sorts of plonk, and the little joker tells me to touch my kick [to shout]. ... i think [all the others] have death adders in their pockets, 'cause not one of them touches his tweeds. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,75</i>)

### have a dog tied up
 
 <i>phr.</i> [(alcoholics')]

>  if they [sc. vagrant alcoholics] have a dog tied up somewhere, that means they owe someone in the place money. When they go to bed fully dressed that's `marching orders' and their `dawn parade' will be held at an early opener. <i>(Feb 14, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,19</i>)

### have a good grip on Mother Earth
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to be timid of horses and horse-riding.

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> He did have a good grip on Mother Earth. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,53</i>)

### have a good thing on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be on to a good thing; to be in a comfortable or easy position; to have a good lurk.

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Meri knew when she had a good thing on and refused to stir out of bed. <i>(1937,Where the Apple Reddens,37</i>)

### have a Maori
 
 <i>phr.</i> to pay for oneself [ca1953 Feb 19 (Wellington Hotel) ... to have a Maori, to pay for one's own beer in a group or school.]

### have a piece of
 
 <i>phr.</i> to reprimand, scold

>  <b>GiLDERDALE</b> <i>have a piece of </i> Reprimand. J. Hornby's <i>Mystery in Maoriland</i> (1958). <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>)

### have a pop at
 
 <i>phr.</i> have a go at, engage in a fight

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>pop</i>, phr. <i>have a pop at</i> a fight; NZA. <i>- to go off pop</i> to start speaking angrily. <i>- not a fair pop</i> not a fair chance; eg `i'd like to have a pop at the little basket, but i apologise for going off pop at you. it's not a fair pop you having to be involved.' c. 1925. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,87</i>)

### have a rat
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be eccentric, cracked; to have an obsession; (occas.) to become angry (cf ratty aj `angry'; cf RAT-HOUSE `asylum') [ca1935 Havelock -->P: (B 1942) Aust: 20c.]

>  ... the clever chap who wrote the epistle would have no hesitation in speaking to this effect: `Dicken! i done me block right enough! i come home and the old finger hit the roof. Tike it from me, he's gotter rat.' <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,15</i>)

### have a rat in one's garret
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  He'd act that strange, he felt so bad, / That someone said he'd got a rat ... <i>(Aug 8, 1899,Bush Advocate,2</i>)

>  His Worship: `Was the accused sober?' Witness: `Well, he looked muzzy.' His Worship: `What do you mean by that?' Witness: `He looked either drunk or a shingle short.' His Worship: `A what?' Witness: `A screw loose.' His Worship: `i don't understand you.' Witness: `He seemed to have a rat in his garret.' His Worship: `Oh, speak English!' Witness: `Off his dot - balmy [sic] - dilly.' Sergeant: `He says the man was mad, your Worship.' Witness: `No, i didn't. i said he was either drunk or ratty.' <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/1/6</i>)

>  Black Sir John Findlay - Mr Findlay - has got a spite on me over the Parnell election. Sir John Not at all, Mr Black. You are here to get money out of my clients and it is my duty to protect them. <i>(Feb 15, 1915,NZ Truth,399/5/5</i>)

 <i>phr.</i>

### have down to a fine art
 
 <i>phr.</i> be very expert at (often something almost illegal or shady). [(spec., sense 1887 Victoria) ca1950 frequently used by undergraduates and others; possibly introduced by servicemen]

>  <b>BRUNO</b> He had [it] down to a fine art. <i>(1948,Maleesh George,5</i>)

### have for breakfast
 
 <i>phr.</i> [see ODE, no label]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Have for breakfast</i>. occ. <i>before breakfast</i> (as a rare appetiser). A humorous way of implying that a thing is easy to do, (gen.) a man easy to beat. E.g. `Why! i have one like him every day before breakfast' or `i could have <i>or</i> done with six like him for breakfast.' For task or feat, the <i>before breakfast,</i> often with <i>do</i> or <i>have</i>, is preferred. C. 20 coll.: mostly Australia and New Zealand. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,379</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>breakfast</i> in phr. <i>have for breakfast</i> find some person or thing easy to overcome or achieve; eg `Just let him try anything. i'll have him for breakfast.' NZA mostly. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> have a pee

 <i>phr.</i> to be a crawler

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> -->To seek the favour of the boss at the cost of one's self-respect. .in 4;`He never supports us if we have an argument with the boss. He's not liked among us, because he's got legs on his belly, that's why.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],485</i>)

### have on
 
 <i>v.</i> [ca1936 chiefly children's]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1946,That Summer</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> to fight; attack with words, fists etc possibly merely v1 <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon</i>)

>  `You get a lot of people who have you on in the street - sidewalk lawyers, we call them,' Pollock says. <i>(Apr 7, 1965,Dominion,7</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> have on: PUT ME ON - an attempt to bamboozle -->to argue with; to dispute with - <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,51</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> have on to challenge or attack; WWii; eg `Have him on about it, he'll back down for sure.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,55</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (with negative) to want nothing to do with

>  <b>M.TALBOT</b> i'm not always understood when i say `i'm having you on!' meaning `i'm pulling your leg'. Now i find that `i'm not having that on' means `i'm having no truck with that.' <i>(Dec 2, 1957,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (Tauranga)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> take up a sexual opportunity with.

>  <b>SARGESON</b> have: have (a woman) on, take up a sexual opportunity with. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

### have on the block
 
 <i>phr.</i> have on the chopping block? [rugby commentary: Wellington had them on the block.]

### have one's ear chewed
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be reprimanded, instructed, etc. [ca1935 I got my ear chewed for coming in late]

### have one's wild up
 
 <i>phr.</i> be very angry [Jim writes: In phrase <i>have one's wild up</i>. To be aroused esp to anger. This is not in any dictionary I have seen. It sounds Irish in origin but is familiar to me (though not personally used).]

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> And she's had her wild up with me ever since i burnt a hole in her bed. <i>(1974,Whanau,112</i>)

### have shit for brains
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be very foolish or stupid [ca1950 Auckland schoolboys' (V.O'Sullivan)]

### have the flags out
 
 <i>phr.</i> be menstruating [I remember hearing "the red flag's up" in the 1940s in the same context]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> flags, phr. have the flags out menstrual period; popularised by Barry Humphries' appalling Ocker persona Bazza McKenzie bewailing this barrier to his chances of scoring. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### have the goods on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have abundant evidence for the conviction of a person. [Cf fit v., have the wood on.]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> have the goods on. To have abundant evidence for the conviction of (a person): N.Z. c[ant]. (-1932). [but cf P8 (p.537) adds: ... by 1935 also Aus., and by 1940 Standard. B1943] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,380</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The origin [of to have the wood on a person] is obscure, but it is probably formed on the phrase - also indigenous ... - to have the goods on, to have sufficient evidence against (a person) to secure a conviction ... an interesting example [of rhyming slang] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

### have the numbers
 
 <i>phr.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: In phrase <i>to have the numbers</i>. Probably Australian in origin (Macquarie 1981). Used in NZ eg Bolger <i>A View from the Top</i>. It is not in NSOD or Collins and the definitions there do not fit this usage.]

### have the wood on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have the/an advantage over, to have the upper hand of: possibly f. tree-falling or axemen's contests, where one wins because one is a chip or thickness of wood ahead of an opponent who loses because he still has a piece of wood to sever before his trunk or block falls: but cf. ADD, and <i>have the googs on</i> [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 9 WOOD,GOT THE WOOD ON]

>  <b>KiRK</b> i hold the `wood' on those who work. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,7</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Among the terms which are of considerable use to us in our daily life, for instance, is <i>to have the wood on a person,</i> to have an advantage over someone. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Then we've taken another hiding. And [check punct.] i thought we had the wood on Jerry today. <i>(1944,Troop Target,168</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Wood on, have the. </i>`To have an advantage over someone' B., 1941): New Zealand: since ca. 1920. Either rhyming <i>have the goods on </i>or ex the game of bowls. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1225</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> wood: <i>had got the wood on</i>, had the advantage over. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,304</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Some expressions that have been claimed for New Zealand are <i>at the rate of knots</i> `very fast', <i>hook your mutton</i> `clear out', <i>have the wood on </i>`have an advantage over' and the oaths <i>ey Charlie and (By) Christchurch </i>(obviously a neat save suggested by the place name). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,177</i>)

>  <b>MORRiESON</b> Got the wood on Jamieson. He's coming across with the section. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,118</i>)

>  <i> wood ... have the wood on NZ & Austral. sl. </i>have an advantage over. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,887</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 550]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>wood</i> have the advantage, often to <i>have the wood on</i>; as in woodchopping contests, at least from 1941; eg `Long as his first serve is not working, you've got the wood on him.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,124</i>)

>  The chestnut is pretty slippery off the mark and will have the wood on a lot of his opponents. <i>(Jan 30, 1915,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  in other words, South Westland, because it had white pine which the Government must get out, had "the wood on the Government." <i>(Feb 10, 1937,Evening Post,20</i>)

### have tickets on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be keen on (a person); espec. to set one's cap at a man or woman to have a special interest in, to be courting. -->(from a fig. use of ? to put one's personal label or ticket on (to claim possession of or to warn others off); or from ? having a raffle ticket on with hope of winning [but raffle tickets are usually in not on a raffle]. [1953 R. Mason `to think highly of'; cf. the recent Associated Chambers of Commerce advertisement: `Vera had tickets on ...']

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> i don't know whether she's got any tickets on me. <i>(1908,Anchorage,140</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> i don't have many tickets on wowsers. <i>(1916,Wowser, The</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `She's a nice girl, that Sister Collins.' `You must have tickets on her, Starkie.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,179</i>)

>  <b>CHERRiLL</b> But although you had tickets and no mistake on Ju at the <i>time</i>, you didn't forget to put the acid on him proper afterwards. ... [139] `She had tickets on Ju once'. `Had what?' The term was new to her [as a recently arrived English wife]. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,99</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>got tickets on</i>: i've got tickets on that bird (car, job. etc.) Means, i've GOT MY EYE ON (i desire) that... <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,48</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `Got tickets on you, has he? Pity, he's too old for you.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,135</i>)

### have tickets on oneself
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have a high regard for oneself, to be full or too fond of oneself. [ca 1941 at least]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>tickets on oneself, have</i> to be conceited; eg `That stuck-up bastard, he's got tickets on himself.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,113</i>)

### Havelock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The jovial Les Mills suggested a stick of Havelock. <i>(1957,Mangamaire School,12</i>)

### Havvers
 
 <i>n.</i> Havelock North

>  if you ever meet someone from that area [sc. Hastings], then you'll note they actually don't come from Hastings, they come from "Havvers". <i>(May 4, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

### Haw haw fellow
 
 <i>n.</i> an Englishman with a plummy accent

>  *Yah yah.-An Englishman with an exaggerated accent. i have heard them spoken of as Haw haw fellows. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

### hawai
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HECTOR</b> it may be useful to give a list of the different fishes the Maoris distinguish in the Taupo district, ... Hawai. - A black Kokopu; probably the Bullhead (<i>Eleotris</i>), which is a common inhabitant in all streams in New Zealand. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,129</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> <i>Eleotris gobioides</i> C. and V. Bull-head, or Hawai <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,302</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> Many types of fungus were eaten by the Maori. These were known by various names such as hawai, wairuru, tiki-tehetehe, maiheru, tawaka (a species of Agaricus) and hakeka (Himeola). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)

### Hawaiki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> All over New Zealand, the natives state that their ancestors migrated to the country from a place called Hawaiki . <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),204</i>)

>  They decided to make the attempt to return across Kiwa's dark sea (the Pacific) to the mythical home land, Hawaiki. <i>(Feb 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

### Hawaikian
 
 <i>adj.</i> of Hawaiki. [(rare)]

>  <b>SHAND</b> How is it, that, while showing so many points [51] of agreement in their Hawaikian ancestry, there are so many generations between the dates of arrival in New Zealand and the Chatham islands. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,50</i>) [NOTES: (rare)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Pekehawani, an ancient Hawaikian name, was here used by Potatau as an honorific for the Puniu River, the boundary between the Waikato and the territory of the Ngati-Maniapoto. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/185</i>)

>  <b>AHSTON-WARNER</b> He can recite the list of his chieftain ancestors ... Several lists, in fact, for there were collateral tables of names down through the generations from the far-back Hawaikian days. <i>(1967,Greenstone,50</i>)

### Hawera Republic
 
 <i>n.</i> [also as South Taranaki Republic]

>  The Hawera republic is determined to allow no stain of Te Whiti's power to remain within their borders. <i>(Jun 28, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>) [NOTES: also as South Taranaki Republic]

>  There's a more or less local precedent.. the South Taranaki Republic of 1879, an early example of Pakeha backlash to Maori claims. <i>(Nov 20, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### Haweran
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Geology) . [Geological classification]

>  The five oldest terraces are assigned a Castlecliffian age, the younger terraces are of Haweran Age. <i>(Aug 1990,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,89/77</i>) [NOTES: Geological classification]

### hawk
 
 <i>n.</i> Usu. <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i>, occas. <i>Circus approximans</i>. See falcon, harrier; also kahu, karearea. See also fish-hawk (Caspian tern); sea-hawk (southern skua). [Spec. and strictly erroneous use of hawk--> a diurnal bird of prey of the sub fam. Accipitrinae to various similar NZ birds.

>  <b>BAYLY</b> i saw two kinds of small Hawkes, both being small. <i>(Apr 12, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/206]

>  ... the Land Fowl/ are Hawks, Parrots, Pigeons and such other birds as are common to this country. <i>(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/316,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Sometimes, indeed, the mako-mako [fn <i>Philedon Dumerilii</i>] pours forth ... its melodious notes; or perhaps a hawk [fn <i>Falco brunnea</i>] is seen watching the movements of a smaller bird; or the fan-tail flycatcher busy in the lower branches. [fn]1 A Bush Hawk or Karearea, <i>Falco novae seelandiae</i> Gm. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/77</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> .. one of the wekas brought on board ... Mr.Phillips' party bagged 20 wild duck - two wood hens and a small hawk ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,391</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> .. one of the wekas brought on board ... Mr.Phillips' party bagged 20 wild duck - two wood hens and a small hawk ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,391</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Their greatest enemies are the hawks ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,147,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Circus Gouldi. Hawk. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Hawk ..... Falconidae. The most common species appears to be the Cercus [sic] Gouldi: or New Zealand Harrier. - Buller. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/305</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> [The Harrier] is commonly referred to as the hawk, but our true hawk or falcon is another species, <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i>, which frequents the forested high country.... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,82</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> HARRiER <i>Circus approximans Other names</i>: Hawk, Swamp Harrier, Gould's Harrier, Kahu. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,98</i>)

>  .. back on the highway, roadkill attracts the native New Zealand Hawk. <i>(Dec 12, 2004,Sunday Star Times,p12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i> (See [New Zealand falcon](new-no.md#new-zealand-falcon))

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>ibid.</i> 1/222 The continuous screaming of the Bush-Hawk is understood by the natives to be a sure indication of change. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> Bush hawks (Falco ferox) ... in the gorge of the Lawrence. <i>(1874,On Recent Changes in the Fauna,8</i>)

>  The Bush-Hawk is generally met with on the outskirts of the woods. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2</i>)

>  QUAiL-HAWK (<i>Nesierax novae-zealandiae</i>); BUSH-HAWK (<i>Nesierax australis</i>). - At one time both varieties of this hawk were fairly common in all the hill country, but only on rare occasions were they met with on the plains. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/188</i>) [NOTES: (Hope)]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kaeaea ... Falco novaeseelandiae, bush hawk. = kaiaia, karearea. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,81</i>)

>  <i>bush hawk n</i> : a small falcon (<i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i>) of New Zealand that resembles the kestrel <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,302</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Bush robins, parakeets, tomtits, grey warblers, riflemen, pigeons and the bush hawk are still to be seen. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,129</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Bush hawk, falcon .. .. karewarewa .. .. <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND FALCON <i>Falco novaeseelandiae Other names</i>: Bush Hawk, Sparrow Hawk, Quail Hawk, Karearea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  bush hawk n. ... n. predatory N.Z. bird. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  New Zealand Falcon <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i> GMELiN, 1788 ...[155] OTHER NAMES: <i>Karearea, sparrowhawk, bush hawk, quail hawk, eastern falcon, southern falcon, bush falcon. ... Bush falcon: in the North island confined to the main ranges, ... Eastern falcon: The main ranges of the South island ... Southern Falcon: Fiordland, the Auckland islands, and Stewart island. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,154</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Falco novaeseelandiae</i> (See [New Zealand falcon](new-no.md#new-zealand-falcon))

>  ... shot 3 wekas and 13 rabbits and one quail hawk - <i>(Jul 10, 1867,Journal,108</i>) [NOTES: 1867 July 10 Phillips Point Journal [TS] 108]

>  FALCO NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Gml. Ka rewa rewa-tara. Quail-hawk. in New Zealand, ... the head of the family [Raptores] must be fairly assigned to this bird, which is commonly known by the name of the Quail or Sparrow-hawk ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/51</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 1. HiERACiDEA NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. Lath. Quail Hawk. Sparrow Hawk. Kaiaia. <i>H. Brunnea </i>Gould. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,1</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Concerning the specific distinctiveness of the Sparrow-hawk and the Quail-Hawk, ... i got two of the former (<i>i.e.</i> the small species). ... Both birds were <i>full</i>-grown, and showed ... the impossibility of their over developing into a large and perfectly Quail-Hawk. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,xv</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Mar 10 Haast in 1873 Buller Birds of NZ xv]

>  <b>POTTS</b> `in New Zealand the courageous family of the <i>Raptoresis</i> very feebly represented; the honourable post of head of the family in all fairness must be assigned to the falcon, which is commonly known by the name of the quail- or sparrow-hawk, not that it is identical with, or that it even bears much resemblance to, the bold robber of the woods of Great Britain - `the hardy sperhauke eke the quales foe,' as Chaucer has it.' <i>(1882,Out in the Open,37</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Kaiaia (<i>Hieracidea Brunnea</i>) is a sparrow-hawk, smaller than the Kahu. ... By the same native name the `quail-hawk' (<i>Hieracidea N.Z</i>.) is also known. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/221</i>)

>  No Kestrel is found in New Zealand, but an approach to the form is made by the very peculiar <i>Hieracidea (</i>or <i>Harpe) novae-zelandiae</i> ... the `Sparrow-Hawk', `Quail-Hawk', and `Bush-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1882,Encyclopedia Britannica,XiV/54</i>)

>  Of the three species recognized, two, the quail-hawk (<i>Harpae Novae Zealandiae</i>) and the bush-hawk (<i>H. ferox</i>) [or sparrow-hawk], belong to a genus peculiar to New Zealand. [The third is the New Zealand harrier, <i>Circus Gouldi</i>, also found in Australia.] <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>) [NOTES: Prof. Parker [Morris]]

>  <i>Nesierax novaehollandiae</i> Gm. Quail-hawk. Of our debatable two species of falcons, the larger one, of which we now treat, is undoubtedly the rarer. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/84</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  QUAiL-HAWK (<i>Nesierax novae-zealandiae</i>); BUSH-HAWK (<i>Nesierax australis</i>). - At one time both varieties of this hawk were fairly common in all the hill country, but only on rare occasions were they met with on the plains. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/188</i>) [NOTES: (Hope)]

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> We saw a quail-hawk (<i>Hieracidea novaezelandiae</i>) dart down on a full-grown young tui ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,270,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <i>quail-hawk ... n.</i> N.Z. falcon or sparrow-hawk. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND FALCON <i>Falco novaeseelandiae Other names</i>: Bush Hawk, Sparrow Hawk, Quail Hawk, Karearea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Kaiaia [Karearea] is the Sparrow-Hawk quite as troublesome and more important than the Common Hawk. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> As we went we heard a karewarewa (the sparrow hawk) screaming, the natives immediately exclaimed, tomorrow will be a rainy day repeating the old saying Ka tangi te karewarewa ki waenga o te rangi pai ka ua apopo [etc.] <i>(Apr 18, 1847,Journal,5/33</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The game list is ... 1 grey duck, 2 dab-chicks, 2 sparrow-hawks and 3 eels. <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Expedition Middle isd. in 1959 Taylor Early Trav. 308]

>  Shot a female sparrow-hawk which J.Deans skinned. <i>(May 25, 1849,Torlesse Papers,80,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Of the <i>Falcon</i> family, there are two species in New Zealand; one, called Kahu, is about the size of a pigeon, and the other, named Karewarewa, is a sparrow-hawk <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/23</i>)

>  Hieracidea Novae Zelandiae. Sparrow-Hawk <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier).]

>  FALCO NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Gml. Ka rewa rewa-tara. Quail-hawk. in New Zealand, ... the head of the family [Raptores] must be fairly assigned to this bird, which is commonly known by the name of the Quail or Sparrow-hawk ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/51</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Concerning the specific distinctiveness of the Sparrow-hawk and the Quail-Hawk, ... i got two of the former (<i>i.e.</i> the small species). ... Both birds were <i>full</i>-grown, and showed ... the impossibility of their over developing into a large and perfectly Quail-Hawk. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,xv</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Mar 10 Haast in 1873 Buller Birds of NZ xv]

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... Native names. Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> HiERACiDEA BRUNNEA \{HARPA FEROX\}. (BUSH-HAWK.) ... Native names. Karearea, Kaiaia, Kaeaea, Kakarapiti, Karewarewa, and Tawaka; but sometimes distinguished as Karewarewa-tara. `Sparrow-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,6,{1888 2nd edn 1/220} ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> fn Captain Mair, writing to me in February 1880, says: -`The Sparrow-Hawks still build at the cliff on the Waitangi Stream where we obtained our young birds in 1867.' <i>(1880,Birds of New Zealand,1/223</i>) [NOTES: 1880 Feb Capt. Mair in 1888 Buller Birds of NZ1/223]

>  On looking up, i found it was one of these courageous little sparrow-hawks [Falco brunnea] which had attacked me ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,137</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kaarearea, n. hieracidea Novae Zelandiae; sparrow-hawk. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,46,(3rd edn) ed.</i>)

>  No Kestrel is found in New Zealand, but an approach to the form is made by the very peculiar <i>Hieracidea (</i>or <i>Harpe) novae-zelandiae</i> ... the `Sparrow-Hawk', `Quail-Hawk', and `Bush-Hawk' of the colonists. <i>(1882,Encyclopedia Britannica,XiV/54</i>)

>  Hierachidea brunnea (Bush-hawk). <i>(1888,TrNZi,210</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kaiaia, n. sparrow-hawk; hieracidea Novae-Zealandiae.</i> <i>ibid.</i> 52 <i>Karearea, ... hieracidea Novae-Zealandiae; sparrow-hawk. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,47</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 1. HiERACiDEA NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. Lath. Quail Hawk. Sparrow Hawk. Kaiaia. <i>H. Brunnea</i> Gould. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,1</i>)

>  At the top of the hill we were attacked by sea-hawks or skua-gulls (<i>Stercorarius antarcticus</i>) ... They are called skua-gulls from their resemblance to a northern species (<i>Stercorarius catarractes</i>), and appear to be rare in New Zealand proper. <i>(1891,TrNZi,23/495</i>)

>  <i>Kaiaia, n. sparrow-hawk; hieracidea Novae-Zealandiae.</i> <i>ibid.</i> 52 <i>Karearea, ... hieracidea Novae-Zealandiae; sparrow-hawk. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,47</i>)

>  <i>Nesierax pottsi</i> Mathews and iredale. Bush-hawk; Sparrow-hawk. Nowhere very common, this fierce little falcon is very generally distributed in both islands. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/84</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> i never saw the native quail, the cuckoo nor the morepork in the Central [Otago, c1860s], but i saw the sparrow-hawk, the rock wren, and plenty of hawks and pihoihoi (ground larks). <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> So you unfeathered animals ... call me `Sparrowhawk'. 'Tis easy to call names, but i am a falcon, fierce, proud and free. <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,41</i>)

>  <b>GiLLESPiE</b> Gould's harrier hawk is frequently seen, but the sparrow hawk is rare. <i>(1958,South Canterbury,9,1971 ed.</i>)

>  A harrier patrols its beat several times a day all the year round. A sparrow hawk darts over us occasionally... <i>(1962,Landfall Country,328</i>) [NOTES: (Kidson `Annus Mirabilis')]

>  <b>MACNiCOL</b> ...the hen-house was likely to be raided by sparrow-hawks in the early morning. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,120</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND FALCON <i>Falco novaeseelandiae Other names</i>: Bush Hawk, Sparrow Hawk, Quail Hawk, Karearea. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The spot on Sugarloaf where weird old Jimmy Gilfillan had his hut and spent all his time shooting every sparrowhawk (New Zealand falcon) he could find, declaring: `They fly down and pick your eyes out.' <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,122</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Falcon Falco novaeseelandiae</i> GMELiN, 1788 ...[155] OTHER NAMES: <i>Karearea, sparrowhawk, bush hawk, quail hawk, eastern falcon, southern falcon, bush falcon. ... Bush falcon: in the North island confined to the main ranges, ... Eastern falcon: The main ranges of the South island ... Southern Falcon: Fiordland, the Auckland islands, and Stewart island.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,154</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Kaiaia [Karearea] is the Sparrow-Hawk quite as troublesome and more important than the Common Hawk. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,113,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> As we went we heard a karewarewa (the sparrow hawk) screaming, the natives immediately exclaimed, tomorrow will be a rainy day repeating the old saying Ka tangi te karewarewa ki waenga o te rangi pai ka ua apopo [etc.] <i>(Apr 18, 1847,Journal,5/33</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> The game list is ... 1 grey duck, 2 dab-chicks, 2 sparrow-hawks and 3 eels. <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition,308</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Expedition Middle isd. in 1959 Taylor Early Trav.308]

>  Shot a female sparrow-hawk which J.Deans skinned. <i>(May 25, 1849,Torlesse Papers,80,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Of the <i>Falcon</i> family, there are two species in New Zealand; one, called Kahu, is about the size of a pigeon, and the other, named Karewarewa, is a sparrow-hawk <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/23</i>)

>  Hieracidea Novae Zelandiae. Sparrow-Hawk <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier).]

>  FALCO NOVAE ZELANDiAE, Gml. Ka rewa rewa-tara.Quail-hawk. in New Zealand, .. the head of the family [Raptores] must be fairly assigned to this bird, which is commonly known by the name of the Quail or Sparrow-hawk ... <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/51</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

### hawkweed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Having been settled and stocked with sheep since the mid-1800s, much of the land between the Awatere Valley and Hanmer had been devastated by intensive grazing, rabbits and burn-offs, which exacerbated erosion, and encourage the spread of weeds such as sweet briar, hawkweed and broom, all of which remained a problem. <i>(May 25, 2004,Dominion Post,A10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. to NZ rural economy] 1. Crepis capillaris, a weed commonly found in various parts of the world. 2. one of the Hieracium species that are overtaking high country grazing slopes.

>  in more recent years Hieracium species - various dandelion-like hawkweeds - have been a major problem. The silvery grey rosettes of hawkweed form a skin across the land (often when over-grazed by a combination of sheep and rabbit) inhibiting the regrowth of both introduced pasture and native plants. [hawke weed] <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,26</i>)

>  Much of the land seems to have been badly sown down, as there are many poor swards, and plenty of hawke weed as you pass along to Toko. The hawke weed and the ox-eye daisy are here as at Stratford. 2. <i>(Feb 22, 1906,Hawera Star,6</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Hieracium</i> species

>  The silvery grey rosettes of hawkweed form a skin across the land (often when over-grazed by a combination of sheep and rabbits) inhibiting the regrowth of both introduced pasture and native plants. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/26</i>)

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HAWKWEED (<i>Hieracium boreale</i>, and <i>H. umbellatum</i>) is reported chiefly from Auckland and Southland. Both species are tall, 2 to 4ft. high, and markedly erect. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,209</i>)

>  <b>HiLGENDORF & CALDER</b> HAWKWEED (<i>Hieracium pilosella</i>) a perennial spreading plant with rosette leaves, long and hairy whitish below, flowers solitary, yellow, on leafless stalks 4 - 5 inches tall in scattered localities in Canterbury. <i>(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,221</i>)

>  <b>ADAM</b> With flat rosettes of hairy leaves and creeping stems, hawkweed is abundant in dry tussock grassland in the South island hill country. ... Botanical name: <i>Hieracium aurantiacum</i>, Central Europe. Family: Compositae. <i>(1980,Wild Flowers,40</i>)

>  ...the open pastures, with scattered plants of low stature, were vulnerable to invasion by rabbits, hawkweed (<i>Hieracium</i> spp.) and, in the driest areas, native scabweed. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/32</i>)

>  Erosion became widespread, and introduced weeds such as hawkweed (<i>Hieracium</i> spp.) thrived. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 53/50</i>)

>  "The invasion of <i>Hieracium</i> hawkweed in our convenant was a major issue for me."... With Brian Molloy's help, he discovered that his practice of keeping the stock off the land in summer was allowing the hawkweed to flower and spread. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/91</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> hawksbeard

>  <b>WAiTE</b> (Griffiths Collection) ... on the road-side are clusters of yellow hawkweed ... [written by a Surrey exile] <i>(1895,Pictorial NZ,59</i>)

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HAWKSBEARD (<i>Crepis capillaris</i> or <i>C. virens</i>) is also called hawkweed, hawkbit, and hawkbite, as well as being confused with cat's ear and dandelion. it is common in gardens, pastures, and waste places throughout the islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,201</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> ... winged gossamer of alien bush-edge weeds - wall lettuce, Canadian groundsell and hawkweed. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,65</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MOUSE-EAR HAWKWEED (<i>Hieracium pilosella</i>) ... is increasing and now grows densely in the upper Waitaki Valley and other parts of Canterbury, and in Central Otago. <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,48</i>)

>  <i>hawkweed, mouse-ear [s] Hieracium pilosella</i> L. <i>ibid.</i> 50 <i>mouse-ear hawkweed</i> [s] <i>Hieracium pilosella</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>hawkweed, orange [s] Hieracium aurantiacum</i> L. <i>ibid.</i> 55 <i>orange hawkweed [s] Hieracium aurantiacum </i>L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Pilosella aurantiaca</i> ORANGE HAWKWEED ... Fox and cubs ... Native to: Europe. (=<i>Hieracium aurantiacum</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,214</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>hawkweed, tussock [s] Hieracium lachenalii</i> Gmel. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>hawkweed, field [s] Hieracium pratense </i>Tausch <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,36</i>)

### Hay's curse
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of <i>Oxalis</i>; a garden weed.

>  <b>R. MASON</b> Hay's curse: A species of oxalis. i heard this in 1933 from T.L. Lancaster who was head of the Departtment of Botany Auckland University College. Hay's Nurseries were well known and old established nurseries in Auckland, still going then, but i believe since closed down. i am not certain of the particular species but the plants have pinkish flowers and produce large numbers of tubers and of corms, by which it spreads and which are almost impossible to get rid of! i understand that Hay introduced the plant into N.Z. it is the only time i have heard the name used, and imagine that it was then obsolescent. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Personal Communication</i>)

### haybox
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hay-lined box used for rearing newborn orphan lambs and occ.calves.

>  HAYBOX JAM AND RAiSiN PUDDiNG... <i>(Jul 9, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),101</i>)

>  <b>GASCOiGNE</b> The old time hay box was a good servant. A deepish box filled with good dry hay enveloped a cold lamb and its properties of heat and revival had to be experienced to be credited. i have sometimes at night given a stud lamb up for lost, left it in a hay box on the laundry floor and gone to bed, to be awakened in the small hours by the bleat of a lamb wanting milk. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,144</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Container insulated with hay which will maintain sufficient heat to cook certain foods slowly. [Ideal for milk puddings especially if milk is first warmed.]

### haylage
 
 <i>n.</i> Hay plastic-wrapped in convenient bales, keeping it preserved for longer.

>  increasing use of haylage - a hay silage which is sufficiently nutritious to allow cows to continue lactating - has changed the business in a big way. While hay has to be dried out, haylage can be baled a day or two after cutting. <i>(Oct 22, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Handy haylage. Empty hay sheds are becoming a familiar sight on lower North island farms as farmers discover the convenience of haylage... Haylage is plastic wrapped in convenient bales, keeping it preserved for longer... Haylage is simple to make. There is no pit to dig as there is for silage and the cut grass is only left lying for 24 hours. <i>(Aug 25, 2000,Dominion,Farming Suppl. 24</i>) [NOTES: check whether date 24/08 or 25/08]

>  October usually yields silage haylage and baleage. More mature grass is harvested in November and December as hay. <i>(Nov 1995,Growing Today,42</i>)

>  Ross and Sue Topping's sons.. - here helping feed out "haylage" in winter - are the fourth generation.. to work the family farm at Ratapiko... <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,34</i>)

### haymaker hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Jake tries to keep the breeze from blowing his haymaker hat off. `Funny how everything feels so strange now that i'm home,' he says. <i>(1984,Tidal Creek,FiNLAYSON,95 ed.</i>)

### haymaking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> harvesting dried grass

>  Haymaking, as i remember it in the 40s and 50s, was a very protracted affair as a number of farmers would combine forces to tackle the task. <i>(Jun 2003,NZ Memories,No. 42/34</i>)

>  i always loved being with Dad at harvesting time, helping with the food and drink for the men working with the haymaking. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/27</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> working a beach claim for gold [goldmining]

>  <b>HARPER</b> [goldfields] They give me a shovel and, with it nearly full of sand, direct me to shake it gently in the water till all the sand has gone, and then round its rim is a thin line of gold, fine as dust, enough to cover a threepenny bit. This is the easiest way of getting gold, so easy, it is called `Haymaking,' but it will not last long, as the black sand brought down by the river, with its gold, from the mountains, and cast up on the beach, where it has lain for centuries, is of limited extent. [Also in -1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 10 <i>(1866,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,100,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> When they come upon one of the rich spots, the fine particles of gold being often visible to the naked eye, they at once remove the black layer of sand out of reach of the tide, and wash it when convenient.* ... [fn] This method of working the beaches was also called `haymaking' or `beachcombing'. These terms were not applied to beach-workings above high-water mark. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,222</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Haast in 1967 May West Coast Gold Rushes (1962) 222]

>  <b>MAY</b> HAYMAKiNG: working a sea-beach claim below high-water. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,527,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEiNZ</b> [caption] `Haymaking' at Shetland Beach, Charleston.`Haymaking' is the term for working a beach claim. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,21</i>)

>  <b>HEiNZ</b> Haymaking : Working a seabeach claim. <i>ibid.</i> 21. The Haymakers... The leads consisted of three types: those deposited between high and low water mark... This type of mining was called haymaking. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a rustic [SOED: N.Amer., Austral., & NZ]

### haystacks
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Action terms related to the movements of the marbles are, Throughs and No Throughs, Tracks and Clears, Stops and Rolls, Lay-Up, Manyies, Kiss, Ons, Gees, Haystacks, Stays, Spiders Can't Cross a River etc. Action Terms related to taking an opponent's marbles are Fires, Double-Hits and Forces. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Even as 'Hayward' kiwifruit were proving a winner and making New Zealand growers unprecedented .., there was a clear-eyed understanding that new varieties could .. be needed... <i>(Jan 2005,NZ Geographic,No. 71/59</i>)

### hazards
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Even a gentle game of `hazards' was a pastime indulged in by the many without a fear of rude police interruption,. . . [before the 1908 Gaming Act]. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

### haze
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As each raceful was finished, the calves were hazed through the gate and out into a clean yard. <i>(1949,High Country Days,95</i>)

### HAZOP
 
 <i>abbr.</i> hazard and operability

>  <i>The independent Herald</i> asked the council, under the <i>Official information Act</i>, for a copy of a hazard and operability (HAZOP) study carried out after a sewage spill from an Anglian Water intenational (AWi) sewage plant at Happy Valley in November 2000. <i>(Feb 18, 2003,independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

### HB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Hawke's Bay

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The most capable of Silver's pursuers after George Chaulklin, probably, was a noted horseman from H.B. , a young fellow named Jack Wedd, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,190</i>)

>  HB fire risk falls [headline] A low forest fire risk in Hawke's Bay is keeping forestry owners happy ... <i>(Jan 17, 1989,Courier (Napier),3</i>)

### HC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> habitual criminal

>  <b>HYDE</b> Three years in prison; reformative detention they called it, which meant you wore a blue suit instead of a brown one, and didn't sit with the hard-labour men and H.C.'s in chapel. <i>ibid.</i> 256 The H.C.'s, that's the habitual criminals, wear a brown coat on Sundays, and we wear blue coats .. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,86</i>)

>  ... one finishing school was the Borstal; then i graduated with an H.C. award at Mount Eden. ... Because a bloke's an H.C. it's reckoned as an assault. <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,11</i>)

### HDC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Health and Disability Commissioner

>  Cull proposes all claims come in the first instance to the office of the health and disability commissioner. The main investigation of complaints would occur there so the HDC would in effect act as gatekeeper for access to other avenues, such as ACC and the Medical Practitioners' Disciplinary Tribunal... Neither ACC nor the MPDT have this central purpose. <i>(Apr 8, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

### he
 
 <i>n.</i> the central player in a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> All chasing games have certain essential characteristics. The central player is called a `He' or an `it.' (in New Zealand this player is most often called a `He.') <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,76</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Two other kinds of games that played an important role in the girls' playground, along with the singing games, were chasing and dialogue games. in New Zealand, chasing or tagging [51] games are initiated by the `He' player (called the `it' player elsewhere). Before 1900 there seem to have been fewer `He' games. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,50</i>)

>  <b>KEiTH & MAiN</b> The first out in almost any game was `He' for the next round. Being `He' was an almost mystical ritual and probably caused more tears than anything else. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### He Kaina No Katoa
 
 <i>phr.</i> see cit.

>  <b>FRASER & GiBBONS</b> HE KAiNA NO KATOA: A Maori word, meaning `The Home to All'. The name given to the New Zealand Hut in the N.Z. camp at Salisbury during the War <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,117</i>)

### head
 
 <i>n.</i> Sc. of a sheep-run, the inland part, cf back (of a run).

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> We spent what spare time we had, at the forest i have named as crossing the head of the run, - ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,58</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep dogs] to block sheep and pull them towards the shepherd.

>  He is well bred, very forceful and will head, hunt and back. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,102</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Head: the act of heading, as: a dog has headed when he has arrived at the far side of the sheep from the starting point. A dog's owner may also may also use the idiom when he will say: 'This dog will head' but also maye be heard to say: 'i can head with him.' <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,40</i>)

>  Handy - a dog that heads a bit, hunts with a bit of noise and possibly backs as well. <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The sheep were Merinos from Whatarangi Station and a few of them waited athe flag long enough for the dog to head them. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,31</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The dog heads them off and pull-pushes actually, for the dog remains behind the sheep - the sheep to the shepherd so he or she can identify them from local knowledge, brands, earmarks or whatever. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,135</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> .. you've got to catch your sheep first, in a corner of a cliff or thicket or an enclosure. Dogs will head the sheep into one and keep it still for you. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,136</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a substantival use of head v. (of a dog, `head' sheep), in make a good head [(farmers') 1947 =the ?length of a strung-out mob; ?the cast needed to head sheep]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Stopping dog: A heading dog which, once it has headed sheep, will just lie and hold the mob until its master arrives. The ordinary heading dog will endeavour to `pull' the sheep. in early days when high country sheep were much wilder and longer heads occasioned stopping dogs - real specialists at straight-out heading - were common. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>) [NOTES: [glossary]]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Boy was certainly the fastest sheep dog i have ever seen. He put in a splendid hunt, which would have been a bad head if the sheep had not been so fast, and hunted them all on to the next man's beat. <i>ibid.</i> 44 Tim and his dog came along full of business, and Rover started up the bank in great style. He made a beautiful head and flopped on his belly about ten yards from the sheep ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,13</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> it was a perfect head and pull. Tat got them into the twelve o'clock hold in the ring. <i>(1970,Tat,122</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... she had that hallmark of the real heading dog - a strong inherent instinct to keep out of sight of her sheep until she had completed her head. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Sc. of water, see also blackburn head, hogburn head, government head, sluice head. [goldmining]

>  <b>EVANS</b> Four additional heads in water race. Ex [certificate] 2189. <i>(Apr 27, 1868,Waikaka Saga,8</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Apr 27 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 8]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Many creeks were dammed and water-races were everywhere. if a miner asked for so many heads, the water was measured out to him by a gauge. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,2,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> `When other and more accurate means of measuring water are not conveniently available, it may be done by using gauge boxes of the dimensions ... `The gauge box shall be 12 feet long, 20 inches or 40 inches wide, inside measurements ... in accordance with the undermentioned table ... in other words, if the depth of the pressure above the board, above the top of the aperture, is five inches, the aperture is twenty inches wide, and two inches deep, the number of sluice heads discharged is one.' <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,8</i>)

>  <b>VEiTCH</b> The largest race, The Ovens Water Race Company, came in 30 miles from the Clutha creek with 15 heads of water to the Dunstan Flat. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,47</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Head: the unit of volume by which water was sold to alluvial miners. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Sc. of tobacco, cf niggerhead, negrohead.

>  <b>McNAB</b> Paid Old Blucher the Chief [?Rauparaha] 100 heads of tobacco for the house. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,441</i>) [NOTES: 1836 May 11 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 441]

>  <b>POLACK</b> This was carrying the jest too far; but i gave, as a [64] tithe, a head of tobacco; which he, at last, admitted was compensation enough. <i>ibid.</i> 2/37 [sc. of tobacco (cf negrohead)] He ... demanded payment for his hurt, and the bad conduct of my [musket] piece. i gave him the price, viz. a head of tobacco. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/63</i>)

>  [?=niggerhead ?fig.] [NZ letter of 23 Aug 1840] [Henry Williams] had not given them [sc. Maoris for land] even a fish-hook or a head of tobacco, except in payment to the boys who carried his things, or to those who became converts to the mihanere (missionary) creed. <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 129</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Apr 19 Joseph Somes to Lord John Russell in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 129]

>  Te Tipa returned with a nice pig ... at the same time giving us back three out of ten heads of Tobacco with which he had been intrusted. <i>(May 9, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,306,1966 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the rammer for crushing quartz in gold-mining [goldmining]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Head, n. the rammer for crushing quartz in gold-mining. <i>(1898,Austral English,194</i>)

>  `Forty additional heads will be shortly added to the crushing power, bringing the battery up to sixty heads.' <i>(1890,Goldfields Victoria,7</i>)

>  The battery consisted of ten head of stamps, of a very old type, driven by a traction engine. <i>(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,16/111</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> Sc. a whaleboat, party; cf headman, headsman. [(whalers') [1840] 1845 E.J. Wakefield [see notes: see STEER]]



 
 <i>v.</i> to reach or come to, or take up position in, the head of

>  <b>GRAYLiNG</b> Had this force continued to march ... it [would have been able] to have passed above the pa and headed the small gullies from which the enemy fired with such deadly effect. <i>(1863,War in Taranaki,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [? A development of OED head sb ? bluff, promontory eg Beachy Head.]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> A shoal ... extends from this towards the eastern Head [of Otakou inlet] - a steep round bluff - leaving a channel ... running close to the bluff, ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,9</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of sheepdogs) to head off (sheep) [(station)]

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> [The dogs] would head, lead, hunt away, force and back, though, of course, they were best at rouseabout work. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,27</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> The dog `Trojan' ... would head, hunt, lead, back, heel cattle, catch a horse, pick up cast sheep, open and close gates, retrieve sacks ..., strike sheep with his paws, walk on his hind legs, ring a bell and perform many other minor tricks. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,2</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> in April 1921 Ron was taken out to help with the autumn muster. One of the musterers ... gave him his first dog ... who would hunt as well as head. Davis also gave him a stout mustering-stick and showed him how to make the best use of it when sidling across a steep face. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,78</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to (have a dog) head off (sheep etc.)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... boys heading home sheep <i>(1846,Journal,36</i>)

>  The sheep got out of the paddoc [sic] and we had some difficulty to head them. <i>(Nov 17, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,66,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>J.G.WiLSON</b> Went for a good long walk ... and found that `Fop' was so fat and lazy that he would not head some sheep i sent him after. <i>(Apr 26, 1874,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1874 Apr 26 J.G.WiLSON Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 184]

>  ~ (1) People do not as a rule say (e.g.) `fifty cattle' but `fifty h. of cattle'. Up country people usually confine this idiom to cattle and sometimes horses. But i see to-day in `The Press' (June 27, 1933) the editor speaks of a decrease of so many h. of sheep disclosed by this year's sheep return. (C.) (2) A dog goes round to the far side of a mob of sheep and stops them. This is called heading. Hence heading dog, one whose work this is. if he does it neatly he makes a good h See also sheep dog. 3. The owner would say, `i can head with him'.(C; [1874 Kennaway Crusts],p. 45) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> He was a powerful dog to head or heel cattle. ... i have often wondered how Teddy came to be such a fine natural heeler. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,6</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> `i will send my dog to head them,' volunteered Thomas. <i>(1937,Thomas,116</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> [sc. runaway horses] `After them, head them!' cried the driver [of the runaway horses]. <i>(1877,Colonial Travel,245</i>) [NOTES: given as b. presumably because it is people not dogs doing the heading]



 
 <i>n.</i> [? A development of OED head sb ? bluff, promontory eg Beachy Head.]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> [Port Chalmers] ... anchoring in perfect safety at about a quarter of a mile from the eastern head [301] (called Tairoa's head) ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,300</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sport.

>  There were a few `heads' up from Christchurch last Saturday and the Tommies [sc. bookmakers] could not understand their presence, but in the last race they explained why they were in attendance by the way they put the stuff on Cavatina. <i>(Jul 25, 1908,NZ Truth,162/2/5</i>)

>  Of course, there are [two-up] `schools' here and there, and only a `head', in other words `a true sport', knows their locale. Once upon a time the free and easy `school' with the `ring-master,' and his `All set - a fair go' broke upon the stilly night. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a prospective customer, a `john'. [(car-salesmen's)]

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. A `plum' or a `head' is a prospective customer, whom the salesman spies walking onto the yard. <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,2/1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(hist.)]

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> `The skippers of many of the colonial trading schooners were alway[s???] ready to deal with a man who had `a really good head,' and used to commission such men as my companion to `pick up heads' for them ...' [get quot : and use full quot for HNZDQ] <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,viii</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Governor Darling quoted in 1935 Harrop (ed) introduction to Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland viii Maning Old New Zealand]

>  <b>MANiNG</b> [a dried or preserved Maori head; a `business' head] We were always ready to deal with a man who had a `real good head' and used to commission ... men ... to `pick up heads' for them. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,59</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep dogs and dog trials] a run by a heading dog. (See [short head](sho-spa.md#short-head); [long head](liv-lyn.md#long-head))

>  <b>DAViS</b> i knew that in the Long Head event, when a dog had run up the hill and reached a position behind the mob, his owner had to stop him in order to constitute a proper "head", and he could not go on with the business of bringing the sheep down towards him in the "draw" until the judge gave him a wave to carry on. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,79</i>)

### head a nor'wester
 
 <i>phr.</i> said of a very good heading dog, that it could do this [(Canterbury musterers')]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... this dog would `head a nor' wester'. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,52</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> .. he will catch a sheep no matter how fast they run or how rough they going - `Head a Nor' wester, that dog' as the saying goes. <i>(1951,High Country,22</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ..[The expression] ... refers to a dog that is particularly good at `casting out' (or running out) to head and stop sheep which are breaking away in the wrong direction' (from the strong Canterbury nor' wester wind) <i>(Apr 21, 1952,Letter</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> And dogs, what dogs the old men had -/ They'd head the wild nor'west gales/ And bring back fogs./ <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,91</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [heading or gathering dogs] to be more than capable of bringing all errant stock to its handler.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This dog would 'head a nor'wester.' <i>(1947,Wayleggo,52</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> He will catch a sheep no matter how fast they run or how rough the going - 'head a nor'wester, that dog,' as the saying goes. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,22</i>)
